1. START HERE
==============================================================================


How To Survive — A Household Pandemic Resilience Plan
=====================================================

A complete, step-by-step plan for getting a household through a severe
pandemic with degraded or interrupted public services: water, electricity,
food, heat, sanitation, medicine and security.

Two tracks run through every chapter:

- [LAND] LAND — you have a house with a garden, yard, field or orchard.
- [FLAT] APARTMENT — you have a flat, a balcony at best, and shared
   infrastructure.

| 📴 Works offline. The entire plan is also built into a single self-contained
| file — offline/survival-plan.html — that needs no internet, no server and
| nothing installed. Copy it to a phone, a USB stick and an e-reader, and
| print it. Rebuild it any time with make offline (Python standard library
| only).

Units are metric. Prices are indicative EUR. The garden calendar is written
for temperate continental Central Europe (Slovenia/Austria/N. Italy/Croatia —
last frost mid-April to mid-May, first frost mid-October). Adjust dates to
your own frost dates; everything else transfers.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Read this first: what this plan actually assumes
------------------------------------------------

A pandemic is not an instant apocalypse. It is a long, grinding degradation.
Planning for the wrong shape of event is the single most common and most
expensive mistake people make.

What actually happens, in rough order:

1. Weeks 0–4 — News, then panic buying. Shelves empty of exactly the things
    you need (flour, oil, tinned goods, paracetamol, toilet paper, baby
    formula). Prices spike. This is the moment when preparing becomes
    impossible, expensive, and antisocial. Everything in this plan must be
    bought before this point.
2. Months 1–6 — Lockdowns or voluntary isolation. Hospitals saturate; ordinary
    illness (appendicitis, heart attack, childbirth, a broken arm) becomes far
    more dangerous than usual. Supply chains work but stutter. Some goods
    simply vanish for months.
3. Months 2–12+ — Staff absence hits infrastructure: water treatment,
    electricity distribution, fuel logistics, waste collection, pharmacy
    restocking. You get intermittent services, not zero services. Rolling
    blackouts. Boil-water notices. Rubbish uncollected. Deliveries halted.
    Cash-only, then barter at the margins.
4. Year 1–3 — Economic damage, inflation, unemployment, shortages of spare
    parts and imported goods. Recovery is uneven. This is the long tail where
    "self-sufficiency" actually pays off.

Design target: 12 months of intermittent utilities, and 3 months of no
resupply at all, while never leaving your property.

That is the standard this whole plan is written against. It is achievable for
a normal household on a normal budget, if you start early and build in order.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


The four hard truths
--------------------

1. You cannot become fully self-sufficient. Nobody is. Not on a hectare, not
    with chickens, not with solar. You will still need salt, fuel, medicine,
    spare parts, seed and metal. The goal is not independence — it is reducing
    dependence, buying time, and lowering your exposure. A household that
    needs a shop once a month instead of twice a week is enormously safer.
2. Skills beat gear. A €3,000 solar system you have never wired, a pressure
    canner you have never used, and a garden you have never grown will all
    fail at the worst moment. Every purchase in this plan carries an implied
    homework assignment: use it now, while mistakes are cheap.
3. You survive with neighbours, not against them. The lone fortress is a
    fantasy that gets people killed. Three to eight cooperating households can
    share a well, a generator, a watch rotation, a nurse and a freezer. One
    household cannot do any of that, and cannot sleep.
4. The things that kill people are boring. Not raiders. Dehydration, dysentery
    from bad sanitation, hypothermia in an unheated house, an untreated
    infection, a missed insulin dose, and carbon monoxide from cooking
    indoors. Spend your money and attention in that order.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


The priority order (spend money in this sequence)
-------------------------------------------------

Rank everything by how fast its absence kills you. Never buy from a lower tier
while a higher tier has a gap.

#  Need                       You survive without it for          Chapter
-  -------------------------  ----------------------------------  ------------
1  Air, warmth, shelter       3 hours (exposure/CO)               04
2  Critical medication        Hours to days (insulin, cardiac,    06
                              epilepsy, steroids)

3  Water                      3 days                              01
4  Sanitation & hygiene       ~1 week before disease              05
5  Food                       3 weeks (but you are useless after  03
                              1)

6  Light, power, information  Weeks (morale and safety collapse   02 · 10
                              first)

7  Renewable food production  Months (matters from month 3        07 · 08 · 09
                              onward)

8  Security, repair, money    Ongoing                             10 · 11


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


The four readiness levels
-------------------------

Do not try to jump to Level 3. Complete each level fully — across all
categories — before starting the next. A household with 12 months of rice and
no water plan is less prepared than one with two weeks of everything.

Level               Endurance             Rough cost (family of  Time to build
                                          4)
------------------  --------------------  ---------------------  -------------
L0 — Ready          72 hours, no          €250 – €500            One weekend
                    utilities

L1 — Buffered       2 weeks, no resupply  €800 – €1,800          One month
L2 — Isolated       3 months, no          €3,000 – €8,000        3 – 6 months
                    resupply; power &
                    water for critical
                    loads

L3 — Self-renewing  12 months, with       €8,000 – €25,000       1 – 3 years
                    food/energy/water
                    production that
                    repeats


L3 is the only level that includes cycles — things that regenerate: seed you
saved, chicks that hatched, compost you made, wood you seasoned. Everything
below L3 is a countdown timer. See Chapter 12.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


How to use this repository
--------------------------

Step 1 — Measure where you actually are. Fill in worksheets/household-
audit.md. It takes about an hour and tells you your current survival time in
days, per category. Do not buy anything before you do this.

Step 2 — Read the planning framework. docs/00-planning-framework.md — how to
set targets, budget, sequence purchases, and define the trigger points that
tell you when to act.

Step 3 — Work the categories in priority order. Chapters 01 → 06 are universal
and come first. Then take your track: 07 + 08 if you have land, 09 if you do
not.

Step 4 — Buy from the tiered lists. worksheets/shopping-lists.md — exactly
what to buy at each level, in purchase order, with quantities for your
household size.

Step 5 — Test it. Then test it again. worksheets/drill-log.md — the blackout
weekend, the water-off day, the isolation drill. Untested preparation is a
guess.

Step 6 — Build what you need. builds/ — construction plans with dimensions,
cut lists, materials and costs, ordered so the highest-value builds come
first. A bucket water filter takes 30 minutes; the chicken coop takes a
weekend.

Step 7 — Make it work offline. offline/survival-plan.html is the whole plan in
one self-contained file — no internet, no server, no apps. Open it in any
browser, or print it. A plan you cannot reach in a blackout is not a plan; see
Chapter 13.

Step 8 — Run the cycles. docs/12-operating-cycles-and-calendar.md — the daily,
weekly, monthly and seasonal routines that keep stock rotated, gardens
producing, and animals breeding. This is the part that turns a stockpile into
a system.

Planning

   tiers, sequencing, the Green/Amber/Red trigger ladder, common mistakes.


Universal survival systems (do these first, both tracks)

   purification, greywater, apartment water sources.

   generators, fuel, wiring safety, the four power tiers.
- 03 — Food storage — calorie maths, the one-year staple base, packing for
   25-year shelf life, nutrition gaps, preservation (canning, fermenting,
   drying, curing), rotation.
- 04 — Heating, cooking & fuel — the warm-room strategy, insulation, firewood
   maths, stoves, cooking fuel ranking, fuel-efficient cooking, carbon
   monoxide.
- 05 — Sanitation & hygiene — the thing that actually kills people; toilets
   without water, humanure, greywater, laundry, soap, menstrual and infant
   supplies, waste and vector control.
- 06 — Health & pandemic protocol — prescription buffers, the medical stock
   list, PPE, isolation room, decontamination, ventilation, dental, mental
   health, when to break isolation for care.


Production — the land track [LAND]

   layout, the hungry gap, seed saving, soil fertility without inputs,
   perennials, water in the garden, pests, tools.

   rabbits, quail, goats, pigs, bees, fish; breeding cycles that actually

Production — the apartment track [FLAT]

   answer), mushrooms, fermentation, container growing, quail, balcony solar,

Holding it together

   mutual-aid group, the charity plan, radios, offline information, family

Offline & paper

   order, the three-copies rule, devices that work when power is uncertain,
   media longevity, the offline reference library to download before you need
   it, keeping it findable by someone other than you.


Build plans [BUILD]

- Build index — every build in priority order, with time, cost and skill
   level; plus the tool kit, timber sizes, fixings, mesh and safety
   fundamentals.
- 01 — Water builds — bucket gravity filter, tippy-tap, first-flush diverter,
   IBC stand and manifold, slow sand filter, solar water heater, ollas and
   drip.
- 02 — Power builds — the complete 12 V solar system with cable-sizing and
   fusing tables, DC lighting circuit, insulated battery box, adjustable tilt
   frame, generator noise screen.
- 03 — Cooking & heat builds — rocket stoves, haybox/thermal cooker, solar
   oven, solar dehydrator, wood store, warm-room tent.
- 04 — Sanitation builds — bucket toilet, urine diverter, humanure compost
   bays, handwash station, plunger washing machine, greywater mulch basin.
- 05 — Food storage builds — root cellar, buried barrel cellar, root clamp,
   cool pantry, mylar packing station, cold smoker, curing racks.
- 06 — Garden builds — no-dig beds, three-bay compost, insect mesh cage, cold
   frame, low tunnel, walk-in polytunnel, seed rack, liquid feed barrel.
- 07 — Animal builds — the full chicken coop plan, predator-proof run, chicken
   tractor, feeders and drinkers, rabbit hutch, quail cage, black soldier fly
   bucket, fodder rack.
- 08 — Apartment builds — sprouting rack, microgreen shelf, mushroom bucket,
   wormery, self-watering containers, balcony rain catch, air cleaner, under-
   bed storage.


Worksheets

- Quick-reference cards — * print these first. The numbers you need at 3 a.m.
   by torchlight: water treatment doses, oral rehydration, bleach dilutions,
   carbon monoxide, when to seek help, the trigger ladder, the daily routine,
   your shut-offs and contacts.
- Household audit — fill this in second.
- Shopping lists — tiered, in buy-order, with quantities.
- Drill log — the tests that prove it works.
- Inventory template — what you have, where, expiry.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Legal, ethical and medical note
-------------------------------

- Prepare early and gradually. Buying a year of food over twelve months is
   prudent; buying it in the week panic starts takes it from someone who needs
   it that day. The ethical way to prepare is the same as the effective way:
   start now.
- Follow public health guidance and get vaccinated. Nothing in this plan is a
   substitute for medical care, and none of it is medical advice — build your
   medication buffer with your doctor and pharmacist, never by skipping doses.
- Well drilling, rainwater use, greywater, wood stoves, solar grid connection,
   livestock keeping, slaughter, waste disposal and firearms are all regulated
   and the rules differ by country and municipality. Check before you build.
- Keep helping people. A household that is calm, stocked and healthy is the
   one that can afford to help neighbours — which is exactly what makes the
   street around you survivable.


==============================================================================
2. 00 — THE PLANNING FRAMEWORK
==============================================================================


00 — The Planning Framework
===========================

How to plan, in what order, with what money, and how to know when to act.

Most people "prepare" by buying things they saw in a video. Six months later
they own a water filter, forty tins of beans, and a torch, and they still die
of hypothermia or dysentery. This chapter is the method that prevents that.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. Build the threat model before the shopping list
--------------------------------------------------

Write down, on one page, the specific disruptions you are planning against.
For a severe pandemic, the realistic list is:

Disruption          Likelihood          Duration            Effect on you
------------------  ------------------  ------------------  ------------------
Supply chain gaps,  Near certain        1–12 months,        Can't buy flour,
empty shelves for                       intermittent        oil, medicine,
specific goods                                              spare parts

Voluntary or        Near certain        Weeks to months at  Can't leave; can't
mandated isolation                      a time              work; deliveries
                                                            stop

Health system       Near certain        3–18 months         Ordinary
saturation                                                  emergencies become
                                                            lethal

Price inflation /   Likely              1–3 years           Your savings buy
currency stress                                             less; cash-only
                                                            periods

Rolling             Likely              Hours to days at a  Fridge, freezer,
electricity                             time                heating pumps,
blackouts                                                   water pumps, comms

Water pressure      Possible            Days to weeks       You need stored +
loss or boil-water                                          treatable water
notices

Waste collection    Likely              Weeks               Sanitation and
suspended                                                   vermin problems

Fuel rationing /    Possible            Weeks               No generator fuel,
stations closed                                             no vehicle, no
                                                            deliveries

Complete grid       Unlikely but        —                   Everything at once
failure > 1 week    catastrophic

Civil disorder in   Location-dependent  Days                Security becomes
your area                                                   primary


Your plan's job is to make each row survivable. Not to make you invincible.
Print the table, mark your current answer for each row, and you have your
priority list for the next six months.


What you are NOT planning for

Be explicit about this too, because it saves money and sanity. You are not
planning for permanent civilisational collapse, nuclear war, or defending a
compound against an army. If that ever happens, the plan is "leave with the
go-bag and find people" — not "hold the house". Everything here is sized for a
bad decade, not the end of the world.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. Audit your household honestly
--------------------------------

Fill in worksheets/household-audit.md. The key outputs are:

a) People. Number, ages, weights, and for each person: chronic conditions,
prescription medicines (name, dose, how many days you hold), allergies,
mobility limits, glasses/dental needs, pregnancy or infants, dietary needs,
and pets (they eat too — 12 months of dog food is 100+ kg).

b) Calorie and water demand. Sum the daily requirement for your actual
household. Don't use "a person" — use your people (see Ch. 03 and Ch. 01).

c) The building. Own or rent? House or flat? Which floor? Heating type and
what happens to it without electricity (most gas boilers and all pellet stoves
are dead without power). Insulation quality. Roof area and material (rainwater
potential). Is there a cellar, a garage, a loft, a south-facing roof or
balcony? Water: mains only, or well/borehole/spring/stream? Sewer or septic?

d) The land. Total m², usable growing m², sun hours, slope, soil type, water
access, existing trees, fencing, outbuildings.

e) Skills in the household. Who can garden, cook from raw ingredients, sew,
weld, do first aid, fix a bicycle, wire 12 V, butcher an animal, shoot, speak
to neighbours, keep calm. List the gaps — those are your training plan.

f) Current endurance, in days. For each category, how many days could you last
starting right now with the doors locked:

  Water          ___ days      Sanitation     ___ days
  Food           ___ days      Medicine       ___ days
  Heat           ___ days      Power          ___ days
  Cooking fuel   ___ days      Hygiene/soap   ___ days

Your real preparedness is the lowest number on that list. Not the average. Fix
the smallest number first, every time. This single rule will direct your
spending better than any list.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Set targets by level, not by wishlist
----------------------------------------

Work through the four levels from the README horizontally — every category to
L1 before any category to L2.


L0 — Ready (72 hours) · one weekend · €250–500

The legal-minimum civil-defence standard. Everyone should be here already.

- 30 L water per person stored + a means to treat more
- 3 days of food needing no cooking
- Torches, batteries, a radio, a power bank
- First aid kit, 2 weeks of any prescription meds
- Cash in small notes; documents in one bag
- A way to keep warm with no heating


L1 — Buffered (2 weeks) · one month · €800–1,800

Covers the overwhelming majority of real disruptions.

- 200 L water/person stored or a reliable treatable source
- 14 days of normal food you actually eat + cooking fuel for it
- A camping stove + fuel; a way to heat one room
- Lighting and phone charging independent of the grid (small solar)
- Full medicine chest, 30+ days of prescriptions
- Bucket toilet kit and hygiene supplies for two weeks


L2 — Isolated (3 months) · 3–6 months · €3,000–8,000

The core of pandemic preparedness — the "don't leave the house for a season"
level.

- 3 months of staples, packed for long storage, with rotation started
- A power system that runs the fridge/freezer, lights and comms indefinitely
- A water source you control (rainwater, well, or 600 L+/person stored) with
   multi-stage filtration and disinfectant chemicals for 3+ months
- Wood/propane for 3 months of cooking and one heated room
- Sanitation fully solved without mains water
- A pandemic isolation protocol, PPE, and a 3-month medicine buffer
- First growing season started, first animals if you have land


L3 — Self-renewing (12 months + cycles) · 1–3 years · €8,000–25,000

- 12 months of stored calories as a buffer, not the plan
- Food production with saved seed, breeding animals, and made compost
- Energy: enough solar/battery for year-round critical loads + 2 winters of
   wood
- Water: rainwater capture + storage sized for your dry season, or a hand-
   pumped well
- Preservation capacity: pressure canner, jars, dehydrator, root cellar,
   smoker
- Spares, tools and consumables to keep all of the above running for a year
- A functioning mutual-aid group


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Budget: what each amount actually buys
-----------------------------------------

If money is the constraint, spend it in exactly this order. Each tier assumes
the previous is complete.


€0 — do these first, this week

- Drink and store tap water in washed 5 L bottles (free)
- Write the family contact plan and print phone numbers on paper
- Learn your water shut-off, gas shut-off and consumer unit
- Download and print the key references (Ch. 11)
- Talk to three neighbours. This is the highest-value free action available.
- Start a compost heap; save seeds from a shop tomato/pepper
- Book a first aid course and a dental check-up


€300 — the survival floor (L0)

Water containers 60 L · 20 L gravity filter or Sawyer-type · NaDCC tablets ·
unscented bleach · torches + headlamps + AA/AAA rechargeables + charger ·
wind-up/solar radio · 20 W solar panel + 20,000 mAh power bank · first aid kit
· OTC medicine chest · 2 weeks of tinned/dry food · camping gas stove + 4
canisters · bucket toilet + coir · 2 kg soap · cash · fire extinguisher + CO
alarm + smoke alarm


€1,500 — the two-week to one-month household (L1)

Everything above plus: 400–600 L water storage · 1 month of staples in buckets
+ mylar + O2 absorbers · 100 W solar + 100 Ah LiFePO4 + 600 W inverter ·
better stove + 2× 11 kg propane cylinders · 20 kg firewood-equivalent or a
small wood burner if you have a flue · full medicine chest + 3-month
prescription buffer · FFP2/FFP3 masks, gloves, goggles, disinfectant · hand
tools · radios


€5,000 — the three-month isolated household (L2)

Everything above plus: 1–2 kW solar + 5 kWh LiFePO4 + hybrid inverter · IBC
rainwater tanks (1,000 L each) + first-flush diverter + pre-filter · 3 months
of staples for the whole household · chest freezer (A+++) · pressure canner +
100 jars · dehydrator · grain mill (hand + electric) · wood stove with cooktop
+ 4 m³ seasoned wood · full garden setup (beds, tools, seeds, netting, hoses)
OR full apartment kit (sprouting, microgreens, mushrooms, fermentation) ·
chickens + coop + 6 months of feed


€15,000+ — the self-renewing homestead (L3)

Everything above plus: 4–6 kW solar + 10–15 kWh storage · well with hand pump
or solar DC pump · polytunnel/greenhouse · 12 months of staples · orchard and
perennials planted · breeding stock (hens + rooster, rabbits, or goats) · 2
years of wood + a chainsaw and safety gear + a log splitter · a proper
workshop, spares and consumables · root cellar built

| Note the shape of this: the first €300 buys perhaps 60% of your total risk
| reduction. The last €10,000 buys maybe 10%. Do not let the dream of a
| homestead stop you from buying a €25 water filter this weekend.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Sequencing: the correct order of operations
----------------------------------------------

Within any budget, execute in this order. Each step is cheap and fast compared
to the one after it, and each makes the next one smaller.

1. Reduce demand before adding supply. Insulating a room, buying a thermal
    cooker, and swapping to LED lighting each cost €20–100 and permanently
    shrink your fuel, food and power needs. Adding generation to cover waste
    is the most expensive possible move. Always cut the load first.
2. Store before you produce. Stored food is instant, certain and cheap.
    Produced food is slow, uncertain and skill-dependent. Storage buys you the
    time in which production can fail and be retried.
3. Water before food. Always. Every time.
4. Sanitation the same week as water. They are the same problem.
5. Medicine buffer before any gear. It takes months of ordinary GP visits to
    build legally. Start the conversation now; it costs nothing today.
6. Low tech before high tech. A hand pump, a hand grain mill, a wood stove and
    a bucket work when electronics fail. Buy the manual version of anything
    critical before the powered version of anything optional.
7. Redundancy on single points of failure only. Two ways to get water, two
    ways to cook, two ways to make light, two ways to treat water. Not two of
    everything — that's hoarding, not planning.
8. Skills and community last in spending, first in time. They cost almost
    nothing but take the longest to acquire. Start them on day one, in
    parallel with everything else.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. The trigger ladder — knowing when to act
-------------------------------------------

The hardest question is not "what to buy" but "when to move". Decide the
thresholds now, in calm, and write them down. Then you act on evidence instead
of adrenaline — days before the crowds, without ever making a panic decision.


[GREEN] GREEN — normal times (this is where you are now)

Posture: Build to your target level. Rotate stock. Grow the garden. Train.
Nothing is urgent; everything is progressing. Monitor: WHO Disease Outbreak
News, your national public health agency, and one or two boring epidemiology
sources. Once a month is enough.


[AMBER] AMBER — an outbreak with sustained human-to-human transmission outside its country of origin

This is the trigger. Not "cases in my country" — that is too late. Actions,
within 72 hours:

1. Top up everything to L2 now, in one trip. Prices and availability are still
    normal. This is the single most valuable action in the entire plan and it
    lasts only a few days.
2. Fill every water container. Fill the car's fuel tank and all fuel cans.
3. Refill prescriptions; request early refills and a written repeat.
4. Draw out cash in small denominations.
5. Order the long-lead items you have been putting off (filters, seeds,
    spares, a battery). Delivery times collapse after this point.
6. Tell your mutual-aid group. Agree a check-in schedule.
7. Start masking in crowded indoor places and reduce optional exposure. The
    goal is to be uninfected and stocked when the wave arrives.


[ORANGE] ORANGE — community transmission confirmed in your country

Actions:

1. Withdraw from optional public contact. Work from home if at all possible.
2. Begin the quarantine protocol on all incoming goods and people (Ch. 06).
3. Pull children out of avoidable gatherings; discuss school with your family.
4. Final gap purchases by delivery, not in person.
5. Start the garden cycle regardless of season — sprouts and microgreens begin
    producing in 5–14 days, indoors, any time of year (Ch. 09).
6. Set up the isolation room and PPE station before anyone is ill.
7. Switch to the crisis daily routine (Ch. 12) so it is habit before it
    matters.


[RED] RED — hospitals saturated, or utilities visibly failing, or transmission in your street

Actions:

1. Full household isolation. Nobody in or out without the decon protocol.
2. Switch to stored/produced resources; stop consuming anything irreplaceable.
3. Go to war-footing on hygiene: handwashing station, surface disinfection,
    isolation of any symptomatic person, ventilation maximised.
4. Daily health check for every household member (temp + pulse ox + symptoms
    log).
5. Light, noise and smell discipline (Ch. 10). Nothing advertises.
6. Mutual-aid group to daily radio check-in.
7. Ration deliberately from day one, not when stocks look low. Set the daily
    allowance based on a 12-month horizon and adjust upward later if resupply
    returns. It is far easier to relax a ration than to impose one late.


[BLACK] BLACK — utilities down indefinitely, no authority response

Full off-grid operation. Water from your controlled source only, always
treated. Sanitation to the humanure/latrine system. Warm-room only. Cooking on
wood. Security watch by rotation with the group. Preserve everything
perishable immediately. This is the level that most preparation never needs —
but the kit for it is the same kit as ORANGE, just used harder.


Coming back down

Have de-escalation rules too, or you will live in RED for years and burn out.
Step down one level after two weeks of improved conditions. Restock to your
target level before relaxing the ration.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Test everything — the drill schedule
---------------------------------------

Nothing is real until it has been tested. Untested kit fails at exactly the
wrong moment, and the failures are always stupid: the inverter has the wrong
plug, the filter needs a fitting you don't have, nobody can light the stove,
the stored water tastes so bad the children refuse to drink it.

Drill                     Frequency                  What it proves
------------------------  -------------------------  -------------------------
Blackout weekend — main   Twice a year, once in      Power, light, cooking,
breaker off, Fri night    winter                     heat, fridge/freezer
to Sun night                                         behaviour, morale

Water-off day — main      Twice a year               Storage, filtration, dish
stopcock closed, 24 h                                washing, toilets, how
                                                     much you really use

Eat-the-store week —      Quarterly                  Rotation, recipes,
only stored food, no                                 whether your family will
shopping                                             actually eat it, gaps

Isolation weekend —       Once a year                The protocol, PPE fit,
nobody leaves, decon                                 boredom, what you forgot
protocol active

Cold night — heat off,    Once a winter              Insulation, bedding,
warm-room only                                       clothing, the actual
                                                     temperature

Full inventory count      Twice a year               What expired, what's
                                                     missing, what you
                                                     overbought

Comms check with the      Monthly                    Radios, phone tree, that
mutual-aid group                                     people still care


Keep the results in worksheets/drill-log.md. Every drill produces a shopping
list and a fix list. That is the point.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. The fifteen mistakes that ruin plans
---------------------------------------

1. Buying gear instead of building skills. The gear is the easy 10%.
2. Storing food nobody eats. If your family won't eat it in good times, they
    won't eat it in bad times — they'll just be hungry and miserable. Store
    what you actually cook, and cook what you store.
3. No water plan. The most common and most lethal gap. Food gets all the
    attention; water kills you six times faster.
4. Forgetting sanitation. Second most common. You will produce 1–1.5 kg of
    waste per person per day whether or not you planned for it.
5. Ignoring heat. In a cold climate, heating dwarfs every other energy need. A
    5 kWh battery is irrelevant next to a house that needs 60 kWh/day of heat.
6. Underestimating animal feed. Six hens eat ~300 kg of grain a year. If you
    don't store or grow it, your chickens become your competitors, then your
    dinner.
7. Single points of failure. One filter, one stove, one pair of glasses, one
    can opener, one key person who knows how it all works.
8. Electronics with no spares. Charge controllers and inverters fail. A spare
    €60 controller keeps a €4,000 system alive.
9. Too much gear, no routine. Preparation without operating cycles is a
    museum.
10. OPSEC failures. Posting the stockpile online, bragging to colleagues,
     running a loud generator. You are advertising.
11. Planning alone. See hard truth #3.
12. No mental health plan. Months of confinement, fear and boredom break
     people and marriages. Plan for morale as seriously as for calories.
13. Waiting for certainty. By the time it's certain, the shelves are empty.
     The AMBER trigger exists precisely to let you act early and cheaply.
14. Never testing. See section 7.
15. Planning for the wrong disaster. Pandemic ≠ war ≠ earthquake. Tactical
     gear is not a pandemic plan; a well-stocked pantry, a water system and a
     working thermometer are.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. The one-page version
-----------------------

If you remember nothing else:

  1. Fix the lowest number on your endurance audit. Repeat forever.
  2. Water, sanitation, medicine, heat, food — in that order.
  3. Cut demand before you add supply.
  4. Store first, produce second. Storage buys time for production to fail.
  5. Buy the manual version of anything critical.
  6. Act at AMBER, not at RED.
  7. Test it, or you don't have it.
  8. Three neighbours are worth more than three thousand euros of gear.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[BUILD] Build it: All build plans — construction plans for everything in this
plan, in priority order

Next: 01 — Water — the first thing that kills you.


==============================================================================
3. 01 — WATER
==============================================================================


01 — Water
==========

Three days without it and you are dead. One week of drinking bad water and you
are dead more slowly and much worse. This is the chapter to finish first.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. How much you actually need
-----------------------------

Forget "2 litres a day". That is drinking water for a sedentary person in mild
weather. A household doing manual work, cooking from raw ingredients, washing
by hand and keeping a garden alive needs far more.

Use                   Litres/person/day  Notes
--------------------  -----------------  -------------------------------------
Drinking              2.5 – 3.5          Up to 6 L with heavy work or
                                         fever/diarrhoea

Cooking & food prep   2 – 3              Dry beans and grains need soaking and
                                         boiling water

Washing hands & face  2 – 3              Do not cut this. It is your disease
                                         control.

Body washing          3 – 5              A sponge bath is 3 L; a shower is
                                         40–80 L

Dish washing          2 – 3              Two-basin method: wash + rinse
Toilet flushing       6 – 20             Only if you use a flush toilet — see
                                         Ch. 05

Laundry               3 – 5              Averaged over a week; ~30 L per hand-
                                         wash load

Household total       15 – 25            Realistic sustainable planning figure
Survival minimum      5 – 7              Drinking + cooking + minimal hygiene.
                                         Survivable for weeks, miserable, and
                                         hygiene risk rises.


Add on top:

- Animals: chicken 0.25–0.5 L/day · rabbit 0.5–1 L · goat 4–8 L · dog 1–2 L ·
   pig 10–20 L
- Garden: in a dry Central European summer, roughly 3–5 L/m²/day for
   intensively cropped beds, much less with heavy mulch and drip irrigation
   (1–2 L/m²/day). A 100 m² garden can want 300 L on a hot day. This is why
   garden water must come from rain, not from your drinking store.


Your household number

  Persons ___ × 20 L         = _____ L/day
  Animals (sum)              = _____ L/day
  Garden (summer only)       = _____ L/day
                               --------
                   TOTAL/day = _____ L/day
          × 14 (L1 target)   = _____ L
          × 90 (L2 target)   = _____ L

For a family of four: 80 L/day, so 1,120 L for two weeks and 7,200 L for three
months. You are not going to store 7,200 L of drinking water in a flat. This
is exactly why the plan is storage + a renewable source + treatment, not
storage alone.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. The three-layer water strategy
---------------------------------

Every household needs all three layers. Storage alone is a countdown timer.

  LAYER 1 — STORED        Immediate, clean, zero effort. Covers days 1–14.
  LAYER 2 — SOURCE        Rain, well, spring, stream, lake. Covers months.
  LAYER 3 — TREATMENT     Turns Layer 2 into Layer 1. The most important layer.

Treatment is the layer people skip and it is the one that decides whether you
get cholera. A household with a stream and no filter is worse off than a
household with 200 L and a Sawyer filter.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Layer 1 — Storage
--------------------


How much, and in what

Target: 15 L/person/day × 14 days = 200 L per person minimum (L1). For L2,
either 600 L/person stored, or 200 L stored plus a proven source.

Container               Capacity          €/unit        Notes
----------------------  ----------------  ------------  ----------------------
Reused 5 L / 10 L PET   5–10 L            Free          Perfect starter.
bottles                                                 Washed, filled, dated.
                                                        Stack in a cool dark
                                                        place. Replace
                                                        annually.

Food-grade jerry can    20 L              €15–25        The workhorse.
                                                        Portable when full (20
                                                        kg). Buy 6–10.

Blue food-grade barrel  60 / 120 / 200 L  €40–90        Cheap per litre. A
                                                        full 200 L drum weighs
                                                        200 kg — you will
                                                        never move it. Site it
                                                        before filling, on a
                                                        solid floor, with a
                                                        siphon pump (€10).

IBC tote (caged tank)   1,000 L           €80–180 used  Best €/litre by far.
                                                        Must be food-grade and
                                                        previously held only
                                                        food (verify!). Opaque
                                                        cover or paint —
                                                        sunlight grows algae.
                                                        Needs a firm level
                                                        base; 1,000 kg.

Bathtub bladder         250–380 L         €25–40        Apartment essential.
(WaterBOB type)                                         Deploy at AMBER,
                                                        before pressure fails.
                                                        Bathtubs themselves
                                                        leak and are filthy;
                                                        the liner solves both.

Stainless/plastic       200–1,000 L       €60–200       For rainwater,
water butt                                              outdoors.


Rules for stored water

- Food-grade containers only (HDPE marked with a "2" or "food safe"). Never
   reuse containers that held chemicals, fuel or milk (milk protein residue is
   impossible to fully remove and will spoil).
- Fill with treated mains water if you have it. If your mains is chlorinated,
   you can store it as-is.
- To store longer than ~6 months, add 2 drops of unscented 5% sodium
   hypochlorite bleach per litre (≈ 2 mg/L free chlorine) at fill time, or use
   a NaDCC tablet at the dose on the packet.
- Date every container. Rotate mains-filled storage every 6–12 months — the
   water doesn't "go off" chemically but plastics leach and biofilm grows.
- Store cool, dark, off concrete floors (use pallets or boards — concrete
   leaches and sweats), away from petrol, paint and solvents (plastics are
   permeable to vapours).
- Freezing splits containers. Leave 10% headspace and keep storage above 0 °C,
   or store in a place you accept losing. Unheated garages in Central Europe
   will freeze.


The hidden water already in your home (know this now)

At the moment pressure fails, you already have:

Source                          Typical volume  How to get it
------------------------------  --------------  ------------------------------
Hot water cylinder / boiler     80–200 L        The biggest single reserve in
tank                                            most homes. Turn off power/gas
                                                to it and close the cold inlet
                                                first, then open the drain
                                                valve at the bottom and a hot
                                                tap upstairs to let air in.
                                                Learn where these valves are
                                                today.

Toilet cisterns                 6–9 L each      Clean water (the tank, not the
                                                bowl). Usable directly.

Pipes in the house              5–15 L          Open the lowest tap in the
                                                building and a high one to
                                                vent.

Ice cubes, drinks, tinned       A few L         Counts in an emergency
vegetable liquid

Central heating system          50–150 L        Not potable — contains
                                                inhibitor chemicals. Fine for
                                                flushing toilets.

Waterbed, aquarium, pool        Large           Not potable; flushing and
                                                washing only. Pool water can
                                                be filtered for washing but is
                                                chemically loaded.


First action at AMBER: fill everything — bath, bladder, jerry cans, pots,
bottles, the washing machine drum, buckets. Water is free and heavy; the
moment you want it, it stops being available.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Layer 2 — Sources you control
--------------------------------


[LAND] Rainwater — the best option for anyone with a roof

The maths (memorise this):

  Litres = Roof footprint (m²) × Rainfall (mm) × 0.8

The 0.8 is the runoff coefficient (losses to splash, evaporation, first-flush,
absorption). Use 0.9 for smooth metal, 0.75–0.8 for tile, 0.7 for rough or
mossy surfaces.

So 1 mm of rain on a 100 m² roof ≈ 80 litres.

A house with a 120 m² roof footprint in a climate with 1,000 mm/year (typical
inland Central Europe; Slovenia is often 1,000–1,500 mm) yields:

  120 × 1000 × 0.8 = 96,000 L per year  ≈  260 L/day averaged

That is more than a family of four needs, from the roof alone. Rain is the
answer for the land track, and the limiting factor is storage, not rainfall.

Sizing storage: size for your longest dry spell, not your annual total. In
Central Europe, plan for a 3–5 week dry summer period. Family of four at 80
L/day × 30 days = 2,400 L, i.e. three IBC totes. Add more if you irrigate: a
garden in a hot July can use 3,000 L/month on its own. Realistic land-track
target: 3,000–6,000 L of rainwater storage (3–6 IBCs, or one buried/above
ground tank).

Build order for a rainwater system

1. Gutters and downpipes — clean, sound, with leaf guards.
2. First-flush diverter — discards the first 0.5–1 mm of each rain event,
    which carries the bird droppings, dust and pollen. Cheap (€30–60) or DIY
    from a vertical pipe with a floating ball. Do not skip this; it does more
    for water quality than any filter downstream.
3. Coarse screen/leaf filter at the tank inlet (1 mm mesh) — keeps out debris,
    leaves and, critically, mosquitoes and rodents.
4. Tank(s), opaque or shaded, with a sealed lid, an overflow routed away from
    the house foundations, and a drain at the bottom for sludge.
5. Take-off above the floor — draw water 10–15 cm above the tank bottom so you
    leave the sediment layer behind (calmed inlet + floating suction is even
    better).
6. Link tanks at the base with a manifold so they fill and draw as one.
7. Treatment before drinking. Always. See Layer 3.

Roof material warnings: Never drink rainwater from asbestos-cement, lead
flashing, tar/bitumen shingles (leaches hydrocarbons), copper with acidic
rain, or any recently treated/painted roof. Clay tile, concrete tile, slate,
glazed tile, and coated steel are fine. If in doubt, use roof water for the
garden, flushing and washing only, and drink from stored/other sources.

Winter: disconnect and drain downpipe filters and exposed pipework before the
first hard frost, or they split. Keep one tank indoors/insulated, or accept
that outdoor tanks are frozen solid in January — which is exactly when you
need them. Partially burying a tank, or building a straw-bale surround, keeps
it liquid.


[LAND] Wells and boreholes

If you have or can get a well, it is the single most valuable resilience asset
a property can have — provided you can lift the water without electricity.

- Shallow/dug well (< 8 m to water): a suction hand pump works. Cheap and
   simple.
- Deep well/borehole (8–80 m): needs a deep-well hand pump (piston pump with
   rods, e.g. Bison/Simple Pump types, €800–2,500) that can often be installed
   alongside the existing electric submersible pump, or a DC solar pump
   (€300–900 for a 12/24 V pump + panel). Both approaches are worth it.
- Test the water (bacteriology + nitrate + arsenic + heavy metals) before you
   rely on it. Nitrate from agricultural land is common and is not removed by
   boiling or ordinary filters — it needs reverse osmosis or ion exchange, and
   it is dangerous for infants.
- Never assume well water is clean because it is clear. Treat it, or test it
   annually.
- Keep a spare pump seal/leather kit, and know how to pull and service the
   pump.


[LAND] Springs, streams, ponds, lakes

Free, renewable, and always contaminated — assume giardia, cryptosporidium, E.
coli, and agricultural/industrial runoff. Usable with full treatment.

- Collect from flowing water where possible; avoid stagnant water and anything
   downstream of fields, roads, septics or livestock.
- Pre-settle turbid water in a bucket for several hours, or filter through
   cloth, before the real filter — silt destroys filter elements fast.
- Note the distance and the effort: 20 L weighs 20 kg. A 200 m round trip four
   times a day is a real daily job. Plan a cart, a yoke, or a gravity line.


[LAND] Snow and ice

1 volume of snow yields roughly 0.1 of water. Melting snow costs a lot of fuel
— never eat snow (it lowers core temperature). Only a last resort.


[FLAT] Apartment sources

You control almost nothing, so storage and pre-emptive filling are everything:

- Bathtub bladder (250–380 L) — deploy at AMBER.
- 8–12 × 20 L jerry cans stored under beds and in cupboards (160–240 L).
- Your hot water cylinder (80–200 L) if you have one — know the drain valve.
- Balcony rain catch: even a 2 m² tarp or an awning funnelled into a butt
   gives 1.6 L per mm of rain — 30–80 L in a decent month. Small but free, and
   ideal for flushing and washing, which is where most of your litres go.
- Building roof: if you can legally access it and your block has a flat roof,
   a single downpipe can supply the whole building. This is a conversation to
   have with your building committee now, not during a crisis. A shared 1,000
   L IBC in a utility room, fed from a roof downpipe, is transformative for a
   block of flats.
- Never rely on being able to carry water up 8 flights with no lift. 20 L per
   trip; a family's daily need is 4 trips. Plan a rope-and-bucket haul from a
   balcony, and store more so you need fewer trips.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Layer 3 — Treatment: filtration and disinfection
---------------------------------------------------

Understand the distinction, because most people get it wrong:

- Filtration physically removes particles, protozoa (giardia, crypto) and — if
   fine enough (≤ 0.2 µm) — bacteria. Most filters do not remove viruses.
- Disinfection (chemical or heat) kills bacteria and viruses but does not
   remove sediment, chemicals, or (for chlorine) cryptosporidium reliably.

Therefore: always do both, in this order.

  SETTLE → PRE-FILTER (cloth/sand) → FILTER (≤0.2 µm) → DISINFECT (chlorine/boil/UV) → STORE CLEAN


Boiling — the gold standard

Bring to a rolling boil for 1 minute (3 minutes above 2,000 m). That kills
everything biological. It does not remove chemicals, nitrate or sediment, and
it costs fuel: roughly 0.1 kWh per litre to heat from 15 °C to boiling — about
1 kg of firewood per 10–15 L, or 90 g of propane per 10 L. Boiling all your
water is fuel-expensive; use it for drinking/cooking water when filters fail,
and filter+chlorinate for the rest.


Chemical disinfection — cheap, light, stores well

Unscented liquid bleach (sodium hypochlorite, 5–6%, no additives/fragrance):

- 2 drops per litre of clear water (4 drops if cloudy or cold).
- ≈ 10 mL (2 tsp) per 40 L, or ≈ 50 mL per 200 L drum.
- Stir, wait 30 minutes (60 min if cold or turbid). You should smell a faint
   chlorine odour afterwards — if not, repeat the dose and wait again.
- Bleach degrades. It loses ~20% potency per year at room temperature, faster
   when warm. Date your bottles, store cool and dark, and replace annually.
   This is why bleach is a poor long-term store.

Calcium hypochlorite granules ("pool shock", 65–70% available chlorine) — the
long-term answer. Stores for years dry and sealed, and 500 g treats roughly
150,000–190,000 litres.

- Make a stock solution: 7 g (≈1 heaped tsp) into 7.5 L of water ≈ a 0.5–1%
   chlorine solution.
- Then dose 1 part stock solution to 100 parts water to treat, and wait 30
   min.
- Safety: buy only the plain calcium hypochlorite type with no algaecide,
   clarifier, stabiliser or anti-fungal additives. Store in a sealed glass or
   HDPE container away from anything flammable, acids, or ammonia — it is a
   strong oxidiser and reacts violently, including with organic matter and
   some plastics. Never store in a metal container. Keep it dry; it releases
   chlorine gas if damp. Store outside the house (shed/garage) if possible.

NaDCC tablets (sodium dichloroisocyanurate — Aquatabs, Katadyn Micropur etc.)

- The most convenient and safest-storing option. 5–10 year shelf life, exact
   dosing, no handling hazard. Typically one tablet per 1 L, 5 L or 20 L
   depending on the product. ~€0.03 per litre treated.
- Buy a mix: a bulk tin of 20 L tablets for the household store, and a small
   bottle of 1 L tablets for the go-bag.
- This should be in every household, both tracks, at every level. Cheap, tiny,
   and it works.

Iodine — works but tastes vile, is less effective against cryptosporidium, and
must not be used long-term by pregnant people or anyone with thyroid disease.
Keep as a backup only.


Filters — what to buy

Minimum recommended kit (both tracks): 1 gravity filter (Berkey-type or
bucket-mounted Sawyers) + 2 spare Sawyer filters + a tin of NaDCC tablets +
500 g calcium hypochlorite (land) or a 1 L bleach bottle rotated annually
(apartment) + a cooking pot you can boil in. Total: €150–400. This covers you
for years.


Turning cloudy water clear

Filters clog fast on silty water. Pre-treat:

1. Settle — stand it in a bucket for 2–12 hours; decant the clear top.
2. Cloth filter — 4–8 layers of cotton (a folded T-shirt or a tea towel).
    Remarkably effective; this alone removes a large share of pathogens
    attached to particles.
3. Coagulate — a pinch of alum (aluminium sulphate) or even crushed Moringa
    seed makes particles clump and settle in 30 minutes. Alum is cheap, stores
    forever, and is worth having.
4. Sand/charcoal pre-filter — gravel, coarse sand, fine sand, charcoal in a
    bucket. Improves clarity and taste. It is a pre-filter, not a purifier.


Storing treated water

Once treated, store in clean, closed, dark containers and use within 2–3 days
unless you added a chlorine residual. Never dip dirty cups into a store — use
a tap or a dedicated pouring vessel. Recontamination at the point of use is
one of the most common causes of illness in a crisis.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Using less — the highest-leverage move
-----------------------------------------

Cutting demand is cheaper than any tank or filter.

- Two-basin dishwashing: wash basin + rinse basin. 4 L does a family's meal
   instead of 20 L of running water. Reuse the rinse water as the next wash
   water.
- Sponge bath / bucket bath: 3–5 L versus 60 L for a shower. A solar shower
   bag (20 L, €15) or a garden-sprayer shower (€20, pressurised, 5 L does two
   people) makes it civilised.
- Navy shower: wet, water off, soap, water on, rinse. 10–15 L.
- Don't flush drinking water. A toilet flush is 6–9 L of potable water. Switch
   to greywater flushing or a bucket toilet (Ch. 05) on day one. For a family
   of four this alone saves 50–80 L/day — the single biggest water saving
   available in any household.
- Cook in ways that keep the water: steam over a pot and reuse the water; cook
   pasta in less water; keep vegetable cooking water for soup or the garden.
- Catch the warm-up water. A bucket under the tap while hot water arrives
   saves 1–3 L per use, several times a day.


7. Greywater — reusing what you already used
--------------------------------------------

Greywater = washing, bathing, laundry, and dish water (not toilet =
blackwater). In a shortage this is free water for flushing and the garden.

- Use it within 24 hours. It turns anaerobic and foul fast.
- Use plant-safe soap if it goes on the garden: no boron, no chlorine bleach,
   low sodium. Simple soap and potash-based soaps are fine; most laundry
   powders are not (high sodium and phosphate build up in soil).
- Apply to soil, never to foliage, and never to root crops or anything eaten
   raw. Fruit trees, berry bushes, sweetcorn, potatoes, and ornamentals are
   ideal.
- Do not store it, do not let it pond, and keep it away from the water source
   and from where children play.
- The simplest system: a basin in the sink → carry to a bucket → flush the
   toilet or water the fruit trees. No plumbing needed, and it's legal
   everywhere.


8. Water safety — the non-negotiables
-------------------------------------

- Assume every untreated source is contaminated. Clear, cold and fast-flowing
   means nothing.
- Separate clean and dirty containers absolutely. Colour-code them. Mark "RAW"
   and "DRINK" in permanent marker. Never let them touch or swap roles.
- Wash hands before handling drinking water. Recontamination at the tap is the
   classic failure.
- Diarrhoea is the emergency. It kills by dehydration, fast, especially in
   children and elderly. Have oral rehydration salts in stock, and know the
   home recipe: 1 L clean water + 6 level teaspoons sugar + ½ level teaspoon
   salt. Sip continuously. This saves lives — it is the single highest-value
   item in your medical kit (see Ch. 06).
- Don't drink from the heating system, the pool, or anything with antifreeze,
   inhibitors or algaecide. Ever.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. Build sequence and shopping
------------------------------


[FLAT] Apartment

------------------------------------------------------------------------------


10. Water checklist
-------------------

  [ ] Household daily requirement calculated and written down
  [ ] L1 storage in place (200 L/person) and dated
  [ ] I know where my mains stopcock is and can close it in the dark
  [ ] I know how to drain my hot water cylinder — and have tried it
  [ ] Filter owned, assembled, and used at least once
  [ ] Spare filter elements in stock
  [ ] Chemical disinfection in stock (NaDCC + bleach or cal-hypo) and in date
  [ ] Clean/dirty containers separated and labelled
  [ ] Rainwater catchment installed and first-flush fitted        (land)
  [ ] Balcony/roof catchment agreed and installed                 (apartment)
  [ ] Non-electric lift from well tested                          (well owners)
  [ ] Source water tested in a lab (bacteria + nitrate)           (well/spring)
  [ ] Greywater route to garden or toilet identified
  [ ] ORS sachets in the medical kit
  [ ] Water-off day drill completed and gaps fixed
  [ ] Frost protection done before winter


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[BUILD] Build it: Water builds — bucket gravity filter · tippy-tap · first-
flush diverter · IBC stand & manifold · slow sand filter · solar water heater
· ollas & drip

Previous: 00 — Planning framework · Next: 02 — Electricity


==============================================================================
4. 02 — ELECTRICITY
==============================================================================


02 — Electricity
================

You do not need "power". You need a short list of specific things to keep
working. Identify that list, shrink it, then build the smallest system that
covers it. Everyone who does it the other way round wastes thousands of euros.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. Step one: the load audit (do this before spending anything)
--------------------------------------------------------------

List every device, its wattage, and hours per day. Multiply. That gives you
watt-hours per day (Wh/day) — the only number that matters for sizing.

  Wh/day = Watts × hours per day
  1,000 Wh = 1 kWh

Measure real wattage with a plug-in energy meter (€10–15). Do not trust labels
— they show peak, not average. Leave the meter on the fridge for 24 hours; the
result will surprise you.


Typical loads

Device              Watts               Typical Wh/day      Notes
------------------  ------------------  ------------------  ------------------
Phone charge        10 W                15–25               Trivial. Two
                                                            phones ≈ 40 Wh

LED bulb / lamp     5–9 W               30–60 for a house   4 lamps × 4 h =
                                                            ~100 Wh

lantern                                                     there is

                                                            electricity.

Power tools         500–1,500 W         Short bursts        Fine with a decent
(drill, grinder)                                            inverter



The three tiers of load

Sort your list into these three buckets. This sorting is the whole exercise.

TIER 1 — LIFE CRITICAL. Must never stop. Medical devices (CPAP, oxygen,
insulin cooling), phone charging, one light, the radio. Typical total: 100–800
Wh/day. Anyone can afford to cover this.

TIER 2 — HIGH VALUE. Protects food, health and sanity. Fridge, freezer, water
pump, more lighting, laptop, router, tool charging, washing machine
occasionally. Typical total: 1,000–2,500 Wh/day.

TIER 3 — COMFORT. Nice, sacrificable. TV, gaming, dishwasher, hair dryer, air
conditioning, electric cooking, electric heating. Typical total: 3,000–20,000
Wh/day. Tier 3 should never touch your off-grid system. Trying to include it
is what makes people conclude "solar is unaffordable". It isn't — Tier 3 is.

Write your three totals here:

  Tier 1: _______ Wh/day      Tier 2: _______ Wh/day      Tier 3: _______ Wh/day


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. Step two: cut the load before you buy generation
---------------------------------------------------

Every watt-hour you remove is a watt-hour you never have to generate, store,
convert or repair. Efficiency is 5–10× cheaper than generation.

Action                             Cost     Saves
---------------------------------  -------  ----------------------------------
Replace all bulbs with LED         €30      80–90% of lighting load
Replace a 10-year-old fridge with  €400     400–900 Wh/day — often pays back
A+++                                        the whole battery

Keep the freezer full (water       Free     10–20%, plus 48 h of cold if power
bottles fill the gaps)                      fails

Move the fridge/freezer out of     Free     Up to 40% in winter
the warm kitchen, into a cool
cellar/garage in winter

Add a thermal cooker/haybox and    €60–120  50–70% of cooking energy
pressure cooker

Wash cold, dry on a line           Free     60–80% of laundry energy
12 V DC LED strips and USB         €40      Avoids 10–15% inverter loss and
charging direct from the battery            lets you switch the inverter off
                                            entirely

Switch the inverter OFF when not   Free     Idle draw is 10–30 W = 240–700
in use                                      Wh/day wasted on nothing

Use headlamps instead of room      €20      90% of lighting
lighting

Kill standby loads with switched   €15      100–400 Wh/day
power strips


Do all of these before buying a single panel. A household that cuts Tier 2
from 2,500 to 1,200 Wh/day halves the cost of its entire power system.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

----------------------------------

                                              machine,         resistive heat

                                                               Wh

              immediately.    house L1        sweet spot       homes with
