Recommendation for almost everybody: build P0 now (this month, €200), then
jump to P1 or P2 as budget allows. P0 alone removes the panic from a blackout.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Sizing the system properly
-----------------------------


Solar — the winter problem

A solar panel's rating is its output in perfect conditions. Real daily yield
depends on peak sun hours (PSH) for your location and month.

Central Europe (≈46 °N), fixed panels at ~35° tilt facing south:

Month              PSH/day    Yield per 1 kW of panels
-----------------  ---------  ------------------------
June–July          5.0 – 5.5  4.0 – 4.5 kWh/day
April / August     4.0 – 4.5  3.2 – 3.6 kWh/day
March / September  3.0        2.4 kWh/day
October            2.0        1.6 kWh/day
November–January   0.8 – 1.2  0.6 – 1.0 kWh/day

Apply a system derate of 0.75–0.8 for wiring, controller, temperature, dirt
and inverter losses — the yields above already include it.

| The rule that matters: size for December, not for June. Winter yield is 4–6×
| worse than summer. In Central Europe you may also get 5–10 consecutive
| overcast days in December with near-zero output.

Worked example — P2 system, family of four:

  Winter critical load (fridge, freezer, lights, comms, pump) = 2,000 Wh/day
  December yield per kW                                        = 0.8 kWh/day
  Panels needed for December autonomy  = 2,000 / 800           = 2.5 kW

2.5 kW of panels costs about €700–1,000 today. In June the same array makes 11
kWh/day — a massive surplus, which is exactly why a winter generator or wood
heat is part of the design, not an admission of failure.

Practical compromises:

- Size panels for ~60–70% of winter need and cover the gap with a generator
   running 1–2 hours on the worst days. This is far cheaper than doubling the
   array.
- Over-panel the charge controller (within its voltage limits) — panels are
   now the cheapest component. A 30% oversize massively improves dull-day
   output.
- Steepen the tilt in winter (latitude + 15°, so ~60° at 46 °N). Adjustable
   mounts gain 20–30% in December and shed snow. Two tilt changes a year is a
   10-minute job with real returns.
- Keep panels clear of snow. A dusted panel makes nothing. Steep tilt + a soft
   brush on a pole.
- Shade is catastrophic, not proportional. One shaded cell can cut a whole
   string's output by 50%+. Survey the winter sun path (the sun is very low
   from November to February — trees and buildings that don't shade in June
   will in December).


Batteries — LiFePO4, essentially always

                          Lead-acid (AGM/flooded)    LiFePO4 (LFP)
------------------------  -------------------------  -------------------------
Usable depth of           50% (going deeper kills    80–100%
discharge                 it)

Cycle life                300–800                    3,000–6,000
Usable kWh from a "5      2.5 kWh                    4.5–5 kWh
kWh" bank

Cost per usable kWh over  High                       3–5× lower
life

Weight                    2–3× heavier               Light
Charging                  Fussy, needs               Simple, fast, sealed
                          absorption/float stages,
                          gasses

Cold charging             Tolerant                   Must not charge below 0
                                                     °C without a heater/BMS
                                                     protection

Upfront cost              €100–150/kWh               €200–350/kWh

Buy LiFePO4 unless you are salvaging free lead-acid. It is cheaper per
delivered kilowatt-hour, safer than other lithium chemistries (no thermal
runaway in normal use), and vastly less maintenance.

Sizing: aim for 1.5 – 2 days of your critical load in usable capacity. Enough
to ride through one bad day and a night without hitting empty.

  Critical load 2,000 Wh/day × 1.5 days = 3,000 Wh usable
  LiFePO4 at 90% usable                → 3.3 kWh nominal → buy 4–5 kWh

Cold weather: keep the battery indoors, above 5 °C. A LiFePO4 bank in an
unheated garage in January will refuse to charge and you will think it's
broken. Either buy cells with built-in low-temperature cut-off and heating, or
site the bank inside.


Charge controller

- MPPT, always. It harvests 15–30% more than PWM, more in cold and dull
   conditions, and lets you use cheap high-voltage panels with a low-voltage
   battery. The price difference is €50.
- Size the controller for panel current with headroom (e.g. 2.5 kW at 24 V ≈
   100 A → a 100 A MPPT).
- Buy a spare controller. It is the most likely electronic failure point, it's
   small and cheap, and without it your panels and battery are decorations.


Inverter

- Pure sine wave. Modified sine damages motors, fridges, chargers, and medical
   devices, and buzzes in audio. Never buy modified sine for a household.
- Size for peak surge, not average. A fridge compressor or a well pump draws
   3–6× its running wattage for a second or two at start. A 500 W pump may
   need a 2,000–3,000 W inverter.
- Low-frequency (transformer) inverters handle surge far better than high-
   frequency ones — worth the extra cost and weight if you must start pumps.
- Idle consumption matters enormously. A 3 kW inverter idling at 25 W burns
   600 Wh/day doing nothing — potentially a third of your generation. Switch
   it off when it isn't needed, and run lights and USB directly from 12 V DC.
- A hybrid/all-in-one inverter-charger (inverter + MPPT + mains charger +
   transfer switch in one box) is the simplest choice for P2/P3 and handles
   grid/generator/solar changeover automatically.


System voltage

- 12 V for P0/P1 (huge choice of cheap 12 V appliances, pumps and lights).
- 24 V or 48 V for P2/P3 (thinner cables, less loss, cheaper per watt).
- Rule of thumb: over ~1.5 kW of load, go 24 V or 48 V.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Generators — the honest assessment
-------------------------------------

A generator is a fuel-to-electricity converter, not an energy source. Its
endurance equals your fuel store, and fuel is the hardest thing to store.

Fuel maths. A small inverter generator makes roughly 2.5–3.5 kWh per litre of
petrol at good load, much worse when lightly loaded. A 5 kWh day therefore
costs ~2 L/day → 60 L/month → 180 L for three months. Storing 180 L of petrol
safely is a serious undertaking, and petrol only keeps 6–12 months with
stabiliser.

Therefore: never run a generator to supply loads directly all day. Instead:

| The right pattern — batch charging. Run the generator at 60–80% load (its
| efficient point) for 1–2 hours, charging the battery bank and running the
| fridge/freezer/washing machine/tools at the same time. Then shut it down and
| live off the battery. This cuts fuel use by 60–80%, quarters the noise
| exposure, and multiplies engine life.

Choosing one

- Inverter generators (Honda EU22i and clones): quiet (~50–60 dB), clean power
   safe for electronics, fuel-efficient at part load. 2–2.5 kW is enough for
   battery charging plus a fridge. ~€500–1,500.
- Dual-fuel (petrol + LPG) is the strategic choice: propane stores
   indefinitely, burns clean, doesn't gum the carburettor, and is easy to buy
   and store in 11 kg cylinders. ~€600–1,200.
- Conventional open-frame 5–7 kW sets are cheap and loud, and produce dirty
   power. Fine for tools and pumps, poor for electronics and terrible for
   OPSEC.
- Diesel stores longer than petrol (1–2 years, longer with biocide and a
   sealed tank), is more efficient, but the small sets are loud and expensive.

Fuel storage

Fuel           Shelf life                      Notes
-------------  ------------------------------  -------------------------------
Petrol         3–6 months bare · 12–24 months  Buy ethanol-free if available;
               with stabiliser (Stabil/Aspen)  ethanol absorbs water and
                                               destroys carburettors. Alkylate
                                               petrol (Aspen) stores 3–5 years
                                               but is expensive.

Diesel         12 months · 2–5 years with      Watch for diesel bug (microbial
               biocide + stabiliser, sealed,   growth) — use a biocide
               cool

Propane / LPG  Indefinite                      The best fuel to store. 11 kg
                                               cylinder ≈ 150 kWh of heat. No
                                               degradation, no spillage, no
                                               fire risk if stored outside and
                                               upright.


- Store fuel outside the house in approved containers, in the shade, away from
   ignition sources, and check your local legal storage limits (often 20–30 L
   of petrol domestically).
- Rotate petrol through your car every 6 months — that's the trick that makes
   fuel storage practical.
- Keep the generator's own consumables: oil, oil filter, air filter, spark
   plugs, and a spare carburettor (€20 and the #1 failure item on small
   engines).

Safety — this kills people every single winter

- NEVER run a generator indoors, in a garage, in a cellar, in a porch, or near
   an open window. Carbon monoxide is odourless and kills in minutes. Not
   "with the door open". Outside, ≥ 5 m from any opening, with the exhaust
   pointed away.
- CO alarms in every sleeping area and in the room with any combustion. €20
   each. Non-negotiable. See Ch. 04.
- Never backfeed a generator into a wall socket to power the house. It kills
   line workers, destroys your appliances, and is illegal. Use a manual
   transfer switch or a breaker interlock kit, installed by an electrician
   (€150–400). Without one, run extension leads to specific appliances only.
- Let it cool before refuelling. Petrol on a hot exhaust is a fire.


Other generation, honestly assessed

- Wind: small turbines rarely produce what's claimed. Only worth it on an
   exposed site with genuine average wind ≥ 5 m/s, on a tall mast. Most
   domestic wind is money better spent on panels. But: wind produces in winter
   when solar doesn't, so a good site makes an excellent complement.
- Micro-hydro: if you have a stream with head and flow, this is the best off-
   grid power there is — 24/7, year-round, weather-independent. Even 200 W
   continuous = 4.8 kWh/day, better than a large solar array in winter.
   Investigate seriously if you have water rights.
- Pedal generator: a fit person sustains 50–100 W. One hour of hard pedalling
   ≈ 75 Wh ≈ what a €30 solar panel makes in an afternoon for free. Good for
   morale and fitness; not a power strategy.
- Thermoelectric (stove-top TEG, Peltier): 5–15 W. Charges a phone off a wood
   stove. A nice redundancy, not a system.
- Car alternator: your car is a 1–2 kW generator you already own. A €30
   inverter on the cigarette socket gives 150 W; wired directly to the battery
   with a proper 1,000 W inverter it will run a fridge while idling. Idling
   burns ~1 L/h, so use it as an emergency charger, not a habit. Same CO rules
   — never in a garage.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Wiring it safely
-------------------

- DC side: correct cable gauge for current and run length (undersized DC cable
   is the classic fire cause — voltage drop under 3%), fuse or breaker within
   30 cm of the battery positive, fuses on every branch, a main isolator
   switch you can reach in the dark.
- Battery location: ventilated, above freezing, not in the living space if
   lead-acid (hydrogen gas), secured so it can't shift, not above or below
   anything precious.
- AC side: anything connecting to house wiring must be done by a qualified
   electrician, with an RCD/GFCI and correct earthing, and must be legal in
   your jurisdiction. A transfer switch or interlock is mandatory.
- Grid-tied solar does NOT work in a blackout. Standard grid-tie inverters
   shut down when the grid drops (anti-islanding), by law. If you already have
   solar, check whether it has a hybrid inverter with battery and an
   EPS/backup output — if not, your panels are useless in exactly the
   situation you bought them for. Retrofitting a hybrid inverter + battery is
   the fix.
- Label everything. Breakers, cables, polarity, what circuit feeds what. Write
   the system diagram on paper and pin it next to the equipment. In six
   months, at 3 a.m., in the dark, you will not remember.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Keeping food cold without much power
---------------------------------------

This is usually the biggest single reason to have power at all.

- A full chest freezer holds food safely for 48 hours unopened (24 h for an
   upright fridge-freezer). Fill air gaps with bottles of water — the frozen
   mass is thermal storage.
- Freeze bottles of water as your "battery" — move them into the fridge
   compartment during outages.
- Chest beats upright by a wide margin (cold air doesn't fall out when
   opened). A chest freezer plus a thermostat controller can also run as a
   fridge at a fraction of a normal fridge's energy (~100–200 Wh/day).
- Duty-cycle it: run the freezer hard while the sun is up, let it coast
   overnight. A well-insulated full freezer barely drifts.
- Winter is free refrigeration. From November to March in Central Europe, an
   insulated box on the north side of the house, a cellar, or an unheated
   pantry does the job for nothing. Plan the year so freezer contents are
   lowest in summer — preserve by canning and drying instead (Ch. 03).
- Root cellar (land track): a properly built cellar holds 2–8 °C year-round
   with zero energy and stores potatoes, roots, apples and cabbages for
   months. It is the highest-return "appliance" on a homestead. See Ch. 03 §7.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. Lighting — cheap, and hugely important for morale
----------------------------------------------------

- Headlamps first. 1–3 W, hands free, directional. Buy one per person plus two
   spares. This is the most cost-effective light in existence.
- Rechargeable AA/AAA (Eneloop-type) + a USB charger. ~€60 for 16 cells and a
   charger, and then you never buy batteries again. Standardise the whole
   house on one cell size so everything is interchangeable.
- 12 V LED strip along a ceiling, run direct from the battery: 5 W lights a
   whole room and needs no inverter.
- Solar garden lights — bring them in at night. Crude but free and endless.
- Candles and oil lamps: useful backup, but they are a fire risk, consume
   oxygen and produce soot and CO. Keep them for ambience and last resort,
   never unattended, never near children. Paraffin/lamp oil stores
   indefinitely.
- Light discipline: blackout curtains. In a dark street, a lit window is a
   billboard. See Ch. 10.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. Build sequence
-----------------


[FLAT] Apartment

1. Now (€150): 2 × 20,000 mAh power banks · 100 W folding panel with USB/DC ·
    4 headlamps · 16 rechargeable AA + charger · wind-up/solar radio.
2. L1 (€400–800): A 500–1,000 Wh portable power station (LiFePO4, pure sine,
    e.g. EcoFlow/Bluetti/Anker class) + 200 W of folding or balcony panels.
    This single purchase covers lights, phones, laptop, router and a CPAP —
    and it's plug-and-play, which matters when you cannot modify the building.
3. L2 (€1,200–2,500): 1.5–2 kWh station + 400 W balcony solar (check local
    rules — plug-in "balcony solar" up to 600–800 W is now legal in several
    European countries) + a 12 V compressor cool box (40 W) as an efficient
    fridge.
4. Never a generator in a flat. There is nowhere safe to run it.


[LAND] Land / house

1. Now (€200): P0 kit as above, plus a plug-in energy meter and LED
    conversion.
2. L1 (€900–1,800): 400–600 W panels · 100 Ah/12 V LiFePO4 (1.2 kWh) · 30 A
    MPPT · 1,000–1,500 W pure sine inverter · proper fusing and cable · 12 V
    LED lighting circuit.
3. L2 (€4,000–8,000): 2–3 kW panels on an adjustable mount · 5–10 kWh LiFePO4
    at 24/48 V · 3 kW hybrid inverter-charger · transfer switch installed by
    an electrician · 2.2 kW dual-fuel inverter generator + 4 × 11 kg propane
    cylinders · A+++ chest freezer · spare MPPT controller, spare fuses, spare
    cable, spare connectors.
4. L3 (€12,000+): 5 kW+ array · 15 kWh storage · well pump on DC · micro-hydro
    or wind if the site supports it · full house backup circuits · second
    inverter held as a spare.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


10. Electricity checklist
-------------------------

  [ ] Energy meter bought; fridge/freezer actually measured
  [ ] Loads sorted into Tier 1 / 2 / 3 and totals written down
  [ ] All efficiency measures done BEFORE buying generation
  [ ] Tier 1 (life-critical) covered by battery alone, with 2 days of autonomy
  [ ] System sized on DECEMBER sun, not annual average
  [ ] Pure sine inverter; surge rating checked against the biggest motor
  [ ] Battery sited above 5 °C
  [ ] Spare charge controller, fuses, cable and connectors in stock
  [ ] DC fusing correct and a main isolator fitted within reach
  [ ] Transfer switch/interlock installed if a generator feeds the house
  [ ] CO alarms fitted; generator siting rule written and understood by everyone
  [ ] Fuel stored legally and safely, dated, with stabiliser, rotation scheduled
  [ ] System diagram drawn on paper and pinned up
  [ ] Existing grid-tie solar checked for blackout (EPS) capability
  [ ] Headlamps + rechargeables for every person, one battery size
  [ ] Blackout weekend drill completed in WINTER


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[BUILD] Build it: Power builds — the complete 12 V solar system with cable
sizing and fusing · DC lighting circuit · insulated battery box · adjustable
tilt frame · generator noise screen

Previous: 01 — Water · Next: 03 — Food storage


==============================================================================
5. 03 — FOOD STORAGE
==============================================================================


03 — Food Storage
=================

Stored food is the cheapest, fastest, most certain preparation you can make.
It is also the one people get most wrong — by storing the wrong things, in the
wrong way, in the wrong quantities, and then never eating any of it.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. The calorie maths
--------------------

Everything starts here. Not "cans" or "boxes" — kilocalories.

Person                    kcal/day sedentary  kcal/day with manual work
------------------------  ------------------  -------------------------
Adult man                 2,400               3,200 – 4,000
Adult woman               1,900               2,600 – 3,200
Teenager                  2,200 – 2,800       3,000 – 3,600
Child 4–10                1,400 – 1,900       —
Toddler                   1,000 – 1,300       —
Pregnant / breastfeeding  +300 / +500         —

Crisis life is manual work. Hauling water, chopping wood, digging, hand
washing, walking instead of driving — and in a cold house you burn several
hundred extra kcal a day just staying warm. Plan on 2,800–3,000 kcal/day for
working adults. Undereating is not a minor discomfort: it destroys your
ability to do the physical work the situation demands, wrecks your immune
system exactly when infection is the threat, and sinks morale.


Your household's requirement

  Adults ___ × 2,800 kcal   = __________
  Others (from table)       = __________
                              ----------
              TOTAL / day   = __________ kcal
  
  × 90 days   (L2) = __________ kcal
  × 365 days  (L3) = __________ kcal

Family of four (2 adults, 2 children): ≈ 9,000 kcal/day → 810,000 kcal for 3
months → 3,285,000 kcal for a year.

Converting to weight: staple dry goods average roughly 3,400 kcal/kg (grains
3,400 · pasta 3,600 · beans 3,400 · sugar 4,000 · oil 8,800 · rice 3,600).

  3,285,000 kcal ÷ 3,400 kcal/kg ≈ 965 kg of dry staples for a family of four per year

That is about 1 tonne of food, occupying roughly 1.5–2 m³ — a stack of about
50 × 20 L buckets, or one corner of a cellar, garage or spare room (2 m × 1 m
× 1 m). It is far less space than people imagine, and at bulk prices costs
roughly €900–1,600. That is the single best money in this entire plan.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. The one-year staple base (per adult)
---------------------------------------

This is the classic, proven long-term storage baseline, adapted metric. It
delivers roughly 2,300–2,600 kcal/day with adequate protein. Multiply by your
household; scale children at 0.6–0.8.

Category            kg/adult/year  Examples
------------------  -------------  -------------------------------------------
Grains              180 kg         Wheat berries, rice, oats, maize, barley,
                                   pasta, flour

Legumes             30 kg          Dry beans, lentils, chickpeas, split peas,
                                   soy

Fats & oils         12 kg (≈13 L)  Olive oil, sunflower oil, coconut oil,
                                   ghee, lard, tallow

Sugars              20 kg          Sugar, honey, jam, syrup, dried fruit
Dairy               8 kg           Whole/skim milk powder, hard cheese, UHT,
                                   butter powder

Salt                4 kg           Iodised table + coarse/pickling salt for
                                   preserving

Protein extras      15–25 kg       Tinned fish/meat, dried eggs, TVP, nuts,
                                   seeds, jerky

Vegetables & fruit  40–60 kg       Tinned, dried, freeze-dried, plus your own
                                   preserves

Extras              as needed      Yeast, baking powder, bicarbonate, vinegar,
                                   spices, tea, coffee, bouillon, cocoa


For a family of four, one year: ~720 kg grain, 120 kg legumes, 50 L oil, 80 kg
sugar, 32 kg dairy powder, 16 kg salt.


Do not build this as a monolith

Split your storage into three layers, because they serve different purposes:

Layer A — Everyday deep pantry (2–8 weeks). Ordinary food you eat weekly, just
more of it. Tins, pasta, rice, coffee, oil, UHT milk, frozen goods. Rotates
naturally. Build this first — it covers 90% of realistic events.

Layer B — Bulk staples (3–12 months). Sacks of grain, beans, sugar, salt and
oil, packed for the long term. Cheap per calorie, boring, requires cooking
skill and fuel. This is your insurance layer.

Layer C — Morale and micronutrients. Coffee, tea, chocolate, spices, alcohol,
sweets, sauces, vitamins, sprouting seed. Weighs almost nothing, costs little,
and is worth far more than its calories. Month four of lentils with no
seasoning breaks people. This layer is not a luxury; it is what makes Layer B
edible for a year.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Shelf life — what actually lasts
-----------------------------------


Effectively indefinite (25–30+ years, packed properly)

White rice · wheat berries · maize/corn · rolled oats · dry beans (they get
harder — cook with a pressure cooker) · lentils · pasta · sugar · salt
(forever) · honey (forever) · milk powder · dried potato flakes · baking soda
· freeze-dried food.


Long (2–10 years)

Tinned food (long past the date if the tin is sound — see below) · white flour
(1–2 yrs; wheat berries are far better) · dried fruit · nuts in the shell ·
vinegar · spirits · bouillon · vitamins (2–3 yrs) · TVP.


Short — rotate hard (6 months – 2 years)

- Brown rice: 6–12 months. The bran oil goes rancid. Store white rice for the
   long term; brown rice only in your rotating pantry.
- Wholemeal flour: 3–6 months. Store the whole grain and mill it as needed.
- Cooking oils: 1–2 years. Olive and coconut keep best; sunflower and rapeseed
   go rancid faster. Store cool and dark, buy in tins or dark glass, and
   rotate relentlessly. Fat is both the hardest thing to store and the hardest
   thing to produce. Consider ghee (2 yrs at room temperature) and rendered
   lard/tallow (1 yr cool, longer canned or frozen).
- Nuts and seeds shelled: 6–12 months (freeze to extend).
- Coffee beans: 1–2 years sealed; ground, much less. Green (unroasted) coffee
   beans keep 5+ years — buy green and roast in a pan.
- Anything with fat in it is on a clock. This is the rule that governs food
   storage.


About "best before" dates

On shelf-stable food these are quality dates, not safety dates. Tinned goods
stored cool are usually good for 5–10 years and often far longer; quality and
vitamin content decline, safety usually does not. Exceptions — throw away
without tasting: any tin that is bulging, leaking, rusted through, or spurts
when opened; anything that smells or looks wrong; any home preserve with a
failed seal. Botulism has no taste and no smell. High-acid tinned foods
(tomatoes, fruit, anything vinegared) degrade fastest and can corrode the tin
from inside — rotate those on the printed date.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. How to pack for 25 years
---------------------------

The enemies are oxygen, moisture, light, heat, and pests. Remove all five.

The standard method — the only one worth learning:

  Dry staple  →  Mylar bag (≥ 5 mil / 100 µm)  →  O2 absorber  →  heat-seal
              →  food-grade bucket (20 L) with gasket lid  →  cool, dark, dry

1. Mylar bags, 5–7 mil, 20 L size (typically 50 × 60 cm). €1–2 each. Mylar is
    an oxygen and light barrier; plastic buckets alone are not (they breathe).
2. Oxygen absorbers. Sizing: 2,000 cc per 20 L bucket (or 2 × 1,000 cc);
    300–500 cc for a 4 L bag. Work fast — once the packet is open, absorbers
    are live; keep unused ones in a sealed jar with the air squeezed out. A
    correctly sealed bag goes hard and "bricked" within a day — that is your
    proof it worked.
3. Seal with a hair straightener, clothes iron on a board, or an impulse
    sealer (€30). Leave one corner open, press out the air, seal the last
    corner.
4. Food-grade bucket with a gamma-seal or gasket lid. The bucket is pest and
    crush protection; the mylar does the real work.
5. Moisture must be < 10% going in, or you create a botulism risk in an
    anaerobic bag. Shop-dry grains and beans are fine. Never pack anything you
    dried yourself unless it is cracker-dry, and never pack anything with fat
    (nuts, brown rice, ground flour) this way for the long term.
6. Label the outside AND put a label inside: contents, weight, date packed,
    date to review. You will not remember. Use permanent marker and tape; ink
    fades.

Alternatives: food-grade 200 L barrels with a bag liner for grain in bulk;
glass jars with O2 absorbers for small quantities (rodent-proof and reusable —
excellent for apartments); vacuum sealing (good for 1–5 years, but the bags
are oxygen-permeable over decades and puncture easily).

Pest control: weevil eggs are already in most grain when you buy it. Freezing
a sack for 72 hours at −18 °C kills eggs and larvae — do this before packing.
Diatomaceous earth (food grade) mixed at ~1% also works and is harmless.
O2-absorbed mylar kills insects anyway by suffocation, which is a large part
of why the method works.

Storage conditions. Cool is everything: shelf life roughly halves for every
+10 °C. Aim for 10–18 °C, dark, dry, off the floor on pallets or boards, away
from chemicals and fuel. A hot loft is the worst place in the house; a cellar
or a north-facing interior room is the best.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. The nutrition trap
---------------------

A year of grain, beans, sugar and oil will keep you alive and will slowly make
you ill. Long-term storage diets are reliably deficient in:

Nutrient            Deficiency                    Fix
------------------  ----------------------------  ----------------------------
Vitamin C           Scurvy in 8–12 weeks.         Sprouts (Ch. 09) ·
                    Bleeding gums, wounds that    sauerkraut and other lacto-
                    won't heal, exhaustion, then  ferments · rosehips ·
                    death.                        dried/tinned fruit · vitamin
                                                  C tablets (dirt cheap, buy 3
                                                  years' worth — this is the
                                                  single best-value item in
                                                  your store)

Vitamin A           Night blindness, immune       Carrots (store well),
                    failure                       squash, dried greens, liver,
                                                  cod liver oil, multivitamin

Vitamin D           Immune and bone problems —    Supplement (everyone, every
                    and near-universal in         winter), oily fish, eggs,
                    Central European winters      sunlight
                    even in normal times

B12                 Neurological damage, anaemia  Tinned fish/meat, eggs,
                                                  dairy powder, fortified
                                                  yeast, supplement

Folate, B-complex   Anaemia, fatigue              Sprouts, legumes, greens,
                                                  supplement

Omega-3             Inflammation, mood,           Tinned oily fish
                    cognition                     (sardines/mackerel — cheap,
                                                  long shelf life, outstanding
                                                  value), linseed/flax,
                                                  walnuts

Iron & zinc         Anaemia, poor immunity, slow  Meat, legumes + vitamin C to
                    healing                       aid absorption, cast-iron
                                                  cooking

Fibre & gut health  Constipation, misery          Whole grains, ferments,
                                                  vegetables

Fat                 Energy collapse, vitamin      Oils, nuts, fatty meat. Do
                    A/D/E/K absorption failure    not build a low-fat store.

Complete protein    Muscle loss, poor healing     Combine grains + legumes at
                                                  the same meal (rice + beans,
                                                  bread + lentils) — together
                                                  they are a complete protein


The three cheap interventions that fix most of this:

1. A jar of sprouting seed (mung, lentil, alfalfa, radish, broccoli,
    fenugreek) — 5 kg costs €25, stores for years, and gives fresh vitamin-C-
    rich food in 3–5 days, indoors, with no light, soil or power, in any
    season. It is the single most important nutritional item in the whole
    plan.
2. A multivitamin + vitamin C + vitamin D per person per day for a year.
    ~€40/person/year. Rotate every 2–3 years.
3. Cabbage and salt. A 5 kg crock of sauerkraut needs nothing but cabbage,
    salt and time, keeps for months, and is a serious source of vitamin C and
    gut flora.

Special diets need special planning: infants (formula has a hard expiry and no
substitute — plan explicitly and generously), coeliac, diabetic (a high-carb
storage diet is dangerous — build around legumes, fats and proteins instead),
allergies. These cannot be improvised and must be solved in GREEN.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Preservation — turning harvests into stores
----------------------------------------------

This is the skill set that converts "I grew food" into "I have food in
February".


Lacto-fermentation — start here

Easiest, cheapest, needs no power, no equipment beyond a jar, and adds
nutrition (vitamin C, B vitamins, probiotics).

- Brine: 2–3% salt by weight (20–30 g salt per litre of water, or per kg of
   vegetables for dry-salted kraut).
- Keep everything under the brine — exposure to air is what spoils it.
- Ferment at 18–22 °C for 3–14 days, then move somewhere cold (cellar, 2–10
   °C) where it keeps for 6–12 months.
- Works on: cabbage (sauerkraut), cucumbers, carrots, beetroot, turnips,
   beans, peppers, garlic, mixed vegetables, hot sauce.
- Buy salt in bulk. It never expires, it is essential to almost every
   preservation method, and it is one of the few things you genuinely cannot
   produce at home inland.


Water-bath canning — high-acid foods only

For pH ≤ 4.6: fruit, jams, pickles, tomatoes with added acid, chutneys,
anything vinegared. Jars are submerged in boiling water for a set time.
Equipment: a big pot with a rack, jars, and new lids every time (screw bands
are reusable, sealing lids are not — stock several hundred, or buy the
European style with replaceable rubber rings).


Pressure canning — the homestead game-changer

The only safe way to preserve low-acid foods: meat, poultry, fish, stock,
beans, and all non-acidified vegetables. It reaches 116–121 °C, which is what
destroys Clostridium botulinum spores. Boiling water canning cannot do this,
and the failure mode is fatal.

- A quality pressure canner (All American / Presto class) is €200–400 and is
   arguably the highest-value single tool a homestead can own: it lets you
   store meat, stock and vegetables without a freezer and without electricity
   once the jars are done.
- Follow a tested, published recipe for time and pressure. Every time.
   (National Center for Home Food Preservation / Ball Blue Book — print them.)
   This is not a place for improvisation or old family guesswork.
- Adjust pressure for altitude.
- Buy: the canner, a spare gasket and safety plug, 200+ jars, 500 lids, a jar
   lifter, a funnel.


Drying / dehydrating

Ancient, cheap, and space-efficient — dried food is ~20% of its wet weight.

- Solar drier (a glazed box with mesh trays and a vent) costs €40 in materials
   and uses no power. Ideal in a Central European August.
- Electric dehydrator (€60–150, ~300–500 W) is faster and weather-independent
   — run it off surplus solar at midday.
- Works on: fruit, tomatoes, mushrooms, herbs, onions, peppers, beans,
   courgette, and meat (jerky — must be lean; fat goes rancid).
- Store dried food in jars or mylar with an O2 absorber. It must be crisp-dry,
   or it moulds.


Root cellaring — zero energy, huge capacity

A cellar, a buried barrel, a clamp or an unheated north-facing pantry holding
2–8 °C at 85–95% humidity will keep, with no power at all:

- Potatoes (dark, 4–8 °C — never below 4 °C or they go sweet): 5–8 months
- Carrots, beetroot, parsnip, turnip, celeriac (in damp sand): 4–6 months
- Cabbage (hung by the root): 3–5 months · Onions & garlic (dry, cool, airy,
   0–5 °C): 6–9 months · Apples (wrapped, away from potatoes): 3–6 months ·
   Winter squash & pumpkins (dry, 10–15 °C — warmer than the rest): 4–8 months
- Keep apples separate from everything: their ethylene sprouts potatoes and
   spoils neighbours.
- A clamp (potatoes/roots in a heap on straw, covered with straw then 20 cm of
   soil, with a ventilation chimney) costs nothing and works.


Salting, curing and smoking

Salt pork, bacon, ham, salt fish, biltong. Effective, needs care with nitrates
for anything cured cold and slow. Learn from a good book before you have a
carcass and no freezer. Simple dry-salting of pork belly and legs is reliable
and ancient; cold-smoked charcuterie is an advanced skill with a real risk.


Fat preservation

Rendered lard and tallow keep 6–12 months in a cool dark place, longer canned.
Confit — meat cooked slowly in fat and stored submerged in it — keeps for
months in a cellar. Fat sealing is an excellent, half-forgotten technique.


Freezing

Fast and easy, but it is the least resilient method — it depends on
uninterrupted power for its entire life. Use the freezer as your working
buffer, not your strategic store, and plan to empty it into jars, the
dehydrator and the smoker if power becomes unreliable. Keep a bag of ice or a
frozen bottle in the freezer with a coin on top: if you return to find the
coin at the bottom, it thawed and refroze while you were away — do not eat the
contents.


Eggs without refrigeration

Unwashed fresh eggs keep 2–4 weeks at room temperature and 2–3 months in a
cool cellar — the natural bloom is intact, so don't wash them until use. Water
glassing (submerging clean, unwashed, fresh eggs in a 1:10 solution of
pickling lime / calcium hydroxide) keeps them edible for 12–18 months without
power. This is the classic way to carry spring's egg glut through winter.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Rotation — the discipline that makes it real
-----------------------------------------------

Stored food you never touch is money spent on rubbish you will throw away.

The rules:

1. Eat what you store, store what you eat. Every item in Layer A and most of
    Layer B should appear in your normal cooking.
2. FIFO. New goes to the back. Physically arrange shelves so this happens
    automatically (a can rotator, or simply loading from one end).
3. Date everything with a marker on purchase, not just the printed date.
4. Keep a written inventory. Use worksheets/inventory-template.csv. Update it
    when you open a bucket and when you shop. An unknown store is not a store.
5. The quarterly "eat-the-store week" — cook only from storage for one week
    every three months. It finds the gaps (no yeast! no oil! nobody likes the
    dried soya!) while fixing them is still trivial.
6. Learn to cook it now. Dry beans need soaking and 1–2 hours of fuel (30
    minutes in a pressure cooker). Wheat berries need a mill. Powdered milk
    needs technique to taste acceptable. Sourdough needs a starter you keep
    alive. Discovering this in month two is a disaster; discovering it this
    Saturday is a pleasant afternoon.
7. Buy in tranches, not in one panic. A little extra every shop builds a year
    of food invisibly, without straining budget or supply.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. Tools that make stored food usable
-------------------------------------

Tool                         €                     Why it matters

------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. Build sequence
-----------------


[FLAT] Apartment

Space is the constraint. ~1 m³ per person-year of staples if densely packed.

- Under every bed (flat boxes, 20 L bags), in the back of every wardrobe, on
   top of kitchen units, under the sofa, in the hallway cupboard, on a shelf
   above the doors. A 60 cm × 200 cm under-bed space holds 150 kg of food.
- Favour calorie-dense, low-volume, no-cook-needed items: oil, nuts, pasta,
   rice, sugar, honey, powdered milk, tinned fish, peanut butter.
- Glass jars with O2 absorbers on shelves are both storage and rodent-
   proofing, and they look like an ordinary pantry rather than a stockpile.
- Fuel is the hidden constraint — dry beans need an hour of gas. Store more
   quick-cooking items (pasta, couscous, bulgur, instant oats, tinned/ready
   foods) than a house would, plus a thermal cooker (Ch. 04).
- Target: L1 (2 weeks) easily; L2 (3 months) with discipline; L3 (12 months)
   only in a large flat with a cellar or storage unit.


[LAND] Land / house

- Dedicate a cool room, cellar or insulated garage corner. Shelving, pallets,
   a thermometer/hygrometer, and rodent control (steel, not plastic, bins).
- Buy bulk direct: farm co-ops, mills, agricultural merchants, wholesale food
   suppliers. 25 kg sacks are typically 30–60% cheaper per kilo than
   supermarket packs.
- Build or convert a root cellar — it multiplies your storage capacity for
   almost nothing and works forever.
- Then integrate with production: what you grow (Ch. 07) and raise (Ch. 08)
   tops up the store each year instead of draining it.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


10. Food checklist
------------------

  [ ] Household daily kcal requirement calculated
  [ ] 2-week deep pantry of normal food in place (L1)
  [ ] 3-month staple base bought and packed (L2)
  [ ] Packing method understood; mylar + O2 absorbers + buckets in stock
  [ ] Grain frozen 72 h before packing (weevils)
  [ ] Everything labelled and dated, inside and out
  [ ] Written inventory exists and is current
  [ ] Storage is cool (< 18 °C), dark, dry, off the floor, rodent-proof
  [ ] Fat/oil stock present and on a rotation schedule
  [ ] Salt: 4 kg per person per year, in bulk
  [ ] Vitamin C, vitamin D and multivitamins for 12 months per person
  [ ] Sprouting seed (5 kg+) and a sprouting jar/tray
  [ ] Hand grain mill owned AND used at least once
  [ ] Pressure cooker owned and used
  [ ] Pressure canner owned, tested, tested recipes printed  (land/L3)
  [ ] Manual can openers ×3
  [ ] Special diets (infant, coeliac, diabetic) explicitly planned
  [ ] Pet food included in the plan
  [ ] Quarterly eat-the-store week scheduled in the calendar
  [ ] Comfort layer: coffee, spices, chocolate, treats — do not skip this


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[BUILD] Build it: Food storage builds — root cellar · buried barrel cellar ·
root clamp · cool pantry · mylar packing station · cold smoker · curing racks

Previous: 02 — Electricity · Next: 04 — Heating, cooking & fuel


==============================================================================
6. 04 — HEATING, COOKING & FUEL
==============================================================================


04 — Heating, Cooking & Fuel
============================

In a Central European winter, heat is a bigger energy problem than
electricity, food and water combined — by an order of magnitude. And the ways
people improvise heat and cooking indoors kill more people in a crisis than
the crisis does.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. The scale of the problem
---------------------------

A typical Central European house loses 8,000–20,000 kWh of heat per winter (an
uninsulated older house much more; a passive house a tenth of it). Compare
that with a 5 kWh battery.

  Poorly insulated 120 m² house in January:  60–100 kWh/day of heat
  A 10 kWh battery bank:                     10 kWh, once
  A 3 kW solar array in January:              2.4 kWh/day

You cannot heat a house with electricity in a crisis. Do not try. Every
electric heater, oil-filled radiator and fan heater in your house is useless
without the grid, and running them off batteries or a generator is absurd — 2
kW for one room is more than most whole off-grid systems produce in a day.

The strategy has three parts, in strict order:

  1. SHRINK the space you heat          (free, biggest win)
  2. REDUCE the heat that escapes it    (cheap, second biggest win)
  3. SUPPLY heat from stored fuel       (expensive, do last)


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. Step one: the warm room
--------------------------

Heat one room, not a house. This is how Europe lived for a thousand years and
it works.

- Pick the room: small, south-facing if possible, with a chimney or flue if
   you have one, away from the coldest walls, with a door that closes.
- The whole household lives and sleeps there in the coldest weeks. Mattresses
   on the floor. It is cramped, it is also warm, and bodies add heat (each
   person is ~80–100 W).
- Close doors to every other room. Close off the upstairs. Hang blankets over
   doorways and at the bottom of stairs (stairwells are chimneys).
- Keep the rest of the house above freezing (5 °C) to protect pipes, not
   comfortable. If you cannot, drain the plumbing in the unheated parts before
   the first hard frost: burst pipes flood a house you cannot get a plumber
   to.

Result: heating one 15 m² room instead of a 120 m² house cuts the fuel
requirement by 70–85%. A whole winter becomes 2–4 m³ of wood instead of 15–20
m³. Nothing else you do comes close to this.

The heated-bed trick: a well-insulated bed with a hot water bottle (or a hot
stone/brick wrapped in cloth) needs no room heat at all to be comfortable.
Heating a body is a hundred times cheaper than heating a room. Layered wool
blankets, a duvet, a hat, and a hot water bottle at the feet will get you
through a 5 °C bedroom indefinitely.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Step two: cut the losses (cheap, do it this autumn)
------------------------------------------------------

Measure                     Cost                    Effect
--------------------------  ----------------------  --------------------------
Draught-proofing — door     €50–100                 10–20% of heat loss. Best
seals, letterbox brush,                             €/kWh in the building.
keyhole covers, chimney
balloon in unused flues,
sealing around pipes and
sockets

Heavy curtains, closed at   €50 or free (blankets)  10–15% through windows
dusk, opened at sunrise on
sunny days

Window insulation film      €10–20/window           Turns single glazing into
(shrink-fit)                                        quasi-double. 20–30% of
                                                    window loss.

Bubble wrap on windows you  €10                     Crude, surprisingly
don't look through                                  effective

Reflective foil behind      €15                     5% for radiators on
radiators                                           external walls

Rugs and underlay on bare   Varies                  Floors are 10–15% of loss
floors                                              in older houses

Loft insulation to 300 mm   €300–800 DIY            20–25% of total heat loss.
                                                    The single best permanent
                                                    investment.

Sealing and insulating the  €100                    5–10%
cellar hatch / floor void

Door snakes, thermal door   €30                     Noticeable in a warm room
curtains

Dressing properly: base     €100                    Lets you hold the room at
layer, wool jumper, hat,                            16 °C instead of 21 °C — a
thick socks, slippers                               30–40% fuel saving


The thermal comfort rule: every 1 °C lower costs about 6–8% less fuel. Going
from 21 °C to 17 °C saves roughly a quarter to a third of your heating fuel,
and with wool and a hot drink it is entirely liveable.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Step three: heat supply
--------------------------


What happens to your existing heating without electricity?

Check this today, because most people are wrong about it:

System                                  Works without mains power?
--------------------------------------  --------------------------------------
Gas boiler + radiators                  [NO] No. Needs power for the pump, fan
                                        and controls. Dead in a blackout.

Gas boiler + gravity-fed system (old,   [!] Partly, if truly gravity
rare)                                   circulated

Heat pump                               [NO] No — and it is a large electrical
                                        load anyway

Pellet stove / pellet boiler            [NO] No. Needs power for the auger and
                                        fan. Also needs pellet deliveries.

Oil boiler                              [NO] No (pump and burner are electric)
District heating                        [NO] No, and entirely out of your
                                        control

Wood stove / log burner                 [YES] Yes
Masonry/tiled stove (kachelofen)        [YES] Yes — and it stores heat for
                                        12–24 h

Open fireplace                          [!] Works, but is 10–20% efficient and
                                        often net negative — it pulls more
                                        warm air up the chimney than it
                                        delivers

Bottled gas heater                      [!] Works, but consumes room oxygen
(catalytic/flueless)                    and makes water vapour and CO —
                                        ventilation essential, never overnight

Paraffin/kerosene heater                [!] Same caveats, plus fumes and fire
                                        risk


If your answer is [NO], you need a non-electric heat source before winter. A
gas boiler can often be kept running with a very small battery/inverter (the
pump and controls may only be 50–150 W → 1.2–3.6 kWh/day) — check yours and
size for it. That is often the cheapest fix for an existing house.


Wood — the backbone for the land track

Energy content: seasoned hardwood ≈ 4 kWh/kg, or roughly 1,800–2,100 kWh per
stacked m³ (beech/oak/hornbeam at the top, spruce/pine around 1,300–1,500
kWh/m³ and burning faster).

How much you need per winter:

Scenario                                               Wood
-----------------------------------------------------  ----------
Warm-room only, one efficient stove, 4–5 months        2 – 4 m³
Whole small house, well insulated                      6 – 10 m³
Whole older house, poorly insulated                    15 – 25 m³
Cooking only, year-round, on a rocket/efficient stove  1 – 2 m³

Seasoning is the thing people get wrong. Freshly cut wood is 45–60% water.
Burning it gives you half the heat, coats your chimney with creosote (chimney
fire), and produces choking smoke.

- Split, stacked, covered on top but open at the sides, off the ground.
- Hardwood: 18–24 months. Softwood: 9–12 months.
- Target < 20% moisture — buy a €15 moisture meter and actually check.
- This means you must start two years before you need it. Wood is the one
   preparation that cannot be bought at AMBER. Buy or cut this year's wood and
   next year's, and keep a rolling two-year stack forever.

Stoves: a modern clean-burn wood stove is 70–80% efficient versus 10–20% for
an open fire. Buy one with a cooktop surface — then the same fuel heats the
room, cooks the food, heats water in a kettle and dries clothes. That multi-
functionality is worth more than a few percent of efficiency.

- Installation must be legal and safe: correct flue, register plate, hearth,
   clearances to combustibles, a swept and lined chimney. Get it signed off.
- Sweep the chimney at least annually, more with soft or damp wood. Buy your
   own rod set (€50) — you will not get a sweep in a crisis.
- A masonry/tiled stove (kachelofen) stores 100+ kWh in its mass and releases
   it for 12–24 hours from one or two firings. If you are building or
   renovating, it is the best cold-climate heat storage that exists.
- Rocket mass heater: a DIY design that burns small wood extremely cleanly and
   stores the heat in a cob/masonry bench. Very fuel-efficient and cheap to
   build, but bulky, non-standard and often not compliant with building
   regulations — check before building.

Sourcing wood without a forest: pallets (untreated/HT only — never MB methyl-
bromide-treated or painted), tree surgeons' arisings (often free if you
collect), storm-fall, council permits for gathering, coppice agreements with a
neighbour, offcuts from a joinery. Build the relationships in GREEN.

Tools: bow saw and a good axe before a chainsaw (they always work). Then
chainsaw + PPE (helmet, ear and eye protection, chaps/trousers, gloves),
files, chain oil, 2 spare chains, and 2-stroke fuel. A log splitter or a
splitting maul and wedges. Chainsaw injuries are the classic homestead
disaster — get training.


Bottled gas — the backbone for the apartment track and the best bridge fuel

- Propane (LPG) stores indefinitely, needs no stabiliser, and delivers about
   13.8 kWh/kg — an 11 kg cylinder ≈ 150 kWh.
- For cooking only, an 11 kg cylinder lasts a family roughly 6–10 weeks. Four
   cylinders ≈ 6–9 months of cooking. This is a very achievable store.
- For heating, gas goes fast: a 4 kW heater burns an 11 kg cylinder in ~37
   hours of running. Gas is a cooking fuel and a short-term heat fuel, not a
   winter heating strategy.
- Store cylinders outside or in a ventilated outbuilding, upright, away from
   drains and cellars (LPG is heavier than air and pools). Many leases and
   building rules limit or forbid cylinders in flats — check, and if
   forbidden, use small butane canisters and an alcohol stove instead.
- Butane (the blue camping canisters) stops vaporising below about 0 °C —
   useless outdoors in winter. Propane works to −40 °C. For cold-weather use,
   buy propane or propane/butane mixes.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Cooking without the grid
---------------------------

Rank your cooking options by fuel independence:

The fuel-efficient cooking doctrine (this doubles or triples your fuel store):

1. Soak beans, chickpeas and grains overnight — cuts cooking time by half.
2. Pressure cook whatever you can.
3. Bring to the boil hard, then transfer to the thermal cooker for the long
    simmer. This is the single biggest fuel saving available.
4. Lids on, always. Uncovered pots waste 2–3× the energy.
5. Match the pot to the burner; a small pot on a big flame wastes most of it.
6. Batch cook — one fire, several meals. Cook tomorrow's food with today's
    fire.
7. Use the residual heat: bread, then a casserole, then dried herbs, then a
    hot water bottle, all from one stove firing.
8. Windshield any outdoor stove — wind can double fuel use.
9. Eat one-pot food. Stews, porridge, soup, dal. Fewer pots, less washing
    water, less fuel.

Baking without an oven: a Dutch oven / cast-iron pot with a lid on a wood
stove or in embers; a stove-top "bread pot"; a solar oven; a hot-box.
Flatbreads, tortillas and pan breads need only a frying pan — learn these,
they are the cheapest bread in fuel terms.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Carbon monoxide: the thing that will actually kill you
---------------------------------------------------------

More people die from improvised indoor heating and cooking in a crisis than
from almost any other cause. CO is colourless, odourless, tasteless, and the
early symptoms — headache, nausea, sleepiness, confusion — are exactly the
symptoms that stop you realising you need to act.

The absolute rules:

- NEVER burn charcoal, a BBQ, a generator, a petrol engine, or a patio heater
   indoors, in a garage, in a cellar, in a tent, in a porch, or in a caravan.
   Not with a window open. Not "just for a bit".
- NEVER use a gas cooker as a room heater.
- NEVER sleep with a flueless combustion appliance running.
- Any flame consumes oxygen and produces CO2, water vapour and some CO.
   Ventilate — a 2–5 cm window gap is a small heat cost and a large safety
   gain.
- Fit CO alarms (EN 50291): one in every sleeping room, one in any room with a
   combustion appliance, one near the generator's nearest wall. €20 each.
   Check them monthly, replace every 5–7 years. This is the cheapest life
   insurance in this entire plan.
- Fit smoke alarms too, and keep a fire extinguisher (ABC powder, 2 kg) and a
   fire blanket in the kitchen and by the stove. Crisis cooking on unfamiliar
   appliances in a crowded warm room is exactly how house fires start, and
   there may be no fire brigade coming.
- If anyone gets a headache, feels sick or sleepy when the heater is on and
   better when outside: get everyone out, ventilate, do not go back in.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Hot water
------------

- On the stove. A kettle or a pot living permanently on the wood stove
   provides washing water all day for free.
- Wood stove back boiler — heats a cylinder as a by-product of room heating.
   Must be correctly installed with a heat leak/dump radiator and pressure
   relief, or it is a bomb. Use a professional.
- Solar shower bag (20 L, €15): black PVC bag on a sunny wall reaches 40 °C in
   a few hours, April–September. The cheapest hot water there is.
- Solar thermal panel / batch heater: a genuinely good investment in a sunny
   climate; a DIY batch heater (a black tank in a glazed box) costs €100.
- Wash with much less: a basin, a flannel and 3 L of hot water gets a person
   properly clean.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. Build sequence
-----------------


[FLAT] Apartment

1. Now (€120): 2-burner butane/propane camping stove + 8 canisters · CO alarm
    · fire blanket · thermal cooker or a DIY haybox · warm bedding and base
    layers for everyone.
2. L1 (€250): + 4 more canisters or a small propane cylinder if permitted ·
    alcohol stove + 5 L of fuel (safest indoor option) · window film · heavy
    curtains · draught excluders · sleeping bags rated to 0 °C.
3. L2 (€400): + a pressure cooker · more fuel (target 3 months of cooking: ~10
    kg of propane or 20 butane canisters) · a room-in-a-room tent for winter
    (a cheap 4-person tent inside the living room genuinely works — it traps
    body heat and turns an unheated flat into a survivable one).
4. Never a generator, charcoal, or a flueless heater overnight. In a flat you
    cannot make CO safe; you can only avoid making it.


[LAND] Land / house

1. Now (€150): CO + smoke alarms · fire extinguisher + blanket · draught-
    proofing · thermal cooker · rocket stove.
2. L1 (€800–2,500): wood stove with cooktop, professionally installed and
    signed off · 2 m³ of seasoned wood · chimney rods · moisture meter · 2 ×
    11 kg propane + regulator and hoses · bow saw, axe, maul.
3. L2 (€1,500–4,000): + loft insulation · 4–6 m³ seasoned wood (this year's
    and next year's) · chainsaw + full PPE + spares · log store built · solar
    shower or back boiler · warm-room prepared and tested.
4. L3: masonry stove or rocket mass heater · two-year rolling wood supply ·
    coppice or woodland access · solar thermal · full-house insulation
    upgrade.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. Heating & fuel checklist
---------------------------

  [ ] I know exactly what happens to my heating in a blackout
  [ ] A non-electric heat source exists for at least one room
  [ ] Warm room chosen, prepared, and slept in for one test night in winter
  [ ] Draught-proofing, curtains and window film done before autumn
  [ ] Loft insulated to 300 mm
  [ ] Wood: this winter's supply seasoned AND next winter's stacked
  [ ] Moisture meter owned; wood measured under 20%
  [ ] Chimney swept this year; own rods in stock
  [ ] Cooking: at least TWO fuel-independent methods available
  [ ] Thermal cooker / haybox owned and used
  [ ] Pressure cooker owned and used
  [ ] 3 months of cooking fuel stored (propane preferred)
  [ ] Fuel stored legally, outdoors, upright, ventilated
  [ ] CO alarms in every sleeping room and near every combustion appliance
  [ ] Smoke alarms tested; extinguisher and fire blanket in place
  [ ] Everyone in the household knows the "never indoors" rule by heart
  [ ] Winter bedding, wool layers, hats and hot water bottles for every person
  [ ] Unheated-zone plumbing can be drained before a hard frost
  [ ] Cold-night drill completed


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[BUILD] Build it: Cooking & heat builds — rocket stoves · haybox/thermal
cooker · solar oven · solar dehydrator · wood store · warm-room tent

Previous: 03 — Food storage · Next: 05 — Sanitation & hygiene


==============================================================================
7. 05 — SANITATION & HYGIENE
==============================================================================


05 — Sanitation & Hygiene
=========================

Historically, in every siege, refugee camp, flood and collapse, more people
die of dysentery, cholera and typhoid than of anything else. This chapter is
not optional and it is not glamorous. It is the one that keeps your family
alive while everyone else's plan is about food.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. The problem, stated plainly
------------------------------

Each person produces, every day:

- ~1.2–1.5 litres of urine (sterile when fresh, smelly and ammoniacal within
   hours)
- ~150–250 g of faeces (containing, if that person is ill, billions of
   infectious organisms)
- Grey water from washing
- Household waste

A family of four produces roughly 6 L of urine and 800 g of faeces per day,
every day, for as long as the crisis lasts. Over three months that is 540 L of
urine and 72 kg of faeces. You must have a plan for it before day one.

And the failure mode is fast: one person with a gastrointestinal infection,
one unwashed pair of hands, one fly walking from the bucket to the bread, and
the whole household is ill. Diarrhoea while isolated, with a saturated health
system, in an unheated house, with limited water, is genuinely life-
threatening — especially for children and the elderly.

The controls, in order of importance:

  1. Keep hands clean          (the single biggest intervention)
  2. Keep faeces away from food, water and flies
  3. Keep clean water clean    (see Ch. 01)
  4. Keep sick people separate (see Ch. 06)
  5. Wash and dry things


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. Handwashing — the highest-value habit in a crisis
----------------------------------------------------

Handwashing with soap cuts diarrhoeal disease by 30–50% and respiratory
infection meaningfully too. It is more effective than any item you can buy.

When: after the toilet, after handling waste or animals, after being outside
or handling deliveries, before preparing food, before eating, before and after
caring for anyone ill, after coughing or sneezing.

How, with almost no water:

- Tippy-tap: a 5 L jug or bottle on a string, pivoted by a foot lever, pouring
   a thin stream. Costs nothing, uses 0.2 L per wash, and — crucially — you
   never touch the container with dirty hands. Build one, indoors or out.
- Tap-fitted jerry can (a 20 L can with a spigot on a shelf) — same effect.
- Wet, lather 20 seconds, scrub between fingers and under nails, rinse into a
   greywater bucket. Air-dry or use a dedicated personal towel.
- Soap matters more than water volume. Even cold, muddy water with soap beats
   clean water without.
- Alcohol hand rub (≥ 60%) is a supplement, not a substitute — it does not
   work well on visibly dirty hands and is poor against norovirus. Have both.

Stock soap generously. It is cheap, light, stores for years (bar soap improves
with age as it hardens), and is an outstanding barter good. Target 1 bar per
person per month, plus 5 L of liquid soap and 5 L of washing-up liquid, per
year. Learn to make soap from lye + rendered fat as an L3 skill.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Toilets without mains water
------------------------------


First: can you still flush?

If water supply fails but the sewer/septic still works, you can flush with any
water — greywater, rainwater, pool water, stream water.

- Pour 4–6 L briskly straight into the bowl (not the cistern) to trigger the
   siphon. Use the cistern only if you have water to spare.
- Practise "if it's yellow, let it mellow" — flush solids only.
- Warning: if you live in a block of flats and the sewer or the building's
   pumping station backs up, flushing pushes sewage into the lowest flats. If
   there is any sign of backup, or if the mains sewer depends on electric
   pumping stations in your area, stop flushing immediately and go to the
   bucket system.


The bucket (sawdust / "humanure") toilet — the household standard

Reliable, cheap, odour-free when done properly, and usable indoors. This is
the answer for both tracks.

Build it:

- Two or more 20 L buckets (one in use, one being emptied/held).
- A toilet seat with a lid on a simple plywood box, or a purpose-made bucket-
   toilet seat (€25).
- A bin of cover material and a scoop.

Use it:

1. Put 3–5 cm of cover material in the bottom of an empty bucket.
2. Use the toilet as normal (urine and faeces together is fine, but see
    separation below).
3. Cover completely after every single use — a generous handful of material
    until nothing is visible. This is the whole trick: a covered deposit does
    not smell, does not attract flies, and composts aerobically.
4. Keep the lid down between uses.
5. When two-thirds full, seal and swap to the next bucket.

Cover material — stock this deliberately, you need a lot of it: sawdust (not
from treated or painted wood) · wood shavings · shredded dry leaves · coconut
coir (compressed bricks — compact to store, ideal for flats) · shredded paper
and cardboard · peat-free compost · chopped straw · dry garden soil · rice
hulls. Plan ~1–2 L of cover material per person per day → roughly 150–250 L
per person per three months. Coir bricks are the apartment answer: one 5 kg
brick expands to ~70 L and stores on a shelf.

Urine separation transforms the system. Urine is ~90% of the volume and almost
all of the smell when mixed. A urine-diverting seat (€40–80), or simply a
separate bottle/jug for urine, means:

- The solids bucket fills 5× more slowly and barely smells.
- Much less cover material is needed.
- The urine is a superb, sterile nitrogen fertiliser (see below). Men and
   children can use a dedicated bottle; a urine-diverting insert handles
   everyone.


Where does it go?

[LAND] Land track — compost it.

- Build two bays of 1 m³ each from pallets, on soil, sited away from any water
   source and downhill from wells.
- Start with a 30 cm base of straw or wood chips to absorb liquid.
- Empty the bucket into the centre of the pile, then cover immediately and
   completely with straw, leaves or chips. A properly covered humanure pile
   has no odour and no flies. Never leave deposited material uncovered.
- Fill one bay for a year, then leave it sealed and undisturbed for a second
   full year while you fill the other. Two years total is the conservative
   standard used by experienced practitioners.
- A thermophilic pile reaching 50–60 °C kills pathogens in days, but piles
   built in cold weather from small daily additions often don't get there —
   which is why the long rest period is the real safety margin, not the
   temperature.
- Use the finished compost on fruit trees, berries, ornamentals and
   grain/fodder crops. Do not use it on root vegetables, salads, or anything
   eaten raw. Be conservative; this is one place where caution costs nothing.
- Wash hands, and keep a dedicated pair of gloves and a boot brush for the
   job.

[LAND] Land track — the alternative: trench latrine. Faster and simpler if you
have space and are not staying forever.

- At least 30 m from any well, spring or watercourse, and downhill from it.
- 30 cm wide, 1 m deep, as long as needed. Throw a scoop of soil in after each
   use; backfill and mound over when 30 cm from the top, and mark it.
- Not for winter (frozen ground) and not for high water tables.

[FLAT] Apartment track — the hard problem. This is the single toughest
apartment challenge and it must be solved in advance. Options, in order of
preference:

1. Sewer still works → flush with greywater. Easy. Most likely scenario.
2. Building agreement: a sealed 120–200 L drum in a bin store, courtyard or
    basement, emptied by the council or a contracted service when collections
    resume; or a communal composting point in a courtyard garden. Agree this
    with neighbours and the building committee in GREEN, not in RED. Several
    flats cooperating can manage this; one flat alone cannot.
3. Double-bagging: heavy-duty bin liners inside the bucket, tied off, stored
    in a sealed outer drum on a balcony until collections resume. Add cover
    material and a scoop of garden lime or wood ash to control odour.
    Realistic for weeks, unpleasant for months, and it depends on collections
    eventually returning.
4. Urine: dilute 1:8 with water and pour down a still-working drain or onto
    balcony plants/trees below (ask first). Undiluted urine burns plants.
5. Never dump waste in a stairwell, lift shaft, rubbish chute or watercourse.
    That is how a building becomes uninhabitable and how an outbreak starts.

Urine as fertiliser (both tracks): fresh urine from a healthy person is
sterile and is an excellent nitrogen-phosphorus-potassium fertiliser. Dilute
1:8 to 1:10 with water and apply to soil around growing plants (not on leaves,
not on anything about to be eaten raw, and stop 3–4 weeks before harvest). One
adult's annual urine contains roughly enough nitrogen for 200–300 m² of
vegetable garden — a genuinely significant free input when fertiliser is
unavailable. Don't use urine from anyone on medication you'd rather not
concentrate in your soil.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Washing and laundry
----------------------

Body washing — see Ch. 01 §6. A basin, a flannel, 3 L of warm water and soap
gets a person genuinely clean. A weekly proper wash plus daily
face/hands/armpits/ groin/feet is the standard that prevents skin infections
and keeps morale up. Do not let washing lapse. Feeling filthy is corrosive to
morale in a way that compounds everything else, and skin infections in a
crisis are dangerous.

Laundry without a machine:

- Two-bucket method: wash bucket with hot soapy water, rinse bucket with cool
   clean water. A plunger-style washer (a "breathing mobile washer", €25) or a
   clean toilet plunger with holes drilled in it makes agitation far less
   exhausting.
- Or a bucket + lid + plunger hole as a manual "washing machine"; or a hand-
   cranked camping washer (€60–100).
- A mop wringer or a hand mangle removes far more water than wringing by hand,
   and drying is the bottleneck in winter.
- Budget ~30 L per load; reuse the rinse water as the next wash water.
- Dry indoors in the warm room with ventilation, or outside. Wet laundry in a
   sealed room causes condensation, mould and respiratory problems — always
   vent.
- Wash underwear, socks and anything soiled frequently; outer layers rarely.
   Air clothes instead of washing them. Wool needs washing far less often than
   cotton and stays warm when damp — build the household's clothing around it.
- Stock: washing soda, bar soap or laundry soap, a scrubbing brush, a
   washboard (€15, genuinely useful), spare pegs and line, and enough clothing
   that laundry can lag by a week.

Dishes: scrape first, two-basin wash + rinse, air-dry (drying cloths spread
bacteria). Add a splash of bleach to the rinse water if anyone is ill.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

-----------------------------
