------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Waste, flies and rodents
---------------------------

Waste reduction comes first — with no collections, whatever you bring in you
keep. Buy in bulk with less packaging, compost all food waste, burn clean
paper and card (land track), and separate what will eventually be collected.

Organic waste → compost heap (land) or a small bokashi bucket / wormery
(apartment — a wormery under a sink handles a family's vegetable waste
silently and produces fertiliser for the balcony).

Non-compostable waste → sealed bins, kept outside the living space, well away
from food and water. Rinse anything food-contaminated before storing it.

Flies are the vector that connects faeces to food. Control them:

- Cover every deposit of waste, always.
- Screens on windows and food covers on the table.
- Fly traps and papers; keep a stock.
- Keep the compost properly covered with a carbon layer.

Rodents eat your food store, chew wiring, and carry leptospirosis, hantavirus
and salmonella. Control them:

- Food in metal or thick plastic bins, never in sacks on the floor.
- Snap traps (stock 12+) and keep the area clear of nesting material.
- Block entry points with steel wool and mortar.
- A cat earns its food.
- Use poison very cautiously if you have pets, children or eat the local
   wildlife.

Medical/infectious waste (masks, tissues, dressings from a sick room) →
double-bagged, sealed, separate, ideally burned (land) or held apart (flat).
See Ch. 06.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Disinfection — what concentration for what
---------------------------------------------

Household bleach (5–6% sodium hypochlorite), diluted fresh (chlorine solutions
lose strength within ~24 hours):

Use                                      Dilution                 Contact time
---------------------------------------  -----------------------  ------------
Drinking water                           2 drops per litre        30 min
Surfaces, general cleaning               1:100 (10 mL per litre)  1 min
Body fluids, vomit, faeces, sick-room    1:10 (100 mL per litre)  10 min
surfaces

Soaking dishes/cloths from a sick        1:100                    10 min
person

Boots/footbath at a threshold            1:50                     —

- Never mix bleach with ammonia, acids, or toilet cleaners — it releases
   chlorine or chloramine gas and it will hurt you badly in a small room.
- Ventilate while using it.
- Alternatives: 70% alcohol (surfaces, fast, flammable), hydrogen peroxide 3%,
   boiling water (dishes, cloths), soap and mechanical scrubbing (always the
   first step — disinfectant on a dirty surface does little).
- Wood ash and lime are the zero-supply-chain options: wood ash is alkaline
   and mildly disinfectant, useful thrown on latrine contents; garden lime
   (calcium hydroxide) controls odour and pathogens in pits and drums.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. Build sequence
-----------------


[FLAT] Apartment

1. Now (€60): 2 × 20 L buckets + a bucket-toilet seat · 2 coir bricks · a
    bidet bottle · 5 kg of soap · 200 bin liners · nitrile gloves · a tippy-
    tap or a jerry can with a spigot.
2. L1 (€150): + urine-diverting insert or dedicated urine bottles · 4 more
    coir bricks · menstrual cups · plunger washer + 2 buckets · fly screens ·
    5 L bleach · washing soda.
3. L2 (€250): + 3 months of cover material (8–10 coir bricks) · a sealed 120 L
    outer drum for the balcony · lime or wood ash · reusable nappies if
    relevant · a written agreement with the building/neighbours about where
    waste goes.


[LAND] Land / house

1. Now (€80): as apartment L0, plus a spade and a designated latrine site.
2. L1 (€200): + two pallet compost bays built · a sawdust/shavings supply (a
    trailer-load from a sawmill is often free) · straw bales · washboard and
    mangle · outdoor handwashing station.
3. L2 (€400): + a dedicated outhouse or a purpose-built composting toilet ·
    greywater line to the orchard · a year of cover material under cover ·
    solar shower · rodent-proof metal bins for the food store.
4. L3: an established two-bay humanure rotation in its second year · soap-
    making from lye and tallow · a wormery/bokashi system for kitchen waste ·
    a reed bed or mulch basin for greywater.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. Sanitation checklist
-----------------------

  [ ] Bucket toilet built and TESTED (actually used for a weekend)
  [ ] 3 months of cover material stored and dry
  [ ] Urine separation arranged
  [ ] The destination for waste is decided, legal, and agreed with neighbours
  [ ] Compost bays built and sited >30 m from any water source, downhill (land)
  [ ] Handwashing station exists at the toilet and at the kitchen
  [ ] Soap stocked: 1 bar/person/month for 12 months
  [ ] Bidet bottle or family cloth as a paper-reduction plan
  [ ] Menstrual cups / reusable pads bought BEFORE they are needed
  [ ] Reusable nappies if there is or may be an infant
  [ ] Laundry method tested by hand-washing a full load
  [ ] Drying plan for winter that does not cause condensation
  [ ] Bleach stocked and in date; dilution table printed and posted
  [ ] Fly screens, fly traps, food covers
  [ ] Rodent-proof food storage; 12 traps
  [ ] Waste separation and storage plan for no collections
  [ ] ORS in the medical kit and everyone knows the recipe
  [ ] Gloves, dedicated boots and a hand-hygiene routine for waste handling


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[BUILD] Build it: Sanitation builds — bucket toilet · urine diverter ·
humanure compost bays · handwash station · plunger washing machine · greywater
mulch basin

Previous: 04 — Heating, cooking & fuel · Next: 06 — Health & pandemic protocol


==============================================================================
8. 06 — HEALTH & PANDEMIC PROTOCOL
==============================================================================


06 — Health & Pandemic Protocol
===============================

This is the chapter that is specifically about a pandemic rather than about
self-sufficiency in general. It has two halves: keeping the disease out, and
staying healthy when the health system cannot help you.

| Not medical advice. Everything here is for planning and preparation. Build
| your medication supply with your doctor and pharmacist, never by skipping
| doses or obtaining prescription drugs outside the system. Get vaccinated.
| Follow official public health guidance — it is written by people with far
| more information than you have about the specific pathogen. If someone is
| seriously ill, seek medical care. A saturated hospital is still better than
| no hospital.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. The biggest medical risk is not the virus
--------------------------------------------

For most households, the greatest danger in a pandemic is losing routine
healthcare: the insulin you can't collect, the blood pressure medicine that
runs out, the appendix that bursts while ambulances are queued, the infected
cut, the dental abscess, the birth with no midwife.

Therefore the priority order is:

  1. Prescription continuity   — start this TODAY, it takes months to build legally
  2. Not catching it           — PPE, isolation, ventilation, decontamination
  3. Treating what you can     — the medical chest and the skills
  4. Knowing when to break isolation and get real help


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. Prescription medicines — the single most important preparation
-----------------------------------------------------------------

For anyone in your household on regular medication, this is the top priority
in this entire plan. More important than food, water or power, because the
timeline is shorter: an insulin-dependent person without insulin has days.

How to build a buffer legally and safely:

- Talk to your GP now, in GREEN, and explain that you want a modest buffer for
   supply disruption. Many will support a 90-day prescription instead of 28
   days, especially post-2020. Ask about an emergency/holiday supply.
- Fill repeats as early as the system allows, every time. A 7-day early refill
   each month builds a full extra month over the course of a year, entirely
   within the rules.
- Never skip or halve doses to build a stockpile. That does more harm than the
   shortage you are insuring against.
- Rotate: use the oldest first so the buffer is always fresh.
- Get a written copy of every prescription — drug name (generic, not brand),
   dose, frequency, and the condition treated. If you ever need care
   elsewhere, from anyone, this piece of paper is priceless.
- Storage: cool, dark, dry. Most medicines degrade fastest in heat — the
   bathroom cabinet is the worst place in the house. Many solid-dose drugs
   remain potent for years past their date (the US SLEP programme found ~88%
   of tested drugs still met specification 5+ years past expiry) — but
   liquids, antibiotics in suspension, insulin, nitroglycerine, EpiPens, eye
   drops and anything refrigerated are genuine exceptions and lose potency or
   become unsafe. Never gamble on a life-critical drug being still good.
- Insulin specifically: unopened vials need 2–8 °C. Plan the cold chain — a
   solar-powered fridge or 12 V cooler on the critical-load circuit (Ch. 02),
   a cool cellar, or a clay-pot/evaporative cooler as a last resort. An in-use
   vial keeps ~28 days at room temperature. This is a Tier 1 electrical load.
- Oxygen concentrators, CPAP, nebulisers, powered wheelchairs: each is a Tier
   1 load. Size the power system for them explicitly, and have a manual
   fallback where one exists.

Also stock: spare glasses (get an extra pair made and keep a copy of your
prescription), contact lens solution, hearing aid batteries, mobility aid
spares, and anything else whose absence disables someone.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. The household medical chest
------------------------------

Build this over several months. Quantities are for a family of four for
approximately 6–12 months.


Life-saving basics

Item                           Qty          Why
-----------------------------  -----------  ----------------------------------
Oral rehydration salts (ORS)   40+ sachets  The most life-saving item on this
                                            list. Diarrhoea and fever kill by
                                            dehydration. Home recipe: 1 L
                                            clean water + 6 tsp sugar + ½ tsp
                                            salt.

Digital thermometer (+ spare)  2            Fever monitoring
Pulse oximeter                 2            Detects the "silent hypoxia" of
                                            respiratory illness. €20.
                                            Genuinely useful for deciding when
                                            someone needs hospital care.

Blood pressure monitor         1            For anyone with cardiovascular
                                            risk

Blood glucose meter + strips   if diabetic  Strips expire — rotate
First aid manual (printed)     1            Paper works in the dark


Pain, fever and everyday medicine

Paracetamol/acetaminophen (adult + children's liquid) · ibuprofen (adult +
children's) · aspirin (also for suspected heart attack — know the protocol) ·
antihistamine, non-drowsy and drowsy · loperamide (use with caution — not for
bloody or feverish diarrhoea; rehydration is the treatment) · oral rehydration
salts · antacids/omeprazole · laxative and stool softener · anti-emetic ·
cough suppressant and expectorant · throat lozenges · decongestant ·
hydrocortisone 1% cream · antifungal cream (athlete's foot, thrush) ·
antiseptic cream · burn gel · oral gel (toothache) · saline nasal spray · eye
wash / saline pods · electrolyte drinks · vitamin C, vitamin D, multivitamin,
zinc.


Wound care

Nitrile gloves (500) · sterile saline (or make it: 9 g salt per litre of
boiled water) · povidone-iodine and/or chlorhexidine · sterile gauze in
several sizes · non-adherent dressings · adhesive dressings, assorted · wound
closure strips (steri-strips) and skin glue · micropore and cloth tape · crepe
and elastic bandages · a trauma dressing and a CAT tourniquet (know how to use
it) · triangular bandages · burn dressings · blister plasters · tweezers,
scissors, a scalpel or sterile blades · a splint (SAM splint) · a thermometer
cover · instant cold packs · safety pins · a proper first aid bag that is
findable in the dark.


Pandemic-specific PPE

Item                             Qty          Notes
-------------------------------  -----------  --------------------------------
FFP2/FFP3 (N95/N99) respirators  100+         The workhorse. Fit matters more
                                              than rating — a leaking FFP3 is
                                              worse than a well-fitted FFP2.
                                              Try several shapes now and find
                                              one that seals on each adult's
                                              face. Beards break the seal.

Surgical masks                   200          For a sick person to wear
                                              (source control), and for lower-
                                              risk tasks

Nitrile gloves                   500
Safety goggles or a face shield  1 per adult  Eyes are a transmission route
Disposable aprons / gowns        50           Or a dedicated washable "care
                                              coat"

Hand sanitiser (≥ 60% alcohol)   3 L
Disinfectant (bleach)            10 L         See the dilution table in Ch. 05
                                              §7

CO2 meter                        1 (€80)      The best cheap proxy for
                                              ventilation. Below 800 ppm =
                                              well ventilated. Above 1,500 ppm
                                              = the air you are breathing has
                                              been in other people's lungs.
                                              Invaluable for deciding when a
                                              room is safe.

HEPA air cleaner or a DIY        1–2          A box fan + 4 MERV-13/F7 filters
Corsi–Rosenthal box                           taped into a cube costs ~€60 and
                                              outperforms many commercial
                                              units. Runs on ~50 W. Genuinely
                                              effective at reducing airborne
                                              virus indoors.



Skills and references (worth more than the kit)

- Do a proper first aid course now. Ideally a 2-day one. Then a refresher.
- Print and keep on paper: Where There Is No Doctor and Where There Is No
   Dentist (both freely available from Hesperian) — they are written precisely
   for "care when there is no professional available".
- Learn: recovery position · CPR · choking · bleeding control and tourniquet
   use · splinting · recognising dehydration, sepsis and hypoxia · fever
   management in children · wound cleaning and closure · when NOT to close a
   wound.


Dental — the badly neglected one

Toothache with no dentist is one of the most miserable and morale-destroying
things that can happen in a long crisis, and a spreading dental infection can
be dangerous.

- Get all outstanding dental work done in GREEN. This is free preparation.
- Stock: temporary filling material (Cavit/zinc oxide, €10), clove oil
   (eugenol, a genuine topical anaesthetic), dental wax, floss, extra
   toothbrushes, bicarbonate of soda, a dental mirror and probe, and
   chlorhexidine mouthwash.
- Brush and floss religiously — in a high-sugar storage diet with no dentist,
   oral hygiene is preventive medicine.


Antibiotics — a realistic note

Do not obtain or self-prescribe antibiotics. Misuse causes resistance, masks
serious illness, and the wrong drug for the wrong infection is useless or
harmful. What you can do: talk to your doctor about whether a standby course
is appropriate for anyone with a specific recurrent condition (some people
with COPD, recurrent UTIs, or who travel are legitimately prescribed rescue
packs); make sure any existing rescue medication (asthma inhalers, steroid
packs, EpiPens) is in date and duplicated; and focus your effort on the
prevention side — wound hygiene, clean water, and handwashing — which is where
a household can genuinely reduce its need for antibiotics.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Keeping the disease out — the household protocol
---------------------------------------------------


The layers

No single measure works. Stack them, and accept that each is imperfect:

  Distance → Ventilation → Filtration → Masks → Hygiene → Quarantine → Isolation


Reduce contacts to the minimum

The most effective control is the number of people whose air you share. At
ORANGE and RED: no visitors indoors, one designated shopper (if any),
deliveries left at the door, outdoor meetings only.


Ventilation is the biggest lever for an airborne pathogen

- Open windows. Cross-ventilate for 5–10 minutes every hour rather than
   leaving a window cracked all day (less heat loss, better air exchange).
- Use the CO2 meter to make it concrete: ventilate until it drops below 800
   ppm. It turns an invisible risk into a number everyone in the household can
   see and act on.
- Run a HEPA cleaner or a Corsi–Rosenthal box in the room the household
   shares, and in any sick room.
- Ventilation fights heating. In winter, ventilate hard and briefly, wear
   layers, and accept the fuel cost — this is a trade you should consciously
   make in favour of air.


The decontamination threshold (set this up at ORANGE)

Create an airlock zone just inside your entrance — a boot tray, a chair,
hooks, a bin, a bucket of 1:100 bleach, and hand sanitiser.

Entering the house:

1. Shoes off in the tray, outside the line.
2. Outer layer off and hung in the airlock (or into a laundry bin).
3. Mask off last, by the straps, into a bin or a dedicated paper bag.
4. Hands washed with soap for 20 seconds before touching anything else.
5. Shower and change if you have been somewhere high-risk.

Incoming goods: the realistic evidence is that surface transmission is a minor
route for respiratory viruses compared with air — so do not exhaust yourself
disinfecting groceries. A proportionate protocol: leave non-urgent deliveries
in a holding area (garage, porch, balcony) for 24 hours, unpack outdoors or in
the airlock, discard outer packaging, and wash your hands. That is the step
that matters. Wipe down only high-touch items if you wish.

People coming in (a returning family member, a helper): if the pathogen's
incubation allows, quarantine in a separate room for the recommended period
with daily symptom and temperature checks, masks when sharing space, and a
separate bathroom or a disinfect-after-use rule.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. If someone in the household gets ill
---------------------------------------


Set up the sick room in advance (do it at ORANGE, not when someone is coughing)

- Choose a room with a window that opens and, ideally, its own bathroom or the
   bathroom nearest to it.
- Put in: a bed, a chair, a bin with a liner, a jug of water and a cup, a
   basin, a thermometer, a pulse oximeter, tissues, ORS, paracetamol, a phone
   or a bell, and an air cleaner if you have one.
- Tape a symptom and observation chart to the door.
- Everything that goes in stays in until it is cleaned.


Care protocol

- One designated carer, ideally the youngest healthy adult at lowest risk, and
   not anyone in a high-risk group. Minimise the number of people exposed.
- Carer wears: FFP2/FFP3 (fitted), eye protection, gloves, apron. On leaving:
   gloves off, apron off, eye protection cleaned, mask off last, hands washed.
- The sick person wears a surgical mask whenever the carer is in the room, if
   they can tolerate it.
- Window open, air cleaner running, door closed.
- Separate crockery, washed hot with a little bleach in the rinse. Separate
   towels.
- Laundry: handle minimally, don't shake it, wash hot (60 °C if possible).
- Bin: double-bagged, tied, held 72 h before joining the household waste.
- Everything the sick person touches stays in the room.


Monitor and record — twice a day, written down

  Time · Temperature · Pulse · Breaths per minute · SpO2 (oximeter) ·
  Fluids in (mL) · Urine out (rough) · Symptoms · Medication given

A written chart lets you see a trend instead of guessing, and it is exactly
what a medical professional will ask for if you do get through to one.


Supportive care that actually helps

Fluids, fluids, fluids (ORS if there is diarrhoea, vomiting or high fever) ·
rest · paracetamol/ibuprofen for fever and discomfort (follow the dosing on
the packet; watch total daily paracetamol carefully — overdose is easy and is
serious) · keeping warm but not overheated · sitting up or prone positioning
if breathing is difficult · food when they want it, calories over nutrition.


[!!] Seek professional help — break isolation if you must — for:

- SpO2 below 92% (or a sustained drop from their baseline), or severe
   breathlessness, or breathing so fast they cannot complete a sentence
- Blue lips or face · chest pain or pressure · coughing blood
- Confusion, unable to be roused, unresponsive
- Signs of severe dehydration: no urine for 8–12 hours, sunken eyes, no tears
   (children), dizziness on standing, skin that stays tented when pinched
- Fever with a non-blanching rash (glass test), a stiff neck, or a severe
   headache with light sensitivity — possible meningitis/sepsis
- Any fever in an infant under 3 months
- Seizures · severe abdominal pain (especially lower right — appendicitis) ·
   uncontrollable bleeding · a suspected fracture or head injury
- Any symptom that frightens you and is getting worse

An overwhelmed hospital is still vastly better than a kitchen table. The plan
is not "never get medical care" — it is "reduce the number of times you need
it, and know exactly when you do."


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Staying healthy for a year indoors
-------------------------------------

The chronic risks of a long confinement are as real as the acute ones.

- Move every day. Muscle and cardiovascular loss is fast and it is a mortality
   risk. Bodyweight exercise, a skipping rope (€5), stairs, garden work, a
   20-minute daily routine that everyone does. Physical work in a garden is
   both food and fitness.
- Get daylight. 20–30 minutes outdoors, even overcast, for circadian rhythm,
   mood and vitamin D. A balcony or a doorstep counts. Supplement vitamin D in
   winter regardless.
- Sleep on a schedule. Confinement destroys sleep timing, and poor sleep
   degrades immunity, judgement and mood.
- Eat enough, and eat vegetables. See Ch. 03 §5 — the nutrition trap is real
   and its symptoms (fatigue, poor healing, low mood, frequent infection) will
   be blamed on stress when the cause is diet.
- Alcohol discipline. Consumption reliably rises under confinement and stress,
   and it damages sleep, immunity, judgement, and relationships. Decide the
   household rule now, while it is an abstract question.
- Protect your eyes, back and hands. Unfamiliar manual work causes injuries;
   gloves, good tools and taking it slowly prevent them. An injury when there
   is no help is a category-changing event.


Mental health — plan for it as seriously as for calories

Months of fear, confinement, boredom, grief and enforced proximity break
people and break families. This is not a soft topic; it is the reason plans
fail.

- Structure is the medicine. A daily routine with fixed times for waking,
   meals, work, exercise, chores and a shared evening is what holds a
   household together. See Ch. 12.
- Everyone gets a job. Purpose and competence are protective. Children
   especially need real responsibilities, not just entertainment.
- Everyone gets privacy, even if it is only an hour and a corner with
   headphones. Enforce it.
- Ration the news. Once or twice a day, from a fixed source, for a fixed time.
   Continuous doom-scrolling is corrosive and changes nothing.
- Stay connected. Scheduled calls, radio check-ins with the neighbours,
   letters. Isolation is the risk factor, not the virus.
- Plan entertainment deliberately: books (paper), board games, cards,
   instruments, craft supplies, seeds, puzzles, projects. A crate of these
   costs €100 and is worth more than another sack of rice.
- Mark time. Birthdays, festivals, a special meal every week. When days are
   identical, time becomes formless and morale collapses. Deliberate
   celebration is a survival technique.
- Watch for: withdrawal, not eating, not sleeping, drinking, irritability
   escalating to violence, hopelessness. Talk about it early and explicitly,
   agree now that raising it is allowed, and know your local crisis line
   numbers — write them on paper.
- Children: honest, age-appropriate information beats overheard fragments.
   Keep schooling going in some form. Keep play sacred.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Build sequence
-----------------

Level     Do this
--------  --------------------------------------------------------------------
Now, €0   Book a first aid course · book overdue dental and medical check-ups
          · order a spare pair of glasses · ask your GP about 90-day
          prescriptions · print the medication list for every household member
          · download and print the Hesperian books

L0, €120  Basic first aid kit · thermometer · paracetamol/ibuprofen · ORS ·
          antihistamine · plasters and dressings · 2 weeks of all
          prescriptions

          gloves · 1-month prescription buffer · first aid manual on paper

          goggles, aprons · CO2 meter · HEPA/Corsi–Rosenthal box · dental kit

------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. Health checklist
-------------------

  [ ] Every household member's medications listed on paper (generic names + doses)
  [ ] Prescription buffer being built legally, with the GP's knowledge
  [ ] Refrigerated medication has a powered cold-chain plan (Tier 1 load)
  [ ] Spare glasses + prescription copy

  [ ] ORS: 40+ sachets, and everyone knows the home recipe
  [ ] Pulse oximeter and thermometer (×2 each)
  [ ] FFP2/FFP3 fit-tested on each adult's actual face
  [ ] CO2 meter and an air cleaner for the shared room
  [ ] Sick room identified, and its kit boxed and ready
  [ ] Decontamination airlock planned (materials on hand, deployed at ORANGE)
  [ ] Symptom/observation chart printed
  [ ] "Seek help now" criteria printed and posted where everyone can see them
  [ ] First aid course completed within the last 3 years
  [ ] Hesperian books printed and on the shelf
  [ ] All outstanding dental work done; dental emergency kit stocked
  [ ] Vaccinations up to date for everyone
  [ ] Daily exercise, daylight and sleep routine agreed
  [ ] Entertainment/morale crate assembled
  [ ] Crisis line numbers written on paper
  [ ] Emergency contact + medical history card for each person in the document pack

[BUILD] Build it: Corsi–Rosenthal air cleaner — a €60 box fan and four filters
that outperforms most commercial purifiers

Previous: 05 — Sanitation & hygiene · Next: 07 — Land & garden


==============================================================================
9. 07 — LAND & GARDEN 🏡
==============================================================================


07 — Land & Garden [LAND]
=========================

Growing food is the only preparation that renews itself. It is also slow,
skill-dependent, and unforgiving of being started late. Plant the first bed
this month, whatever month it is.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. The honest expectations
--------------------------

Read this before you plan anything.

1. Year one will disappoint you. Your soil is poor, your timing is wrong, your
    slugs are numerous, and you don't yet know your site. Expect 25–40% of the
    yield an experienced gardener gets from the same ground. This is normal
    and it is not a reason to stop — it is the reason to start now, while
    failure costs nothing but a season.
2. Vegetables are not calories. A gorgeous bed of lettuce, tomatoes and
    courgettes is worth maybe 200–600 kcal/m²/year. It will not feed you. It
    provides the vitamins, minerals, flavour and morale that make your stored
    staples survivable. Storage feeds you; the garden keeps you healthy and
    sane. Design around that division of labour.
3. The garden is a supplement in year one, a substantial contributor in year
    three, and a foundation in year five. Perennials and soil take that long.
4. Full self-sufficiency needs land most people don't have. See the table
    below. Aim for meaningful contribution, not autarky.
5. Preservation capacity, not growing capacity, is the real limit. Anyone can
    grow 60 kg of tomatoes in August. Having them in February requires jars, a
    canner, a dehydrator and a cellar. Build the preserving side in parallel
    (Ch. 03 §6).


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. How much land do you actually need?
--------------------------------------

Goal                      Area per person            What it looks like
------------------------  -------------------------  -------------------------

Add for animals (Ch. 08): chickens need 10–20 m² of run for a small flock but
far more land than that to feed — a hectare of grain feeds a lot of chickens
and 6 hens eat ~300 kg of grain a year. Pasture for a goat: 1,000–2,000 m².

Plan your actual site:

  Total plot                 ______ m²
    minus house/paths/shed   ______ m²
    minus too shaded (<5 h summer sun) ______ m²
    minus too wet/steep/rocky          ______ m²
  = USABLE GROWING AREA      ______ m²
    of which: annual beds ____   perennial/fruit ____   animals ____   wood/coppice ____


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Turnip / swede  3 – 5           900 – 1,500     4–5 months      Fast, cold-
/ kohlrabi                                                      hardy, doubles
                                                                as fodder

Tomato,         3 – 10          200 – 600       Fresh only;     Low calories,
courgette,                                      preserve        very high
cucumber,                                                       vitamin,
pepper, salad                                                   flavour and
                                                                morale value.
                                                                Grow them —
                                                                just don't
                                                                count them as
                                                                food security.



The allocation rule for a survival garden

  50–60%  calorie crops    (potatoes, squash, maize, roots, beans)
  20–25%  storage/winter   (cabbage, kale, leeks, onions, garlic, parsnips)
  15–20%  fresh/vitamin    (tomatoes, salad, cucumbers, peppers, herbs)
   5%     experiments      (you must keep learning what works on YOUR site)


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Laying out the garden
------------------------


Beds

- 1.2 m wide, any length, with 45–60 cm paths. You can reach the middle from
   either side, so you never step on the soil. Compacted soil is the most
   common cause of poor yields.
- Orient rows north–south where possible so plants don't shade each other.
- Raised beds (15–25 cm of soil above path level, edged or not) warm earlier
   in spring, drain better, and are easier on the back. On dry sites, sunken
   beds conserve water instead.
- No-dig / no-till: cover the ground with cardboard, then 5–10 cm of compost
   on top, and plant into it. It suppresses weeds, feeds soil life, halves the
   labour, and after 2–3 years gives better yields than digging. This is the
   method to use — especially when you have no fuel, no machinery and limited
   calories to spend on labour.


Rotation — 4 beds, 4 years

Rotation prevents the build-up of soil-borne disease and balances nutrient
demand. Move each group one bed along each year:

  Bed 1  LEGUMES      peas, beans           → fix nitrogen, leave it behind
  Bed 2  BRASSICAS    cabbage, kale, swede  → use that nitrogen; lime if needed
  Bed 3  ROOTS/ALLIUM carrot, parsnip, beet, onion, leek → low nitrogen demand
  Bed 4  POTATOES/SOLANACEAE + squash       → heavy feeders, get the compost

Never grow potatoes or tomatoes in the same ground two years running (blight
and eelworm). Onions and brassicas both benefit from a 3–4 year gap.


Protected growing — the highest-value structure you can build

A polytunnel or greenhouse changes your calendar by 6–8 weeks at each end and
makes winter salad possible.

- A 3 × 6 m polytunnel costs €400–900 and is the best value; a glasshouse is
   prettier, more fragile, and more expensive.
- Cold frames (a glazed lid on a box, €40 or free from old windows) do most of
   the same job at 1/20th of the cost, for hardening off, winter salad, early
   sowing and overwintering.
- Fleece and cloches (€30) buy 2–4 weeks at each end of the season and protect
   against late frosts — the cheapest season extension there is.
- Aim for: seedlings in a cold frame from February, tomatoes/peppers/cucumbers
   in the tunnel from May, and winter salad/spinach/claytonia in the tunnel
   November–March.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. The hungry gap — the thing that catches everyone out
-------------------------------------------------------

March, April and May are the hardest months, not winter. Stores are exhausted,
last year's roots are sprouting, and this year's crops are barely seedlings.
Historically this is when people starved.

Design for it explicitly:

Strategy                      What to do
----------------------------  ------------------------------------------------
Overwintering standing crops  Kale, leeks, parsnips, swede, Brussels sprouts,
                              chard, claytonia, corn salad, land cress,
                              sprouting broccoli (purple sprouting broccoli
                              crops in exactly April — plant it)

Long-storing roots            Onions and garlic (into May/June), potatoes (to
                              May if cellared properly), carrots in sand,
                              beetroot

Autumn sowings                Broad beans and garlic sown October–November
                              crop far earlier than spring sowings

The polytunnel                Winter salad, spinach, radish, early carrots
Preserves                     This is what the jars and sauerkraut are for —
                              the gap, not the autumn glut

Sprouts and microgreens       5–14 days to fresh food, indoors, any month. The
                              hungry gap insurance policy (Ch. 09)

Perennials                    Rhubarb (April), asparagus (April–June), sorrel,
                              Welsh onion, wild garlic, nettle tops, dandelion
                              — most of the earliest food in the year is
                              perennial

Stored calories               Keep your grain and bean store as the explicit
                              March–May buffer, and ration to it



------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Seed — the true renewable
----------------------------

Seed is what turns one garden into an infinite number of gardens. Without
saved seed you are running a countdown timer, not a system.


Buy the right kind

- Open-pollinated (OP) or heirloom varieties ONLY for anything you intend to
   save. F1 hybrids do not come true from saved seed — the offspring are a
   variable mess. F1s are often more vigorous and disease-resistant, so keep a
   few, but your strategic seed store must be OP.
- Buy locally-adapted varieties where you can; they are already suited to your
   climate and diseases.
- Store a 3–5 year seed bank. Seed is tiny, cheap and light. A comprehensive
   seed store for a 300 m² garden costs €150–300 and fits in a shoebox. There
   is no better value per gram in this whole plan.
- Buy extra of the things you cannot easily save: F1-only varieties you rely
   on, and biennials.


Seed viability (years, stored cool, dry and dark)

1–2 yrs                   3 yrs                      4–6 yrs
------------------------  -------------------------  -------------------------
Onion, leek, parsnip,     Carrot, pea, bean,         Tomato,
salsify, sweetcorn        pepper, spinach, chives    cabbage/brassicas,
                                                     beetroot, cucumber,
                                                     squash, melon, lettuce,
                                                     radish, turnip


Store seed in paper envelopes inside a sealed glass jar with a silica gel
sachet or a spoonful of dry rice, in the fridge or a cold cellar. Cold + dry
roughly doubles viability. Label with variety and year. Test germination
annually: 10 seeds on a damp paper towel in a bag — count how many sprout in
10–14 days.


Saving seed — start with the easy ones

Easy (self-pollinating, minimal isolation, save from day one): tomato,
French/runner bean, pea, lettuce, pepper (mostly), and any self-fertile herb.
Save from these in your first year. Take seed from your best plants, not your
leftovers — the ones that were healthiest, earliest and tastiest. That is how
you breed a strain adapted to your own garden.

- Tomato: scoop seeds + pulp into a jar of water, ferment 2–3 days until it
   moults, rinse, dry on a plate (not paper).
- Beans and peas: leave the pods on the plant until they rattle and are
   papery.
- Lettuce: let it bolt and flower, collect the fluffy seed heads.

Harder (cross-pollinating — needs isolation distance or hand-pollination):

- Squash/pumpkin: cross freely within the same species. Grow only one variety
   per species, or hand-pollinate and tape the flowers.
- Maize: wind-pollinated, needs 300 m+ separation or different flowering
   times. Save from at least 100–200 plants or the strain degrades from
   inbreeding.
- Cucumber, melon: 400 m+ or hand-pollinate.

Hardest (biennial — they flower in their second year, so you must overwinter
the plant): carrot, beetroot, cabbage and all brassicas, onion, leek, parsnip
(parsnip is actually easy — it self-seeds readily), turnip, swede. Brassicas
also cross-pollinate wildly with each other and need 800 m+ or cages. Plan to
buy these in, or pick just one biennial to master per year.


Also store

Seed potatoes (save your own from healthy, disease-free plants — after a few
years buy in certified stock to reset virus load), garlic cloves, onion sets,
and root cuttings from your perennials.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Fertility without a supply chain
-----------------------------------

The moment fertiliser deliveries stop, your garden lives on what you can close
the loop on. This is the difference between a garden and a mine.

A vegetable garden needs roughly 10–20 g of nitrogen per m² per year, plus
potassium, phosphorus, calcium and trace elements, plus organic matter to hold
water and feed soil life.

Source                    What it gives              How to use it
------------------------  -------------------------  -------------------------
Compost                   Everything, slowly, plus   The foundation. Build two
                          organic matter             or three bays (1 m³
                                                     each): fill one, turn
                                                     into the next, use the
                                                     third. Mix ~2:1 browns
                                                     (straw, leaves, card,
                                                     wood chip) to greens
                                                     (kitchen waste, weeds,
                                                     grass, manure). Keep damp
                                                     and turn it. A hot pile
                                                     finishes in 3 months; a
                                                     cold one in 12. You
                                                     cannot have too much
                                                     compost.

Urine                     Nitrogen, phosphorus,      Dilute 1:8–1:10, water
                          potassium — a genuinely    onto soil around growing
                          balanced fertiliser        plants. One adult's
                                                     output ≈ enough N for
                                                     200–300 m²/year. Free,
                                                     sterile, and the biggest
                                                     single fertility input
                                                     available to most
                                                     households (Ch. 05).

Humanure compost          Everything + organic       Two-year composting, then
                          matter                     fruit trees and non-food
                                                     crops only (Ch. 05)

Animal manure             N, P, K + organic matter   Chicken manure is hot —
                                                     compost it 6+ months or
                                                     it burns plants. Rabbit
                                                     manure is cold and can go
                                                     straight on beds — a real

                                                     manure: compost, and
                                                     beware aminopyralid
                                                     herbicide residues that
                                                     destroy tomatoes and
                                                     beans (test a sample on a
                                                     bean seedling before
                                                     spreading a load).

crops                     weed suppression, erosion  Field beans, vetch,
                          control                    clover, lupin fix 50–200
                                                     kg N/ha. Phacelia,
                                                     mustard and rye add bulk
                                                     and protect soil over
                                                     winter. Chop down before
                                                     they seed and leave on
                                                     the surface or dig in
                                                     shallowly. Never leave
                                                     soil bare over winter.

Comfrey (Bocking 14)      Potassium, especially —    Plant a dozen roots in a
                          perfect for tomatoes,      corner. Cut 3–4 times a
                          potatoes and fruit         year; use as mulch, in
                                                     the compost, or as a
                                                     liquid feed (leaves in a
                                                     barrel of water for 4
                                                     weeks — it smells
                                                     appalling and works
                                                     brilliantly). Sterile
                                                     Bocking 14 doesn't seed
                                                     and take over.

Wood ash                  Potassium + calcium,       From your wood stove. A
                          raises pH                  light dusting only — it
                                                     is strongly alkaline.
                                                     Never on potatoes
                                                     (encourages scab) or on
                                                     acid-loving plants. Keep
                                                     it dry until used.

Nettles                   Nitrogen                   Liquid feed, same method
                                                     as comfrey. Free,
                                                     everywhere.

Biochar                   Nothing itself — a         Charcoal from a smothered
                          permanent habitat for      fire, crushed, charged by
                          microbes and a             soaking in urine or
                          water/nutrient sponge      compost tea before use. A
                                                     genuine long-term soil
                                                     improvement, and a way to
                                                     use woody waste.

Leaf mould                Organic matter, water      Pile autumn leaves in a
                          retention                  wire cage, wait 1–2
                                                     years. Free, effortless.

Lime                      Calcium, raises pH         Buy a few sacks now.
                                                     Brassicas want pH 6.5–7.5
                                                     and clubroot is
                                                     suppressed by lime. It
                                                     does not perish.

Rock dust, bone meal,     Trace elements,            Worth stocking a sack of
seaweed                   phosphorus                 each — they store
                                                     indefinitely and cover
                                                     what the loop can't.


Test your soil pH now (€10 kit). Most vegetables want 6.0–7.0. Getting pH
wrong locks out nutrients no matter how much fertility you add.

Mulch everything. Straw, leaves, grass clippings, cardboard, compost. Mulch
halves your watering, suppresses weeds, feeds the soil and prevents erosion.
It is free labour-saving and the single most useful habit in a low-input
garden.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. Water in the garden
----------------------

- Mulch first — it saves more water than any irrigation system.
- Water deeply and infrequently (once or twice a week, thoroughly), not a
   little every day. Deep water grows deep roots that survive drought.
- Water at the base, in the morning or evening. Overhead watering in sun
   wastes most of it and spreads fungal disease.
- Drip line or soaker hose from a rainwater tank on a stand — gravity gives
   enough pressure for soaker hose at 1 m of head. €60 for a garden's worth.
- Ollas (unglazed clay pots buried to the neck, filled weekly) water roots
   directly with near-zero loss. Ancient, effective, and free if you have old
   pots.
- Swales and shaped beds on a slope slow and sink runoff instead of losing it.
- Prioritise ruthlessly in a drought: water what you cannot replace (fruit
   trees, perennials, seedlings, and the crops you eat in winter). Let salad
   die. Squash and potatoes need water at flowering and tuber-set; mature
   roots and brassicas will survive a lot of neglect.
- Rainwater, greywater and stored water — never drinking water on the garden.
   See Ch. 01.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. Pests and disease without sprays
-----------------------------------

- Physical barriers are 90% of the answer. Insect mesh over brassicas (against
   cabbage white and root fly), fleece over carrots (carrot fly flies low — a
   60 cm barrier works), netting over fruit, collars around brassica stems.
   Buy plenty of mesh and netting now — it lasts years and there is no
   substitute.
- Slugs are the number one garden enemy in a wet Central European spring. Beer
   traps, night-time hand collection (genuinely the most effective method), a
   duck or two (superb slug hunters — Indian Runners), removing hiding places,
   copper tape on pots, coarse wool pellets, and starting plants big in
   modules rather than direct sowing.
- Blight (potato/tomato) arrives in warm wet July weather. Grow resistant
   varieties, space for airflow, avoid overhead watering, earth up potatoes,
   and cut and remove the haulm the moment blight appears — the tubers keep
   growing and stay clean underground.
- Rotation and hygiene: 4-year rotation, remove diseased material (burn it,
   don't compost it), clean tools, don't work among wet plants.
- Encourage predators: a pond (frogs eat slugs), flowers for hoverflies and
   lacewings (poached egg plant, phacelia, calendula, umbellifers), hedges and
   log piles for hedgehogs and beetles, bird boxes. A garden with a healthy
   predator population has far fewer pest explosions.
- Companion planting and interplanting: partly folklore, but strong-smelling
   plants (onions among carrots, marigolds, herbs) genuinely confuse some
   pests, and mixed plantings limit how fast an outbreak spreads.
- Larger pests: fence against deer (1.8 m) and rabbits (buried 30 cm, 1 m
   high); net against birds; trap rodents around the store. Fencing is a
   capital item — buy and install it before you need it.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


10. Perennials — plant them NOW, they take years
------------------------------------------------

This is the most time-sensitive item in the entire plan, because there is no
way to shortcut it. A tree planted today fruits in 3–8 years. A tree planted
at AMBER fruits never.

Plant                             First crop  Notes
--------------------------------  ----------  --------------------------------
Strawberries                      Year 1      Fastest fruit there is. Multiply
                                              free from runners.

Raspberries (summer + autumn      Year 2      Very productive, spreads itself,
varieties)                                    freezes/jams well

Currants (black/red/white),       Year 2–3    Tough, reliable, high in vitamin
gooseberries, jostaberry                      C, propagate free from hardwood
                                              cuttings

Rhubarb                           Year 2      The earliest garden food of the
                                              year; near-immortal

Asparagus                         Year 3      20+ year crop once established
Apples, pears, plums, cherries    Year 3–6    The backbone of a food-secure
(on semi-dwarfing rootstock)                  property. Choose storing
                                              varieties — some apples keep to
                                              March in a cellar. Plant several
                                              varieties for pollination and to
                                              spread the season.

Grapes                            Year 3      Fresh, dried (raisins store for
                                              a year), juice, vinegar, wine

Elderberry, rosehip, sea          Year 2–4    Huge vitamin C, medicinal,
buckthorn                                     thrive on neglect. Sea buckthorn
                                              is one of the richest vitamin C
                                              sources that grows here.

Hazelnuts                         Year 4–6    Fat and protein that stores —
                                              the most valuable nut for this
                                              climate, and it coppices for
                                              firewood

Walnuts                           Year 8–15   Plant anyway. Fat, protein,
                                              stores in the shell for a year,
                                              and superb timber.

Herbs (rosemary, thyme, sage,     Year 1      Flavour, tea, and medicine, from
oregano, mint, bay)                           almost no space

Comfrey, sorrel, Welsh/walking    Year 1–2    Perennial vegetables — they crop
onion, wild garlic, Good King                 in the hungry gap and need
Henry, sea kale                               almost no work

Willow / hazel coppice            Year 3–7    Renewable firewood, poles, bean
                                              sticks, basketry. A coppice on
                                              500 m² gives a sustainable
                                              annual cut forever.


Do this in the next planting season, whatever else is unfinished. Bare-root
trees and bushes in November–March are cheap (€10–25 each); the whole list
above for a garden costs €300–600 and is the highest-compounding investment
here.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


11. Tools for a garden that has to work
---------------------------------------

Hand tools first, and buy quality — these should last 30 years: spade ·
digging fork · rake · hoe (Dutch/push hoe and a draw hoe) · trowel and hand
fork · secateurs (+ a spare) · loppers · pruning saw · garden line ·
wheelbarrow · watering cans (2 × 10 L) · buckets · sharpening stone and a file
· a good knife.

Then: seed trays and modules · pots · propagator or a warm windowsill · labels
and a pencil (ink fades) · fleece, mesh, netting · canes and string · compost
bins · hose and soaker line · a scythe (genuinely practical for grass, green
manures and fodder, and needs no fuel — but needs learning and peening) · a
broadfork · a garden cart · a soil thermometer · a moisture meter · a
notebook.

Spares and consumables: twine, wire, staples, spare mesh, spare fleece,
replacement tool handles, tool oil, glass or polythene for the frames/tunnel
(polytunnel plastic lasts 5–7 years — buy the replacement sheet now).

The garden notebook is a tool. Record what you sowed, when, which variety,
what happened, what the weather did, what you harvested and when. Three years
of notes is worth more than any book, because it is about your site.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


12. Year one — a realistic first-season plan
--------------------------------------------

Don't try to do everything. Do this:

Autumn/winter (start here whenever you're reading this)

1. Mark out four beds of 1.2 m × 5 m (24 m² of growing space). That is enough
    to learn on and not enough to defeat you.
2. Cardboard + 10 cm of compost on top. No digging.
3. Order seed (OP varieties), compost, and bare-root fruit.
4. Plant the fruit trees, bushes, rhubarb and asparagus now — they are on the
    longest clock.
5. Build a compost bay. Start filling it.
6. Plant garlic and broad beans (October–November) for an early crop.

Spring

7. Sow in modules on a windowsill or in a cold frame: brassicas, leeks,
    lettuce, tomatoes, squash, and start the potato chitting.
8. Plant potatoes at the frost-free date (mid-April in most of Central
    Europe); earth up as they grow.
9. Direct sow: carrots, beetroot, peas, beans (after last frost), radish.
10. Mesh over the brassicas and carrots on day one, not after the damage.

Summer

11. Water deeply, mulch heavily, weed weekly (little and often — an hour a
     week beats a day a month).
12. Start saving seed from beans, peas, lettuce and tomatoes.
13. Preserve as you go — don't wait for a glut you can't process.
14. Sow the autumn/winter crops in July–August: kale, chard, leeks, spring
     cabbage, winter salad, purple sprouting broccoli.

Autumn

15. Harvest, cure and store: potatoes (dry in the dark for 2 weeks first),
     onions, squash (cure 10 days in sun), roots into sand.
16. Sow green manure on every empty bed.
17. Write down what worked and what failed. Plan year two from your own data.
18. Add two more beds.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


13. Garden checklist
--------------------

  [ ] Usable growing area measured, sun-mapped and written down
  [ ] Soil pH tested
  [ ] First beds built (no-dig) and planted, whatever the season
  [ ] 4-year rotation plan drawn on paper
  [ ] Calorie crops are 50%+ of the planned area, not just salad
  [ ] Open-pollinated seed bank for 3–5 years bought and stored cold and dry
  [ ] Seed germination test done this year
  [ ] Seed saved from at least beans, peas, tomatoes and lettuce
  [ ] Seed potatoes and garlic for next year set aside
  [ ] Compost bays built and filling
  [ ] Urine diverted and used as fertiliser
  [ ] Green manure sown on every bare bed
  [ ] Comfrey patch planted
  [ ] Lime, rock dust and bone meal stocked
  [ ] Mesh, netting and fleece bought — enough to cover everything
  [ ] Slug strategy in place before spring
  [ ] Blight-resistant potato varieties chosen
  [ ] Fruit trees, bushes, rhubarb and asparagus PLANTED (this is the urgent one)
  [ ] Nut trees planted
  [ ] Perennial vegetables and herbs established
  [ ] Rainwater irrigation from tanks, with mulch everywhere
  [ ] Cold frame or polytunnel up; spare polythene in store
  [ ] Full set of quality hand tools + spares + sharpening kit
  [ ] Root cellar or cool store ready before the first harvest
  [ ] Preservation equipment ready BEFORE the glut (jars, canner, dehydrator)
  [ ] Hungry-gap crops (kale, leeks, parsnips, PSB, overwintered beans) planted
  [ ] Garden notebook started


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[BUILD] Build it: Garden builds — no-dig beds · three-bay compost · insect
mesh cage · cold frame · low tunnel · walk-in polytunnel · seed-starting rack
· liquid feed barrel · leaf mould cage

Previous: 06 — Health & pandemic protocol · Next: 08 — Animals & protein


==============================================================================
10. 08 — ANIMALS & PROTEIN 🏡
==============================================================================


08 — Animals & Protein [LAND]
=============================

Animals are the only part of a homestead that can turn things you cannot eat —
grass, weeds, kitchen scraps, insects, garden waste — into things you can.
That is their entire strategic purpose. Understand it, or you will build a
system that consumes more food than it produces.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. The feed problem — read this before buying a single chicken
--------------------------------------------------------------

Almost every plan for keeping animals in a crisis fails on the same
arithmetic.

Six laying hens:

  Feed:   6 hens × 130 g/day × 365 days   = 285 kg of grain per year
          285 kg × ~3,400 kcal/kg          ≈ 970,000 kcal IN
  
  Eggs:   ~1,200 eggs/year × 70 kcal       ≈   84,000 kcal OUT
          plus spent hens and cockerels    ≈   30,000 kcal
                                             ------------
          Return on grain fed              ≈ 12%

If you feed your chickens grain you could have eaten, you destroy about 88% of
those calories. Six hens eat roughly what one adult eats. That is not a food
strategy — that is a luxury paid for in your own rations.

Animals only make sense when they eat what you cannot:

Feed source               What it is                 Who eats it
------------------------  -------------------------  -------------------------
Kitchen and garden waste  Peelings, trimmings,       Chickens, pigs, rabbits
                          spoiled produce, whey      (greens only)

Forage and pasture        Grass, weeds, insects,     Chickens (free-range),
                          worms, seeds               geese, goats, rabbits

Crop residues             Bean haulm, brassica       Rabbits, goats, chickens
                          leaves, carrot tops, beet
                          leaves

Fodder crops grown        Mangel-wurzel, fodder      All of them — this is the
deliberately              beet, turnip, kale,        key link, and almost
                          pumpkin, comfrey,          nobody plans it
                          lucerne, clover

Hay                       Cut and dried              Rabbits, goats
                          grass/clover

Insects                   Black soldier fly larvae   Chickens, quail, fish —
                          or mealworms raised on     40% protein, essentially
                          food waste                 free

Gleanings                 Windfall fruit, spilled    All
                          grain, spoiled stores,
                          mill sweepings

Milk by-products          Whey, skimmed milk,        Pigs, chickens
                          buttermilk


The rules that make livestock work:

1. Store feed for the animals as deliberately as for yourself. If you keep
    chickens, store 6–12 months of feed (300–600 kg for six hens). Otherwise
    you will be eating your flock in month four.
2. Grow fodder. Mangel-wurzel yields 6–10 kg/m² and stores all winter in a
    clamp. 100 m² of mangels and fodder beet is a winter's feed for a flock
    and a couple of rabbits. Pumpkins store on a shelf and chickens love them.
    This single practice is what separates a renewable system from a
    countdown.
3. Size the flock to the forage, not to your ambition. In a real shortage,
    cull down to a breeding core. Six hens and a rooster can rebuild a flock
    of thirty in one season; thirty starving hens produce nothing.
4. Maximise free-ranging. A hen with genuine range for insects and greens can
    get 30–60% of her diet herself in summer, and the eggs are better.
5. Composting and manure are part of the return. Chicken manure fertilises the
    garden that feeds you. Count it.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

------------------------
