------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Chickens — the household default
-----------------------------------


Breed choice (this decides whether your flock renews)

- Do NOT buy commercial hybrid layers (ISA Brown, Lohmann, etc.) as your only
   stock. They lay superbly for two years and then collapse, and they have had
   broodiness bred out of them — they will never hatch a chick. A flock of
   hybrids is a consumable, not a system.
- Buy dual-purpose heritage breeds that go broody: Orpington, Sussex,
   Wyandotte, Marans, Australorp, Barnevelder, Plymouth Rock, or a good local
   landrace. Fewer eggs (180–220/yr), better meat on the cockerels, and — the
   critical part — a hen that will sit on eggs and raise chicks for you, for
   free, without electricity.
- A broody hen is a solar-powered incubator that also raises the chicks. This
   is the single most important livestock decision in this chapter.
- Keep a rooster if the law and your neighbours allow. Without one, there are
   no fertile eggs and no next generation. If a rooster is impossible, keep an
   incubator + a source of fertile eggs, and accept the power dependency.


Numbers

- 4–6 hens per 4-person household for eggs. 6 hens ≈ 3–5 eggs/day in season.
- Add 2–4 more if you want meat as well as eggs.
- Winter laying drops to near zero in December–January without supplementary
   light. 14 hours of daylight keeps them laying — a 5 W LED on a timer in the
   coop (a trivial electrical load) restores winter production. Whether to do
   this is a real trade-off: it shortens the hen's productive life, but it
   puts protein on the table in the hardest months.


Housing

- Coop: 0.4 m²/bird minimum, dry, draught-free but ventilated at the top
   (damp, not cold, is what kills chickens), with perches (20 cm/bird) and one
   nest box per 4 hens.
- Run: 2 m²/bird absolute minimum, 10 m²+ if you want real forage value. A
   bare, overgrazed run is a mud-and-parasite problem; rotating two runs, or a
   movable tractor on grass, keeps it healthy.
- Predator-proof it properly. Fox, marten, rat, hawk, dog. Weld mesh (not
   chicken wire — a fox goes through chicken wire), buried or skirted 30 cm, a
   secure roof, and a door that closes every single night. Losing the flock to
   a fox in month two is the classic, avoidable disaster. An automatic door is
   fine, but keep a manual latch.
- Deep litter (wood shavings/straw built up over months) composts in place,
   keeps the coop warm, and produces excellent garden compost. Clean out twice
   a year.


Feeding

- Layer feed: 120–150 g/bird/day. Store 6–12 months (see §1).
- Grit (insoluble, for the gizzard) and oyster shell/crushed eggshell
   (calcium, for shells) — both essential, both store indefinitely, both
   cheap. Stock several years' worth; a hen without calcium lays soft-shelled
   eggs and then stops.
- Supplement with: kitchen scraps, garden waste, comfrey, cut grass, windfall
   fruit, spent brewing grain, mangels and pumpkins in winter, and insect
   larvae.
- Fodder sprouting (barley sprouted in trays for 7 days, 1 kg of grain → 6–8
   kg of green fodder) — be clear about what this does: it does not create
   calories (dry matter actually falls slightly), but it converts stored dry
   grain into fresh green feed with vitamins in winter, which is exactly what
   a confined flock lacks. Useful, not magic.
- Black soldier fly larvae: a simple bucket bioreactor turns kitchen waste
   into 40%-protein larvae that self-harvest by crawling up a ramp. One of the
   best closed-loop tricks available.
- Water: 0.25–0.5 L/bird/day, and it must not freeze. A frozen drinker is the
   most common winter chicken killer. Keep a spare and rotate it from the
   house.


The renewal cycle — how a flock becomes permanent

  March–June   Broody hen sits on 8–12 fertile eggs → 21 days → chicks
               (or set eggs under her from your best layers)
  Weeks 0–8    Hen raises chicks; ~50% will be cockerels
  Weeks 16–20  Cockerels reach 1.5–2.5 kg → slaughter all but one or two
  Month 5–6    Pullets start laying
  Autumn       Cull the oldest third of the hens (years 3+, laying declines) — into
               the pot, the stockpot, or the canner
  Result       Flock size stable, age profile young, genetics refreshed,
               meat on the table twice a year, and NO purchases required

Replace about one third of your layers each year. Hens lay best in years 1–2,
decline from year 3, and are still perfectly good stewing birds (old hens make
the best stock there is — long, slow cooking, or pressure-canned).


Health

Common problems: red mite (check perches weekly — treat with diatomaceous
earth and a thorough clean), worms, scaly leg mite, coccidiosis in chicks,
respiratory infection from damp housing. Keep the coop dry and ventilated,
rotate the ground, quarantine any new bird for 3 weeks, and cull sick birds
early rather than letting disease run through the flock. Stock diatomaceous
earth, poultry tonic and a sharp knife.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Rabbits — the most underrated survival animal
------------------------------------------------

Rabbits deserve far more attention than they get in most plans.

Why: silent (no rooster problem), small footprint, breed prolifically, eat
grass, weeds, hay and garden waste rather than grain, and reach eating size in
10–12 weeks. A trio of does and one buck occupies about 4 m² and produces
80–120 kg of meat a year.

  Gestation 31 days · 6–8 kits per litter · 4–5 litters/doe/year
  3 does × 4 litters × 7 kits × 1.25 kg meat each ≈ 105 kg meat/year

- Feed: hay (the staple — they need it constantly for gut health), fresh
   greens, garden waste, carrots and roots, comfrey, dandelion, plantain,
   bramble leaves. Pellets are convenient but not essential — which is exactly
   the point. Introduce fresh greens gradually; a sudden change causes fatal
   gut problems.
- Housing: hutches off the ground, dry, shaded in summer (rabbits tolerate
   cold far better than heat — above 28 °C is dangerous), with wire floors
   over a dropping tray, or colony housing on deep litter. Does need a nest
   box before kindling.
- Manure is "cold" — it can go straight onto beds with no composting. It is
   excellent, and a genuine convenience advantage over poultry.
- Breeding: take the doe to the buck (never the reverse — she'll defend her
   territory). Keep records: mating date, kindling date, litter size, weaning.
   Rest each doe properly; don't breed her to death.
- [!] Rabbit is extremely lean (~1,300 kcal/kg, almost no fat). A diet of
   rabbit alone causes protein poisoning ("rabbit starvation") — fatal within
   weeks. Always eat rabbit with fat and carbohydrate: lard, oil, potatoes,
   grain. This is a real historical cause of death and it must be designed
   around.
- Slaughter is quick and requires no equipment beyond a sharp knife and a hook
   — the easiest livestock for a beginner to process humanely. Learn it
   properly from someone experienced before you need to.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Quail — the small-space option
---------------------------------

- Coturnix (Japanese) quail lay at 6–8 weeks old — the fastest protein of any
   livestock — and produce 250–300 eggs a year each.
- 5 quail eggs ≈ 1 chicken egg. A dozen birds gives a family a real egg
   supply.
- 0.1 m²/bird: 10 birds live comfortably in 1 m², indoors or on a balcony.
- Quiet (the male's call is soft and far less carrying than a cockerel's).
- [!] They do not go broody. Domesticated coturnix have lost the instinct — so
   renewal requires an incubator (a small one is €60 and about 40 W for 17
   days, well within a modest solar system, but it is a dependency).
- Need higher protein feed than chickens (game-bird crumb, ~20–24% protein);
   insects and sprouted seed help.
- Meat birds at 8–10 weeks, ~200 g dressed. Small but fast.
- The realistic apartment livestock. See Ch. 09.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Larger animals — only with land and commitment
-------------------------------------------------

Goats (dairy): 2 does give 2–4 L of milk a day for 250–300 days a year — milk,
cheese, butter, yoghurt, and whey for the pigs and chickens. But: they need
real browse or a lot of hay (1–2 kg/day each in winter), fencing that actually
holds them (they are escape artists), annual breeding to keep milking, and
twice-daily milking every single day without exception. That last point
defeats most people. Sheep are easier but give less milk; a house cow gives
far more milk and eats far more.

Pigs: the best calorie and fat converter there is, and fat is the hardest
thing to produce at home. A pig eats 2–3 kg/day and reaches 100 kg live in 6–8
months, yielding ~70 kg of carcass including 15 kg of lard — which solves the
cooking-fat problem that nothing else on a homestead solves. They will eat
almost anything: whey, spoiled produce, windfalls, acorns, waste. Two pigs
raised over a summer on surplus and waste, slaughtered in autumn, then cured
and canned, is the classic peasant economy for good reason. Check the law:
feeding household/catering food waste to pigs is illegal in the EU and UK
(disease control), so plan feed you can legally use.

Ducks: better foragers than chickens, lay well, eat slugs voraciously, are
hardier in wet weather, and don't destroy a garden the way chickens do. They
need water to dip their heads in (not necessarily a pond) and they make mud.
Muscovies are quiet, broody and excellent mothers. Seriously consider ducks
over chickens if your site is wet.

Geese: live almost entirely on grass, guard the yard, and provide a large
carcass and a lot of fat — but they are loud and aggressive, which is either a
security feature or a neighbour problem.

Bees: 15–30 kg of honey per hive per year, plus wax for candles and salves,
plus pollination that lifts fruit yields across the whole plot. Low daily
effort once established, but real skill is needed and there is a €300–600
setup cost (hive, suit, smoker, tools, extractor — or share an extractor with
your group). Honey never spoils. The best effort-to-value ratio on a homestead
— start learning from a local association now, because beekeeping is not a
thing you can learn from a book in a crisis.

Fish / aquaponics: high complexity and it fails hard. Fish need constant
aeration (a pump = continuous power, and a failure kills everything in hours),
stable temperature, and feed that is usually bought in. A low-tech farm pond
stocked with carp or tench, fed on kitchen waste and duckweed, is a far more
robust proposition than an aquaponics rig — much lower yield per m², but it
survives a blackout.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Slaughter, butchery and preservation
---------------------------------------

This is a skill, and it must be learned before you need it. Find a local
smallholder, a hunting club, or a course. Doing it badly is cruel, unsafe and
wasteful; doing it well is quick, calm and respectful.

- Learn the humane methods for your species and follow the legal requirements
   in your country (most permit home slaughter of your own poultry and rabbits
   for your own household; larger animals usually require a licensed abattoir
   or a licensed slaughterman — check).
- Equipment: sharp knives (and a steel and a whetstone — a blunt knife is the
   main cause of both cruelty and injury), a killing cone for poultry, a
   gambrel or hooks, a scalding pot (poultry plucking: 60–65 °C for 30–60 s),
   a plucking table, buckets, and a clean cool space.
- Use everything: meat, offal (liver and heart are the most nutrient-dense
   food available to you — do not waste them), fat (render it: lard and tallow
   are precious), bones (long-simmered stock, then to the compost or the
   dogs), blood (black pudding), skin, feathers (pillows and insulation), and
   the intestines if you are making sausage.
- Preserve immediately (Ch. 03 §6): pressure canning is the best power-
   independent option for meat and stock — jars of chicken and stock on a
   shelf need no freezer and no electricity. Then salting, curing, smoking,
   drying, and confit.
- Timing: the traditional autumn slaughter exists because the animal is fat
   from the summer, the weather is cold enough to work safely, and you are not
   feeding it through the winter. Keep breeding stock; eat the rest.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. Legal and neighbourly
------------------------

- Check municipal rules on poultry and livestock in residential areas —
   roosters are the usual sticking point and are banned or restricted in many
   urban areas.
- Registration requirements (many countries require a holding number for
   livestock, and movement/medicine records).
- Slaughter law, waste and carcass disposal law, manure storage rules.
- Talk to your neighbours before, not after. Noise, smell and flies are
   manageable with good husbandry, and a neighbour who gets a dozen eggs a
   week is an ally rather than a complainant. This is also the beginning of
   your mutual-aid group (Ch. 10).


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. Build sequence
-----------------

Level           Land track
--------------  --------------------------------------------------------------
Now, €0         Find a local keeper and visit · join a poultry/beekeeping
                association · read one good book per species · check your
                local rules

L1, €400–700    Coop + secure run built · 4–6 heritage hens · feeders,
                drinkers, grit, oyster shell · 3 months of feed

L2, €800–1,500  + a rooster · 6–12 months of feed stored · fodder crops
                (mangels, pumpkins, kale) planted · rabbit trio + hutches ·
                black soldier fly bucket · first broody hatch completed

L3, €2,000+     + bees · ducks or geese · pigs raised over a summer · goats if
                you have pasture · pressure canner and full butchery kit · a
                second breeding line, or a chick/kit exchange agreement with
                your group (genetics matter — a closed flock degrades)



------------------------------------------------------------------------------


10. Animals checklist
---------------------

  [ ] I have calculated the annual feed requirement in kg — and where it comes from
  [ ] 6–12 months of feed stored, plus grit and oyster shell for years
  [ ] Fodder crops (mangel, fodder beet, pumpkin, kale, comfrey) planted for winter
  [ ] Breeds chosen that go broody / breed without an incubator
  [ ] A rooster (or a fertile-egg plan) — the flock can actually renew
  [ ] Housing is predator-proof with weld mesh, buried skirt, and a nightly door
  [ ] Ventilation without draughts; dry bedding; deep litter started
  [ ] Water that will not freeze in January, plus a spare drinker
  [ ] Free-range or rotational area to cut the feed bill
  [ ] Black soldier fly or mealworm bucket running
  [ ] Rabbits considered seriously (quiet, forage-fed, high output)
  [ ] Fat source planned — rabbit alone is a trap
  [ ] Slaughter and butchery learned from a real person, with sharp tools ready
  [ ] Preservation ready before the first slaughter (canner, jars, salt, smoker)
  [ ] Local law checked: roosters, registration, slaughter, waste
  [ ] Neighbours spoken to; eggs shared
  [ ] Cull-down plan written for a genuine feed shortage (breeding core defined)


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[BUILD] Build it: Animal builds — the full chicken coop plan · predator-proof
run · chicken tractor · no-waste feeder & drinkers · rabbit hutch · quail cage
· black soldier fly bucket · fodder rack · rodent-proof feed store

Previous: 07 — Land & garden · Next: 09 — Apartment playbook


==============================================================================
11. 09 — THE APARTMENT PLAYBOOK 🏢
==============================================================================


09 — The Apartment Playbook [FLAT]
==================================

Everything in chapters 01–06 applies to you. This chapter covers what is
different, what is harder, and — the part that surprises people — what a flat
can actually produce.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. The honest limits, stated up front
-------------------------------------

You cannot feed yourself from a flat. Not from a balcony, not with grow
lights, not with a vertical garden. A 6 m² balcony in full sun, worked
expertly, might produce 15–30 kg of vegetables a season — perhaps 3–5 days'
worth of calories over a whole year. Anyone who tells you otherwise is selling
something.

So the apartment strategy is different in kind, not just in scale:

  [LAND] LAND:      store food  +  PRODUCE food        → aims at self-sufficiency
  [FLAT] APARTMENT: store food  +  PRODUCE NUTRITION   → aims at endurance

Your stored calories are the plan. What you grow indoors is vitamin C, fresh
greens, live enzymes, variety, and morale — the things that stored grain lacks
and that stop a 9-month confinement turning into scurvy, depression and
illness.

That is not a consolation prize. It is the exact gap that kills people on
storage diets, and it is entirely solvable in a flat, in any season, in a few
square metres, with no soil and almost no power.

The second apartment truth: your building is your land. A block of forty flats
has a roof, a courtyard, a basement, a stairwell, and — most importantly —
forty households of skills, tools and labour. The single highest-value thing
an apartment dweller can do is organise the building (§10).


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. What a flat CAN produce — ranked by value
--------------------------------------------

------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Sprouting — the apartment keystone
-------------------------------------

Learn this technique this week. It is the most important thing in this
chapter.

Sprouting takes stored dry seed — which you already have, which keeps for
years, which weighs nothing — and converts it in 3–5 days into fresh food with
vitamin C that the dry seed did not have (vitamin C is synthesised during
germination), plus greatly increased B vitamins, enzymes and digestibility. It
needs no soil, no light, no power, no garden, and no season.

| Be clear about what it does: sprouting does not create calories — dry matter
| actually falls slightly. It converts stored calories into fresh nutrition.
| That is exactly the gap in a long-term storage diet, and nothing else fills
| it so cheaply.


Equipment (€20 total)

- 3–4 wide-mouth 1 L glass jars
- Mesh lids (or muslin and a rubber band)
- A rack or bowl to stand them at 45° to drain
- That's it.


Method

1. Rinse 2–4 tablespoons (30–60 g) of seed, put it in the jar.
2. Soak 6–12 hours in plenty of cool water.
3. Drain thoroughly and invert the jar at 45° so it keeps draining.
4. Rinse and drain twice a day (three times in hot weather). Drainage is
    everything — standing water is what causes spoilage.
5. Harvest at 2–5 days, when the shoot is 1–3× the seed length.
6. Rinse, drain well, refrigerate or keep somewhere cool; eat within 3–5 days.
7. Start a new jar every day. Four jars on rotation = a continuous daily
    harvest, forever, from a bucket of seed.


What to sprout, and expected yields (by weight)

Seed                 Days                 Yield multiple  Notes
-------------------  -------------------  --------------  --------------------
Mung bean            3–5                  4–6×            The workhorse.
                                                          Bulky, mild,
                                                          filling, cheap.

Lentil (whole        2–4                  3–4×            Fast, reliable,
brown/green)                                              nutty. Ordinary
                                                          supermarket lentils
                                                          work.

Chickpea             3–4                  3×              Hearty, good in
                                                          salads and hummus

Alfalfa              4–6                  8–10×           Highest yield by
                                                          weight, very high
                                                          vitamin

Radish / mustard /   3–5                  5–6×            Peppery, high
cress                                                     vitamin C, fast

Broccoli             3–5                  5–6×            High in sulforaphane
Fenugreek            3–5                  4–5×            Medicinal,
                                                          distinctive

Wheat / barley /     2–3                  2–3×            Sprouted grain for
oat groats                                                bread, or grow on
                                                          for wheatgrass

Sunflower (in        2 days then grow on  —               Best as microgreens
shell)               as shoots

[!] Never sprout     —                    —               Kidney/red beans
                                                          (toxic raw — must be
                                                          boiled), tomato,
                                                          potato, aubergine,
                                                          pepper or rhubarb
                                                          seed
                                                          (nightshade/oxalate
                                                          toxicity)



Food safety (take this seriously)

Sprouts are grown warm and damp — which is also ideal for Salmonella and E.
coli, and sprouts have caused real outbreaks.

- Buy seed intended for sprouting where you can, and keep it dry and sealed.
- Rinse thoroughly and drain completely, twice a day, every day. Most failures
   are drainage failures.
- Keep jars at room temperature, not hot. Discard anything that smells sour,
   slimy or musty — trust your nose absolutely.
- Clean jars and lids between batches (boiling water).
- If anyone in the household is pregnant, elderly, very young or
   immunocompromised, cook the sprouts (stir-fried mung sprouts are excellent)
   rather than eating them raw.


How much seed to store

5 kg of mixed sprouting seed per person covers a year of daily fresh food and
costs about €25–40. It stores 4+ years in sealed jars. Nothing else in this
entire plan delivers as much nutritional security per euro or per litre of
storage space. Buy it this week.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Microgreens — fresh greens in 10 days
----------------------------------------

Seedlings harvested at the first true-leaf stage. More substantial than
sprouts, genuinely delicious, and about as fast as food gets.

- Tray: any shallow container, 20 × 30 cm, with drainage. Stack of 4–6 trays
   on a shelf = a continuous supply.
- Medium: 2–3 cm of compost, coir, or even a folded damp paper towel or a hemp
   mat (no soil needed for most).
- Seed: sown thickly — 10–15 g per tray. Cover for the first 2–3 days
   (darkness encourages an even, leggy start), then uncover and give light.
- Light: a bright windowsill works; a €20–30 LED strip at 20–40 W works better
   and works in winter, running 12–16 h/day (≈ 300–600 Wh/day — plan for it in
   your power budget, or use the windowsill and accept slower growth).
- Harvest at 7–14 days with scissors, just above the medium. 150–250 g per
   tray.
- Best for volume and substance: pea shoots and sunflower shoots — these are
   the most filling and the closest to a real vegetable. Then radish,
   broccoli, mustard, kale, beet, coriander, basil.
- Reuse the compost by adding it to a wormery or a balcony pot and refreshing
   it.
- Pea shoots deserve special mention: ordinary dried marrowfat/whole peas from
   the supermarket, soaked overnight and sown thickly, give a big tray of
   sweet shoots in 10 days, and will regrow a second cut. Cheapest fresh green
   there is.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Mushrooms — protein and texture from waste
---------------------------------------------

Oyster mushrooms (Pleurotus ostreatus) are the easiest and the most useful.

- Substrate: used coffee grounds, shredded cardboard, straw, sawdust, or
   paper. Pasteurise straw by soaking in hot (65–75 °C) water for an hour, or
   use fresh coffee grounds within 24 hours (already effectively pasteurised).
- Spawn: buy grain spawn (€10–20/kg). Mix ~5–10% spawn by weight into the
   substrate, pack into a bucket or bag with small holes.
- Colonise in a dark, warm-ish cupboard for 2–4 weeks (it goes white).
- Fruit: move to light and humidity (mist twice a day, or a bag with holes).
   Mushrooms appear over 5–10 days.
- Yield: 1 kg of substrate gives 200–400 g of mushrooms, usually over 2–3
   flushes.
- Spent substrate goes to the wormery or the balcony pots.
- You can propagate your own spawn from a piece of a mushroom stem on damp
   cardboard — this is what makes it a cycle rather than a purchase.
- [!] Only grow species you bought as spawn from a reputable supplier. Never
   eat a wild or unknown mushroom, and never eat anything growing on your
   substrate that doesn't look like what you inoculated. Contaminant moulds
   (green, black, pink) mean discard the batch outdoors.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Fermentation — the zero-power vitamin store
----------------------------------------------

This is how Central and Eastern Europe survived winters for centuries, and it
needs nothing but vegetables, salt, a jar and a cupboard.

Sauerkraut, the complete method:

1. Shred 5 kg of cabbage.
2. Add 2% salt by weight (100 g of salt for 5 kg of cabbage).
3. Massage/pound for 10 minutes until it releases its own brine.
4. Pack tightly into a crock or jars; everything must stay below the brine
    (use a weight, a cabbage leaf, or a water-filled bag).
5. Ferment at 18–20 °C for 1–3 weeks, burping jars or using an airlock.
6. Move to somewhere cold (cellar, unheated room, fridge). Keeps 6–12 months.

Why it matters: sauerkraut retains most of the cabbage's vitamin C and becomes
more digestible. Historically it is the anti-scurvy food of this region. A 10
L crock is cheap, lasts forever, and is entirely power-independent.

Also ferment: cucumbers, carrots, beetroot, turnip, garlic, peppers, green
tomatoes, and hot sauce. And for the cupboard: sourdough starter (bread from
flour and water alone, no yeast purchase), vinegar (from wine, cider or scraps
— and vinegar is a preservative in its own right), and yoghurt/kefir if you
have milk (kefir grains multiply themselves indefinitely — a true renewable).

Stock: a 5–10 L fermenting crock or a dozen 1.5 L jars with airlocks, several
kg of non-iodised salt, and a weight for each vessel.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Balcony and window growing
-----------------------------

Accept the constraint and grow high-value, not high-calorie.

Crop                 Container       Yield per season     Worth it?
-------------------  --------------  -------------------  --------------------
Cherry tomatoes      20–30 L pot     2–4 kg per plant     [YES] Best value on
                                                          a sunny balcony

Chillies & peppers   10 L pot        0.5–2 kg             [YES] Dry them; they
                                                          keep forever and
                                                          transform dull food

Cut-and-come-again   Window box      Weekly cuts for      [YES]
salad                                months

Bush beans           15 L pot        0.3–0.5 kg           [YES] And they fix
                                                          nitrogen

Radish               Window box      4 week cycles        [YES] Fast morale
Potatoes             40 L sack       1–2 kg               [YES] The only real
                                                          calories available

Herbs                Small pots      Continuous           [YES][YES] Best
                                                          value per cm²

Strawberries         Hanging basket  0.5 kg               [YES] Perennial,
                                                          propagates itself

Courgette            30 L pot        2–5 kg               [!] Takes a lot of
                                                          space

Dwarf/patio fruit    40 L pot        2–5 kg from year 3   [YES] If you'll be
tree                                                      there


Practical points

- Sun is the limit. South/southwest facing, 6+ hours. A north-facing balcony
   will do salad, herbs and mushrooms and not much else — don't fight it, grow
   indoors instead.
- Wind dries pots brutally on a high balcony. Screen with mesh; use bigger
   pots (they dry more slowly); mulch the surface.
- Weight: wet compost is ~700 kg/m³. A row of large pots is a real structural
   load — keep them over the edge beams, near the wall, and check your
   building's rules. Never overload a balcony.
- Self-watering containers (a reservoir below the soil) halve your watering
   and survive a missed day. Easy to make from stacked boxes.
- Reuse compost by mixing in wormery castings, a handful of lime, and some
   fresh material each season. Store a couple of bags of compost — it is not
   something you can improvise in a flat.
- Vertical: railing planters, wall pockets, tiered shelves, hanging baskets.
   Trellis climbing beans and cucumbers up a string to the ceiling — vertical
   space is the one dimension a flat has plenty of.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. Quail on a balcony — the realistic livestock
-----------------------------------------------

Reread Ch. 08 §5 for the details. In summary:

- 10 coturnix quail in 1 m² (a 3-tier rabbit hutch or a purpose-built cage)
   give 8–9 eggs a day — the protein equivalent of ~1.7 chicken eggs daily.
- Laying from 6–8 weeks old. Fastest protein of any livestock.
- Quiet, small, and legal in far more places than chickens — but check your
   lease and local rules, and talk to your neighbours first.
- Feed: game-bird crumb (20–24% protein), plus your microgreen trimmings,
   sprout surplus and mealworms. ~25–30 g/bird/day → 10 birds ≈ 100 kg of feed
   a year. Store it.
- They do not go broody — renewal needs a small incubator (€60, ~40 W for 17
   days) and a heat plate for the chicks. That is a power dependency; plan for
   it.
- Droppings go to the wormery/balcony pots. Manage bedding well or the smell
   will end your neighbourly relations and your quail project together.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. Apartment-specific failure modes
-----------------------------------

These are the things that make a flat different, and each needs a decision
now.

------------------------------------------------------------------------------


10. The building is your land
-----------------------------

This is the apartment track's equivalent of a hectare, and it is the highest-
value thing you can do. A block of flats has, collectively:

- A roof — rainwater catchment for the whole building, and possibly solar.
- A courtyard, lawn or verge — a communal garden, compost bays, a waste drum.
- A basement or bike store — bulk storage, a shared freezer, a workshop.
- A stairwell — a communication network.
- Dozens of households — with a nurse, an electrician, a plumber, a gardener,
   a carpenter and a lot of labour among them.

Do this now, in GREEN:

1. Meet your immediate neighbours. Names, numbers, who lives alone, who is
    elderly, who has medical needs, who has skills. This costs nothing and is
    worth more than any purchase in this plan.
2. Propose the boring, popular things first: a communal herb planter, a
    building WhatsApp/Signal group, a shared tool cupboard, a bulk-buying
    group. Nobody objects to these, and they build the relationships that
    matter later.
3. Then the resilience items: a rainwater butt on a downpipe, a compost bin, a
    shared generator or battery for the stairwell lights and phone charging,
    an agreed plan for waste if collections stop, a list of who checks on
    whom.
4. Get it minuted. A building committee decision in GREEN is worth ten
    arguments in RED.
5. Share what you learn. A neighbour with two weeks of food is a neighbour who
    is not knocking on your door in week three — and who might have the
    medicine you run out of in week nine.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


11. The apartment kit list
--------------------------


L0 — this weekend (€250)

6 × 10 L water bottles filled · NaDCC tablets · Sawyer Mini · 4 headlamps · 16
rechargeable AA + charger · 20,000 mAh power bank + 20 W folding panel · wind-
up radio · 2 weeks of food you eat · butane stove + 8 canisters · 2 buckets +
toilet seat + 2 coir bricks · first aid kit · CO alarm · fire blanket · cash ·
documents in one bag


L1 — this month (€700–1,200)

Bathtub bladder · 6 × 20 L jerry cans · gravity filter · 1 month of food · 5
kg of sprouting seed + 4 jars + mesh lids · fermenting crock + 5 kg salt · 500
Wh–1 kWh LiFePO4 power station + 200 W of folding/balcony panels · thermal
cooker · pressure cooker · full medical chest + FFP2s · urine-diverting insert
· plunger washer · menstrual cups · window film · heavy curtains · board games
and books


L2 — three months (€2,500–4,000)

3 months of food, densely packed and inventoried · 10 jerry cans (200 L) ·
balcony rain catch · 1.5–2 kWh power station + 400 W balcony solar (check
local rules) · 12 V compressor cool box · microgreen shelf with LED (4–6 trays
on rotation) · mushroom kit + spawn · wormery · 3 months of cover material
(8–10 coir bricks) · sealed 120 L waste drum · 3-month medicine buffer · CO2
meter · Corsi–Rosenthal box · balcony containers, compost and seed · the
building agreement, in writing


L3 — twelve months (where space allows)

12 months of staples (needs a cellar locker or storage unit) · quail with feed
stored + incubator · continuous sprout/microgreen/mushroom rotation running as
a daily habit · shared building rainwater and solar · a mutual-aid group of
3–8 households with complementary skills · a rural contact — a relative,
friend or allotment — as a fallback and a source of bulk vegetables, eggs and
firewood


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


12. Apartment checklist
-----------------------

  [ ] I accept that stored food is the plan, and indoor growing is the nutrition
  [ ] 5 kg of sprouting seed per person, plus jars — and I have sprouted a jar
  [ ] Microgreen trays and seed in stock; one crop grown as practice
  [ ] Fermenting crock + salt; one batch of sauerkraut made
  [ ] Mushroom spawn and substrate plan
  [ ] Bathtub bladder ready to deploy at AMBER
  [ ] 200 L+ of water in jerry cans, stored where I can actually lift them
  [ ] Hot water cylinder drain valve located and understood
  [ ] Bucket toilet built and tested; 3 months of cover material stored
  [ ] The destination for human waste is decided and agreed with the building
  [ ] Power station + panels: enough for phones, lights, radio and medical devices
  [ ] NO combustion device that could run unflued; CO alarm fitted
  [ ] Fire extinguisher, fire blanket, smoke alarm; two exit routes known
  [ ] Cooking possible with ventilation only: gas/alcohol + thermal cooker
  [ ] 3 months of food stored in dense, calorie-rich form, inventoried
  [ ] Balcony containers, compost and seed ready (if there is sun)
  [ ] Quail considered, lease and neighbours checked
  [ ] Neighbours' names and numbers known; building group exists
  [ ] Building-level plan for water, waste and power discussed and minuted
  [ ] A rural fallback contact identified
  [ ] Blackout weekend drill completed in the flat, in winter

==============================================================================
12. 10 — SECURITY, COMMUNITY & COMMUNICATIONS
==============================================================================


10 — Security, Community & Communications
=========================================

The fortress mentality is the most seductive and most wrong idea in
preparedness. Isolated households fail. Connected neighbourhoods endure. This
chapter is about being un-noticed, being well-connected, and being informed.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. The realistic threat picture
-------------------------------

In a pandemic, the security risks are — in order of actual likelihood:

1. Opportunistic theft — an unlocked shed, a bicycle, a generator left out, a
    delivery on a doorstep, fuel siphoned from a car. Common, low-drama, and
    almost entirely preventable with locks and habits.
2. Desperate neighbours — people who did not prepare, in week six, who know or
    suspect you did. This is a social problem, not a tactical one, and it is
    by far the most likely difficulty you will face.
3. Burglary of empty or apparently empty properties — rises in any economic
    crisis.
4. Disputes — over water, fuel, boundaries, noise, animals, or somebody's
    behaviour during a stressful confinement. More likely to harm you than any
    stranger.
5. Fire and accident with no emergency services available.
6. Organised violence — very unlikely in a pandemic scenario in a stable
    European country, and if it does occur, no household plan defends against
    it. Community, distance and leaving are the only real answers.

Calibrate your effort accordingly. Good locks, good lighting discipline, good
neighbours and a fire extinguisher will do more for your safety than anything
tactical. Preparing for scenario 6 while neglecting scenarios 1–5 is a
misallocation that many people make.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. OPSEC — the discipline of not being interesting
--------------------------------------------------

The best security is that nobody thinks you have anything. Everything below is
free.

Before the crisis

- Don't talk about your preparations. Not at work, not at parties, not on
   social media, not in a Facebook group, not "just to a friend". Every person
   who knows is a person who will remember your address in week six — and who
   will mention it to someone else.
- Take deliveries discreetly; break down and dispose of packaging that
   advertises (a stack of 20 L bucket boxes at the kerb is a billboard).
- Keep the shed, garage and greenhouse locked, and the good tools out of
   sight.
- Be careful with photos: a picture of your pantry, your solar array or your
   chickens, geotagged, is an inventory with a map.

During the crisis

- Light discipline. In a dark street, a brightly lit window is visible for a
   kilometre. Blackout curtains or blankets over windows after dark. Use low,
   warm, directed light — headlamps rather than room lights.
- Sound discipline. A generator is audible for hundreds of metres and says
   "this house has fuel and things worth powering". Run it in daylight, when
   other noise exists, for short batch-charging sessions (Ch. 02), inside a
   baffle box or behind a wall, and never at night.
- Smell discipline. Cooking smells carry astonishingly far when nobody else is
   cooking. Cook at conventional mealtimes; use enclosed stoves; be careful
   with baking bread and roasting meat when others are hungry.
- Sight discipline. Don't unload supplies in view. Don't let a visible garden
   full of food face a public road without a hedge or a fence. Keep the front
   of the house looking as unremarkable and as occupied-but-ordinary as
   everyone else's.
- Routine discipline. Vary times if you must go out. Don't establish a visible
   pattern.
- Conversation discipline. "We're managing, same as everyone" is the answer.
   Not "we're fine, we've got a year of food".


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Layered physical security — in order of value
------------------------------------------------

  1. RELATIONSHIPS  — neighbours who look out for you   (free, best)
  2. APPEARANCE     — nothing worth taking, nothing unusual  (free)
  3. DETERRENCE     — lighting, dogs, visible locks, a lived-in look  (cheap)
  4. BARRIERS       — doors, locks, windows, fences, gates  (moderate)
  5. DETECTION      — alarms, sensors, a dog, a watch rota  (moderate)
  6. DELAY & REFUGE — a securable inner room, a plan, a way out  (last)

Practical, legal, and worth doing:

- Upgrade the doors and locks. Most break-ins are the front or back door
   forced in seconds. A solid door, a good multi-point lock or a deadbolt with
   a long strike screwed into the frame studs, hinge bolts, and a door chain
   cost a few hundred euros and are the single most effective physical
   measure.
- Window locks and laminated security film on accessible glass (film keeps a
   broken window in one piece — cheap and very effective).
- Motion-sensor lights (solar ones need no wiring and work in a blackout).
- A dog. An ordinary, noisy family dog is the best intruder detection there
   is, and good company through a long confinement. Factor in a year of dog
   food.
- Fences, gates and a thorny hedge — hawthorn, blackthorn, berberis, rugosa
   rose. A thorn hedge is a fence that grows itself, feeds birds, gives you
   fruit, and looks like gardening rather than fortification.
- Secure the outbuildings — this is where most theft actually happens.
- Don't store all your supplies in one place. Split the store across at least
   two locations (cellar + loft + a neighbour's garage). A single loss should
   never be total.
- A refuge room — one room with a solid door and a lock where the family can
   gather, with phones, light and water, while you wait for a situation to
   pass or for help. This is the cheap, sane version of a "safe room".

On firearms: laws vary enormously by country and this plan makes no
recommendation. If you already own firearms lawfully, secure storage, safe
handling, training and ammunition rotation are your responsibility. If you do
not, a crisis is the worst possible time to acquire and learn. Far more
households are harmed by a badly stored weapon than are saved by one, and no
weapon solves the problems in §1.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Community — the actual survival strategy
-------------------------------------------

Build a mutual-aid group of 3–8 households. Start this month.

One household cannot: keep a 24-hour watch, contain a fire, treat a serious
injury, cover every skill, share the cost of a well or a generator, care for
children while an adult is ill, or look after two people when both adults are
sick at once. A group of five households can do all of that.


How to build one, starting from nothing

1. Start with something ordinary. A street WhatsApp/Signal group. A tool
    library. A bulk-buy order. A shared skip. A seed swap. Helping an elderly
    neighbour with shopping. None of this requires mentioning "prepping" at
    all.
2. Notice who is capable and who is reliable. Over a few months you will learn
    who shows up. Those are your people.
3. Have the conversation gently, one household at a time, framed around things
    everyone remembers: "after the last few years, I've been thinking we
    should have a plan as a street for if the power goes out for a week."
4. Agree small, concrete things: a check-in signal, who has a chainsaw, who is
    medically trained, who has a generator, who lives alone and needs checking
    on, where you'd meet.
5. Grow it into: shared bulk purchasing (cheaper for everyone, less
    conspicuous than one household buying a tonne of rice), shared expensive
    kit (a chainsaw, a log splitter, a pressure canner, a generator, a
    freezer), a skill exchange, childcare, and a watch rota if it ever comes
    to that.


What a good group has

- Complementary skills: medical (a nurse or paramedic is worth more than any
   amount of kit), electrical, mechanical, building, growing, animal
   husbandry, cooking and preserving, childcare, and at least one person
   everyone trusts to mediate an argument.
- Complementary resources: someone with land, someone with a well, someone
   with a workshop, someone with a vehicle, someone with a big freezer.
- Agreed decision-making. How do you decide something, and who decides when
   you disagree? Sort this out while it is theoretical.
- Explicit reciprocity. Generosity works between people who both give. Agree
   what is shared, what is lent, and what is private — before scarcity makes
   it emotional.


The rural/urban link

If you are in a flat, cultivate a rural contact: a relative, a friend with
land, an allotment, a farm you buy from directly. If you have land, cultivate
urban contacts who have skills, tools and hands. This link runs both ways in a
crisis and both sides need it.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. The charity plan — decide this now, in calm
----------------------------------------------

In week six, someone you know will knock on your door and say their children
are hungry. Decide today what you will do, because deciding in the moment, at
the door, with your own children behind you, is how people end up making
choices they cannot live with.

The reasoning:

- Turning everyone away hardens you, poisons your relationships with the
   community you depend on, and — practically — creates the exact resentment
   that makes you a target.
- Giving without limit empties your stores and helps nobody, including the
   person you gave to.
- So set a policy, in advance, that you can sustain and explain.

A workable approach:

1. Set aside a "charity shelf" as a deliberate line item — perhaps 10% of your
    store, bought and packed separately. Cheap, bulky, high-calorie items:
    rice, oats, flour, beans, pasta, oil, salt. This is not "spare" food; it
    is budgeted, and giving it does not touch your family's rations.
2. Give at the gate, not in the house. Nobody needs to see inside.
3. Give food, not information. Never confirm how much you have.
4. Prefer giving through the group or a community point — a church, a
    neighbour who is organising, a street food table. It is more effective,
    more dignified for the recipient, and it does not mark your door.
5. Prefer giving work where you can: "I can't give much, but there's a day's
    work here and you'll eat with us." Dignity matters, and labour is a thing
    you will need.
6. Prioritise: children, the elderly, the ill, and the people in your mutual-
    aid group — in that order.
7. Have a line, and hold it kindly. "This is what we can give. I'm sorry it
    isn't more." No lectures, no justifications, no arguments at the door.
8. Help people prepare NOW instead. Every neighbour who has two weeks of food
    is one who will not knock in week three. Sharing this plan is a security
    measure, and the cheapest one available.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Communications
-----------------


Getting information in

- Battery/wind-up/solar radio with FM + AM + shortwave. €40–80. FM gives you
   local and national broadcasting; shortwave gives you the international
   picture when domestic media is disrupted or unreliable. Buy one now, and
   learn to use it — listening to shortwave is a skill.
- A second radio in the go-bag. Radios are cheap; being uninformed is not.
- DAB dies with the transmitter network's power; FM and AM are far more
   robust. Don't rely on DAB alone.
- NOAA/weather band or your national weather service — weather matters a lot
   more when your food is outdoors and your heat is wood.
- Note official emergency broadcast frequencies for your country on paper.


Talking to each other

- Mobile networks fail — cells have 2–8 hours of battery, and networks congest
   instantly in a crisis. SMS often gets through when calls do not.
- PMR446 / FRS radios (licence-free in the EU): €50 a pair, 1–3 km in real
   terrain. Perfect for the street, the garden, a shopping trip, and the
   mutual-aid group. Buy 4, plus rechargeable batteries.
- Amateur radio (ham) is the serious option: real range, repeater networks,
   emergency traffic handling, and a community of skilled operators. It
   requires a licence (a few weeks of study and an exam — do it now, it's
   genuinely interesting) and €50–200 for a handheld. A licensed operator in
   your mutual-aid group is a major asset.
- [!] Transmitting on amateur frequencies without a licence is illegal, and
   "it's an emergency" only covers genuine immediate danger to life. Get
   licensed in GREEN.
- Agreed radio schedule: the group checks in at a fixed time on a fixed
   channel, e.g. 09:00 and 19:00 daily. A schedule saves batteries and means a
   missed check-in is itself information.
- Low-tech signals, agreed in advance: a coloured cloth in a window (green =
   all well, red = help needed), a whistle code, a torch signal at night, a
   chalk mark. Free, silent, and they work when everything else doesn't.


The family contact plan (write it, print it, everyone carries a copy)

  - Out-of-area contact person (often reachable when local networks are congested)
  - Everyone's number, address, workplace, school — ON PAPER
    (nobody memorises numbers any more; a dead phone is total memory loss)
  - Primary rally point (home) and secondary rally point (if home is unreachable)
  - Check-in times and the order to try: SMS → call → radio → in person
  - What each person does if separated, per scenario, per location
  - Where the spare key, the documents, the cash and the go-bag are
  - Medical information for each person

Practise it with the children. Twice a year.


Offline information — build a paper library

The internet will not always be there, and a phone with a dead battery
contains nothing. Print what matters.

- Medical: Where There Is No Doctor, Where There Is No Dentist, a first aid
   manual
- Food: a preserving book with tested canning times, a bread book, a
   fermentation book, a foraging guide with good photographs for your region
- Growing: a vegetable growing guide for your climate, a seed-saving guide, a
   fruit pruning guide
- Practical: basic electrics and plumbing, engine and bicycle maintenance,
   knots, sewing, soap making, butchery
- Local: paper maps of your region (road and topographic), a compass
- Your own plan, printed, in a binder, with the checklists filled in
- An e-reader with thousands of books uses about 1 Wh per day and is an
   outstanding use of a small solar system — but keep the critical references
   on paper as well. Electronics fail; paper doesn't.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Security & communications checklist
--------------------------------------

  [ ] Nobody outside the household knows the scale of our preparations
  [ ] No photos, posts or geotagged evidence of supplies exist
  [ ] Blackout curtains or blankets for every window
  [ ] Generator siting, timing and baffling planned for noise
  [ ] Doors and locks upgraded; hinge bolts and long strike plates fitted
  [ ] Window locks; security film on accessible glass
  [ ] Outbuildings and tools secured
  [ ] Motion-sensor (solar) lighting fitted
  [ ] Supplies split across at least two locations
  [ ] Refuge room identified
  [ ] Fire extinguishers, blanket and smoke alarms — no fire brigade coming
  [ ] 3–8 household mutual-aid group forming, with names and numbers
  [ ] Group skills mapped; gaps identified
  [ ] Shared kit and bulk-buying arrangements agreed
  [ ] Charity shelf bought, packed separately, and its policy agreed in writing
  [ ] Neighbours encouraged and helped to prepare themselves
  [ ] FM/AM/shortwave radio + spare, with batteries
  [ ] PMR446 radios ×4 with rechargeables; ham licence being pursued
  [ ] Radio check-in schedule agreed with the group
  [ ] Low-tech signals agreed (window cloth, whistle, torch)
  [ ] Family contact plan written, printed, carried, and practised
  [ ] Paper library printed and shelved
  [ ] Paper maps and a compass
  [ ] Rural↔urban contact established


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[BUILD] Build it: All build plans — including the generator noise screen and
rodent-proof storage

documents


==============================================================================
13. 11 — TOOLS, SPARES, MONEY & DOCUMENTS
==============================================================================


11 — Tools, Spares, Money & Documents
=====================================

The unglamorous chapter. It is about the things that break, the things you
cannot buy, and the pieces of paper that prove who you are.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. The single-point-of-failure audit
------------------------------------

Walk through your whole plan and ask one question: what breaks, and what
happens when it does?

System      The single point of failure       The fix
----------  --------------------------------  --------------------------------
Water       The filter element                2 spare elements + chemical
                                              disinfection + a pot to boil in

Water       The pump                          A hand pump, or a bucket and
                                              rope

Power       The charge controller             A spare (€60). It is the most
                                              likely electronic failure.

Power       The inverter                      12 V DC lighting and USB that
                                              works without it

Power       Cables and connectors             Spare MC4 connectors, cable,
                                              fuses, crimps

Cooking     The stove                         A second, different-fuel stove +
                                              a thermal cooker

Heat        The chimney                       Own rods, sweep it annually
Food        The can opener                    Three. (The classic.)
Food        The grain mill                    The hand version, plus spare
                                              burrs

Light       Batteries                         Rechargeables + solar charger,
                                              one standard cell size

Health      Glasses                           A spare pair per person +
                                              prescription copy

Health      Prescription medicine             A buffer, built legally over
                                              months

Growing     Seed                              A 3–5 year seed bank + saved
                                              seed + OP varieties

Livestock   The rooster / breeding stock      Keep two, or an exchange
                                              agreement with the group

Security    The one person who knows how it   Write it down and teach someone
            all works                         else

Everything  The house burning down            Split the store; fire
                                              extinguishers; insurance
                                              documents offsite


That last one is the most commonly overlooked. If only one adult understands
the power system, the water treatment, the stove and where everything is, then
that person getting ill — which is precisely what a pandemic does — disables
the whole household. Write the manual. Teach the family. Run a drill where
that person says nothing all weekend.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. Tools
--------

Buy quality once. Cheap tools fail at the worst moment, and there will be no
replacement.


Hand tools — the non-negotiable core

Hammer (claw + club) · screwdrivers (or a good ratchet set with bits) ·
spanners (metric set) + adjustable · pliers (combination, long-nose, water
pump) · wire cutters · hacksaw + spare blades · wood saw (panel + tenon) ·
chisels · hand drill or brace + bits (works with no power) · files and rasps ·
sharpening stone + file + honing steel · measuring tape · spirit level ·
square · utility knife + blades · vice or clamps · crowbar · bolt cutters ·
shovel, spade, pick · axe, splitting maul, wedges · bow saw · sledgehammer ·
wheelbarrow.


Powered tools (while there is power)

Cordless drill/driver + 2 batteries · angle grinder + cutting/grinding discs ·
circular saw or jigsaw · chainsaw + full PPE · multimeter · soldering iron ·
inspection lamp. Standardise on one battery platform so packs and chargers are
interchangeable, and buy a 12 V DC charger for that platform so you can charge
from the solar system directly.


Specialist but high-value

Pipe wrench and plumbing fittings · PTFE tape · pipe freeze kit · drain rods ·
soldering/compression fittings · wire strippers and crimps · a multimeter (you
cannot diagnose a power system without one) · a clamp meter · a tyre pump and
puncture kit · a bicycle tool set · a sewing kit and an awl · a manual sewing
machine (treadle or hand-crank — genuinely useful and completely power-free) ·
leatherwork awl and waxed thread · a scythe and peening kit · a hand-cranked
drill · a soil auger.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. The spares and consumables box
---------------------------------

These are the things you cannot make, cannot substitute, and will not be able
to buy. All of it is cheap, small, and stores indefinitely. This box is one of
the highest-value €300 in the whole plan.

Fixings and fastening: screws and nails (assorted, in quantity) · bolts, nuts
and washers · wall plugs · zip ties (500) · picture wire · galvanised wire (a
roll) · staples · split pins · hose clamps (assorted, a box) · jubilee clips ·
O-rings and washers (tap, hose, plumbing) · rivets.

Tapes and adhesives: duct/gaffer tape (5 rolls) · electrical tape · self-
amalgamating tape (fixes leaking pipes under pressure) · PTFE tape · two-part
epoxy · superglue · wood glue · contact adhesive · silicone sealant ·
plumber's putty · self-fusing repair tape.

Sealing and waterproofing: plastic sheeting and heavy polythene · tarpaulins
(2–3, various sizes — endlessly useful) · roofing felt and nails · flashing
tape · window film · expanding foam · bitumen paint.

Electrical: fuses (every rating you use — car blade, ceramic, MCBs) · cable
(mains and DC, several gauges) · connectors and terminal blocks · crimps · MC4
solar connectors · a spare MPPT charge controller · a spare inverter if budget
allows · bulbs and LED strips · switches · a 12 V socket set · batteries
(rechargeable + a sealed pack of alkalines for the go-bag).

Fluids: engine oil · 2-stroke oil · chain oil · grease · WD-40/penetrating oil
· brake fluid · antifreeze · fuel stabiliser · methylated spirits · white
spirit · lamp oil.

Cordage and fabric: rope (10 m each of 6 mm, 10 mm, plus paracord) · baler
twine · bungee cords · sewing thread, needles, buttons, zips, elastic · fabric
patches and denim offcuts · leather scraps · wool blankets · a roll of canvas.

Consumables you will run out of: matches (thousands, stored dry) and ferro
rods (3) · lighters (20) · candles · batteries · water filter elements ·
canning lids (several hundred) · pressure canner gasket and safety plug · salt
· soap · bleach/NaDCC · sandpaper · saw blades · drill bits · grinding discs ·
chainsaw chains (2 spare) and files · spark plugs and a spare carburettor for
each small engine · air and fuel filters · mower/strimmer line · sharpening
stones · pencils and paper (ink and printers fail; you will want to write
things down for a year).

Personal: boot laces · boot repair glue · spare work gloves (6 pairs — you
will destroy them) · spare wellies · thermal base layers · wool socks (lots) ·
a good hat.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Money and barter
-------------------


Cash

- ATMs and card terminals fail early in every crisis — a power cut, a network
   outage, a bank holiday, or a run on cash. Cash works when nothing else
   does, and only for a while.
- Hold enough cash for 4–8 weeks of essential spending, in small denominations
   (nobody will have change for a €200 note, and handing one over tells people
   exactly the wrong thing about you).
- Store it split across at least two secure places, not in an obvious box.
- Keep some smaller coins and notes in the go-bag and the car.
- Insurance: keep the policy documents where you can reach them, and
   photograph everything of value now for a future claim.


Beyond cash

- Hyperinflation and currency stress are realistic in a multi-year crisis.
   Diversification — some savings in a different form (precious metal, foreign
   currency, real assets) — is a personal financial decision, not a survival
   tactic, and this plan makes no investment recommendation. If you do hold
   physical metal, small denominations only; nobody can make change for a 1 oz
   coin with potatoes.


Barter goods — the practical list

The best barter goods are cheap now, impossible later, small, non-perishable,
and consumed continuously. Buy an extra box of these purely for trade:

Tier         Goods
-----------  -----------------------------------------------------------------
Best         Coffee · salt · sugar · soap · lighters and matches · batteries ·
             painkillers · toilet paper · candles · yeast · spices · seeds ·
             water filters · NaDCC tablets · sewing supplies · tampons/pads ·
             condoms · alcohol (spirits — also a disinfectant and a
             preservative)

Good         Tea · cooking oil · honey · tinned meat · rope · tape · fuel ·
             nails and screws · socks · gloves · tools · rechargeable
             batteries · reading glasses · bicycle inner tubes

Best of all  Skills. Medical care, dentistry, repair, welding, sewing,
             butchery, midwifery, teaching. They cannot be stolen, they weigh
             nothing, and they do not run out.

[!] Avoid    Trading weapons, ammunition, or prescription medication — it is
             illegal, it creates danger for you, and it makes you a known
             source


Barter discipline: trade through the group and at the gate, never from your
store, never revealing quantity. A reputation as a trader attracts attention
you do not want.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. The document pack
--------------------

If you ever have to leave, or if you need to prove anything to anyone, this is
what matters. Assemble it this weekend — it costs an afternoon and nothing
else.

One waterproof folder, grab-and-go, plus a duplicate elsewhere:

  IDENTITY        Passports · ID cards · driving licences · birth certificates
                  Marriage certificate · residence permits · citizenship papers
  PROPERTY        Deeds or lease · mortgage papers · land registry extract
                  Vehicle registration and insurance
  FINANCIAL       Bank and account details · insurance policies (home, health,
                  life, vehicle) · pension details · recent statements · tax number
  MEDICAL         For each person: prescription list (generic names + doses),
                  allergies, conditions, blood group, vaccination records,
                  GP and specialist contact details, EHIC/health card
                  Spare glasses prescription
  FAMILY          The contact plan (Ch. 10) · school records · pet vaccination
                  and chip records
  PRACTICAL       Photos of every room and every valuable (for insurance claims)
                  Serial numbers of tools, appliances, generator, bicycles
                  Your own system diagrams: power, water, stopcocks, consumer unit
                  This plan, printed, with checklists filled in
  CASH            Some, in the folder

Three copies, three forms:

1. Paper in the waterproof folder at home, where anyone in the family can find
    it.
2. Encrypted USB (VeraCrypt or similar) in the go-bag, with the passphrase
    known to two people.
3. Encrypted cloud backup and/or a copy with a trusted relative in another
    town. Fire, flood and theft are more likely than any pandemic scenario.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Skills — the things that take longest to acquire
---------------------------------------------------

Rank your skill gaps and learn one per month. They are free, they weigh
nothing, they cannot be stolen, and they are the difference between owning
equipment and being capable.

Tier 1 — everyone in the household should have these First aid and CPR ·
lighting a fire in the wet · using a fire extinguisher · turning off water,
gas and electricity at the main · basic hygiene discipline · cooking a meal
from dry staples · hand-washing clothes · using the radio · where everything
is and how it works.

Tier 2 — at least one adult Cooking from raw ingredients including bread,
beans and grains · food preservation: canning, fermenting, drying · vegetable
growing and seed saving · sharpening tools · basic sewing and mending · basic
plumbing (isolate, drain, fix a leak, replace a washer) · basic electrics and
12 V DC wiring · using a multimeter · small-engine maintenance · bicycle
repair · safe axe and saw use · navigation with a map and compass · water
treatment · butchering a chicken or a rabbit.

Tier 3 — high value, worth someone in the group having Advanced first aid /
wilderness first responder · beekeeping · dairying and cheesemaking · larger-
animal husbandry and slaughter · welding · carpentry and building repair ·
soap making · brewing and distilling (for vinegar and disinfectant — check
your local law) · amateur radio · midwifery · dentistry basics ·
gunsmithing/reloading where lawful · chainsaw certification · tree felling.

How to learn them: local courses, a smallholding association, a beekeeping
club, a repair café, the person down the road who already does it. Every one
of these skills is more fun to learn than it is to read about, and every one
of them is useful in normal times too. That is the test of a good preparation:
it makes your ordinary life better as well.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Tools, spares & money checklist
----------------------------------

  [ ] Single-point-of-failure audit done; each one has an answer
  [ ] The system manual is WRITTEN, and a second person has been taught
  [ ] Full hand-tool set, quality, with sharpening kit
  [ ] Cordless platform standardised, with a 12 V charger
  [ ] Multimeter owned and used
  [ ] The spares box assembled (fixings, tape, epoxy, fuses, cable, cordage)
  [ ] Spare charge controller, fuses, connectors and cable
  [ ] Spare water filter elements ×2
  [ ] Spare glasses per person
  [ ] Spare canning lids, canner gasket, chainsaw chains, spark plugs, carburettor
  [ ] Matches, ferro rods, lighters, candles — in quantity
  [ ] Paper and pencils for a year
  [ ] 4–8 weeks of cash in small denominations, split across two places
  [ ] Barter box bought separately from the family store
  [ ] Document pack assembled: paper + encrypted USB + offsite copy
  [ ] Photos of home contents taken for insurance
  [ ] System diagrams drawn and filed with the documents
  [ ] Skills gap list written; one skill being learned each month
  [ ] At least one adult can do every Tier 2 skill


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[BUILD] Build it: All build plans — the tool kit, timber sizes, fixings and
mesh guide are in the build index

Previous: 10 — Security, community & communications · Next: 12 — Operating
cycles & calendar


==============================================================================
14. 12 — OPERATING CYCLES & CALENDAR
==============================================================================


12 — Operating Cycles & Calendar
================================

A stockpile is a countdown. A cycle is a system. This chapter is what turns
the first into the second — the routines that keep stores rotated, gardens
producing, animals breeding, equipment working and people sane.

This is the chapter that answers "how do I make it repeat?"


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. The four loops
-----------------

Everything in this plan sits in one of four nested loops. If any loop stops,
the whole system quietly degrades until you notice it has failed.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ANNUAL LOOP   Seed → plant → harvest → preserve → save seed    │
  │                Breed → raise → cull → keep breeding stock       │
  │                Cut → season → burn wood → cut again             │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  SEASONAL LOOP   Sow, tend, harvest, store, rest          │  │
  │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │  │
  │  │  │  WEEKLY LOOP   Bake, launder, clean, inventory,     │  │  │
  │  │  │                maintain, plan, rest                  │  │  │
  │  │  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │  │  │
  │  │  │  │  DAILY LOOP   Water, power, animals, food,    │  │  │  │
  │  │  │  │               sanitation, health, comms       │  │  │  │
  │  │  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │  │  │
  │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Plus the continuous indoor loops that run in any season, in any dwelling:
sprouts (3–5 days) · microgreens (7–14 days) · mushrooms (3–5 weeks) ·
ferments (1–3 weeks) · sourdough (daily) · compost/wormery (continuous). These
are the loops an apartment can run, and they are the reason a flat is
survivable for a year.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. The daily routine in a crisis
--------------------------------

Structure is not bureaucracy — it is the thing that holds a household together
over months of confinement. Write this on a card and put it on the wall.


Morning


-----  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
07:00  Wake at a fixed time. Everyone. Drifting sleep is the first sign of a
       household coming apart.

       Health check: anyone symptomatic? Temperature and pulse ox if so. Log
       it.

       Light the stove, put the kettle and the big pot on. One fire does
       breakfast, hot water and the room.

       Water: check levels, run the filter (it should be filtering
       continuously), top up the day's clean supply, fill the hand-wash
       station.

       Power: check battery state of charge, clear snow/leaves off the panels,
       plan the day's loads around the forecast. Charge everything while the
       sun is up.

       Animals: feed, water (break ice in winter), collect eggs, check every
       animal is well and the housing is secure.

08:00  Breakfast together. Eat as a household at fixed times — it anchors the
       day.

       Rinse the sprout jars (morning rinse). Check the microgreens and
       mushrooms.



Day


-----  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
09:00  Radio check-in with the mutual-aid group.
       The day's main work, assigned the night before: garden, wood, repairs,
       preserving, building, laundry, teaching. Everyone has a job.

12:30  Lunch. Usually the previous night's leftovers or something from the
       thermal cooker.

13:30  Second work block. Children: school work. Adults: rotate the heavy job.
15:00  Start the evening meal early — get it to the boil and into the thermal
       cooker, so it finishes itself with no more fuel.

       Daylight tasks finish: anything needing light gets done before dusk,
       always.



Evening


-----  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
17:00  Secure for the night: animals shut in, doors locked, tools in, blackout
       curtains closed, water containers refilled.

       Rinse the sprout jars (evening rinse).
18:00  Dinner together. No radio, no crisis talk.
19:00  Radio check-in. News window: 20 minutes, one source, then off.
       Household meeting (10 min): what happened today, what's the plan
       tomorrow, who is doing what, is anyone struggling.

20:00  Non-productive time, deliberately. Cards, music, reading aloud, games,
       talking. This is not a luxury — it is the maintenance schedule for the
       people.

22:00  Lights out. Log the day in the household journal: weather, stores used,
       work done, anything that changed.


Rotate the unpleasant jobs (emptying the toilet bucket, hauling water,
laundry) on a written rota. Nothing corrodes a household faster than one
person silently doing all the worst work.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. The weekly cycle
-------------------

Day        Task
---------  -------------------------------------------------------------------
Monday     Laundry. One big batch — water heated once, washed, rinsed, hung.
           Reuse the water on the garden or for flushing.

Tuesday    Bake day. Bread for the week; the oven/stove is hot, so also roast,
           dry herbs, render fat, heat wash water. One fire, many jobs.

Wednesday  Maintenance. Sharpen tools, fix what broke, check the stove and
           flue, service one system properly. Something breaks every week;
           deal with it before it accumulates.

Thursday   Garden/production day. The big session: planting, weeding,
           harvesting, preserving. Start the next ferment.

Friday     Inventory and stores. Count what was used, update the written
           inventory, check for damp/pests/spoilage, rotate stock forward, set
           next week's ration.

Saturday   Projects and improvements. Build the thing you've been meaning to
           build. Also: proper wash day for people.

Sunday     Rest, and celebrate something. A better meal. A game. Contact with
           people outside the household. Take the day off deliberately —
           burnout is a survival risk.


Also weekly: test the CO and smoke alarms · check the animals for mites and
parasites · clean the water filter housing · check battery water if you have
lead-acid · full handover briefing so everyone knows the state of every
system.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. The monthly cycle
--------------------

- Full inventory count against the written record. Reconcile. Calculate days
   of endurance remaining for water, food, fuel, medicine and cover material.
   Write the number down and track the trend — this single number tells you
   whether your rationing is right, months before you would otherwise notice.
- Run the generator for 30 minutes under load (unused engines seize; fuel goes
   stale in carburettors). Check oil.
- Rotate fuel. Use the oldest petrol in the car, refill the can.
- Rotate water. Empty, clean and refill a share of the storage.
- Check and rotate medicines. Anything within 3 months of expiry moves to the
   front for use.
- Test the radios, charge every battery bank, verify the group's comms plan.
- Deep clean one system: the water filter elements, the stove and flue, the
   coop, the food store.
- Group meeting with the mutual-aid households — in person if possible.
- Skills hour: teach one household member one thing they can't yet do.
- Review the plan. What changed? What did we learn? Update this document.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------
