------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Maintenance and rotation schedule
------------------------------------

Interval         Task
---------------  -------------------------------------------------------------
Daily            Filter water · check battery SoC · animals fed, watered and
                 secure · sprouts rinsed ×2 · health check · stove ash and air
                 control

Weekly           Test CO/smoke alarms · clean the filter housing · check
                 stores for damp and pests · sharpen what was used · mite
                 check on poultry · update the inventory

Monthly          Generator run under load · fuel rotated · a share of water
                 storage emptied and refilled · medicines checked for expiry ·
                 radios tested · all batteries cycled · full inventory count
                 and endurance calculation

Quarterly        Eat-the-store week · deep-clean a system · full first aid kit
                 audit · drill (blackout, water-off, or isolation) · group
                 meeting · review and update the plan

Every 6 months   Full inventory reconciliation · rotate stored water fully ·
                 rotate petrol · chimney check · panel tilt changed
                 (spring/autumn) · test the whole power system under load ·
                 replace anything expired

Annually         Chimney swept · water filter elements replaced or deep-
                 cleaned · water source lab-tested (well/spring) · fire
                 extinguishers serviced · seed germination test · seed bank
                 topped up · CO alarm test with a test gas · insurance and
                 documents reviewed · wood: burn this year's, season next
                 year's, cut the year after's · lime/pH test · rotate the
                 medicine buffer · replace bleach

Every 2–5 years  Replace NaDCC tablets and O2 absorber stock · replace
                 vitamins · replace CO alarms (5–7 yrs) · replace polytunnel
                 polythene (5–7 yrs) · refresh seed potatoes with certified
                 stock · battery health check · refresh breeding stock
                 genetics



------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. The rotation ledger
----------------------

Keep one page, on the wall or in the binder, that answers "what expires next?"

  ITEM                    QTY     PACKED/BOUGHT   REVIEW BY     ACTION
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Stored water (barrels)  600 L   2026-03         2026-09       Empty/refill
  Petrol (jerry cans)     40 L    2026-05         2026-11       Into the car
  Bleach                  5 L     2026-02         2027-02       Replace
  Vitamins                12 mo   2026-01         2028-01       Replace
  Seed bank               —       2026-01         2027-01       Germination test
  Filter elements         2       2025-11         2027-11       Replace
  Medicine buffer         3 mo    rolling         monthly       Use oldest first
  Canning lids            500     2026-04         —             Count after season
  Chicken feed            400 kg  2026-10         2027-04       Must last to March
  Cover material          250 L   2026-09         —             Top up in autumn
  Wood (this winter)      4 m³    seasoned 2025   burn 2026-27  Cut 2027-28's now

Update it every month. This one page is the difference between a system and a
pile of stuff.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. The annual review
--------------------

Once a year — December is ideal — sit down as a household and do this
properly. It takes an afternoon.

1. Re-run the household audit (worksheets/household-audit.md). What changed?
    New baby, new condition, new medication, someone moved out, a child now
    old enough to have real jobs?
2. Recalculate endurance in days for every category. What is the lowest
    number? That is next year's priority, regardless of what you feel like
    buying.
3. Read the garden notebook. What actually grew well here? What failed twice?
    Stop growing what fails; plant more of what works.
4. Read the drill log. What broke? What did we not know how to do?
5. Review the skills list. What did we learn this year? What's next?
6. Check the group. Who joined, who left, what gaps opened?
7. Set next year's targets: one level up in the weakest category, two new
    skills, one new perennial planting, one new production cycle.
8. Update this plan and print it again. A plan that doesn't change is a plan
    nobody is using.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


10. Cycles checklist
--------------------

  [ ] A written daily routine is on the wall, and everyone knows their jobs
  [ ] Job rotation agreed in writing — nobody silently does all the worst work
  [ ] Weekly rhythm established (laundry / bake / maintain / grow / inventory / rest)
  [ ] Monthly inventory count and endurance-in-days figure being tracked
  [ ] Rotation ledger written and updated monthly
  [ ] Garden calendar adapted to OUR frost dates and pinned up
  [ ] Livestock breeding calendar written; autumn feed audit scheduled
  [ ] Fodder crops in the planting plan, not an afterthought
  [ ] Wood cycle is two years ahead
  [ ] Seed saved every year; germination tested every year
  [ ] Continuous indoor cycles running NOW: sprouts, ferments, compost
  [ ] Quarterly drill scheduled in the calendar
  [ ] Annual review booked for December
  [ ] A rest day every week, defended


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[BUILD] Build it: All build plans — construction plans for everything in this
plan, in priority order

Previous: 11 — Tools, spares, money & documents · Next: 13 — Offline, paper &
keeping the plan readable


==============================================================================
15. 13 — OFFLINE, PAPER & KEEPING THE PLAN READABLE
==============================================================================


13 — Offline, Paper & Keeping the Plan Readable
===============================================

A survival plan stored on a website you cannot reach, or a phone that is dead,
is not a plan. This chapter makes the whole thing work with no internet, no
power, and no functioning device.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. The failure this prevents
----------------------------

Think through the actual sequence. The grid goes down, or the network
congests, or your phone dies on day three and the charger is useless. At that
exact moment you want to know: how many drops of bleach per litre? what is the
oral rehydration recipe? what temperature kills botulism spores? which vent
goes high and which goes low?

If the answer lives in a browser tab, a cloud document, a bookmark, or a
GitHub repository, you do not have it.

  Website        → needs internet + power + a working device
  Cloud document → needs internet + an account + power + a device
  Phone PDF      → needs power + that specific device
  Laptop file    → needs power + that specific device
  E-reader       → needs occasional power (weeks per charge)
  USB stick      → needs SOME working computer
  * PAPER        → needs daylight, or a candle

Every layer above paper can fail simultaneously, and in a bad winter they
will. Paper is the floor you build up from.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. The rule: three copies, three formats, one off-site
------------------------------------------------------

Adapted from the standard backup rule, for a household:

#  Copy                   Format            Where
-  ---------------------  ----------------  ----------------------------------
1  Printed binder         Paper             On the shelf, in the house,
                                            findable by anyone

2  Laminated cards        Paper             By the water store, the stove, the
                                            medical kit, the toilet

3  E-reader or old phone  Digital, offline  In the kit, charged, with the
                                            solar charger

4  USB stick ×2           Digital           One in the document pack, one with
                                            a relative in another town

5  Repository clone       Digital           On the family computer, so you can
                                            reprint and update


One copy must be off-site. Fire, flood and theft are more likely than any
pandemic scenario, and they take the house, the binder and the USB stick in
the document drawer together.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. What to print, in priority order
-----------------------------------

Printing 5,500 lines is about 150–200 A4 pages double-sided. That is one ream
and an afternoon. But if you only print some of it, print it in this order.


Tier 1 — print today, laminate, and post on the wall (8 pages)

worksheets/quick-reference-cards.md — the numbers you need under stress, by
torchlight, without reading a chapter: water treatment doses · oral
rehydration recipe · bleach dilutions · carbon monoxide rules · when to seek
medical help · the trigger ladder · the Amber 72-hour list · the daily routine
· your shut-off locations · your contacts.

These ten cards are the single highest-value pages in the entire plan. Print
two sets — one for the kitchen, one for the kit.


Tier 2 — the working core (about 60 pages)

- 01 Water · 04 Heating, cooking & fuel · 05 Sanitation · 06 Health & pandemic
   protocol
- worksheets/household-audit.md — print it and fill it in by hand. A filled-in
   audit is worth more than the whole rest of the binder.
- worksheets/shopping-lists.md
- 12 Operating cycles — the daily, weekly and seasonal routines


Tier 3 — the rest of the plan (about 80 pages)

Chapters 00, 02, 03, 07–11, the drill log, and the inventory template.


Tier 4 — the builds (about 50 pages)

builds/. Print the ones you intend to build, when you intend to build them —
they are working drawings, so they will get wet, muddy and annotated. Print
the chicken coop and the bucket filter regardless; those two get built by most
households.


How to print the whole thing in one go

Open offline/survival-plan.html in any browser and press Print. It is
formatted for A4: each chapter starts on a new page, tables and diagrams are
kept whole, dark backgrounds are dropped, and external web addresses are
printed in full after their link text so a paper reader can still see where
something came from.

Print double-sided, punch it, and put it in a ring binder with dividers. A
ring binder beats a stapled stack: you can reprint one chapter, you can add
your own notes, and you can pull out the page you need and take it to the job.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. The offline bundle
---------------------

Two files in offline/, both generated from the markdown sources:

File                What it is                    Use it when
------------------  ----------------------------  ----------------------------
survival-plan.html  The complete plan as one      Any device with a browser
                    self-contained page. No
                    network, no fonts, no
                    images, no server, no CDN.
                    Opens from a USB stick by
                    double-clicking. Readable on
                    a phone, works in dark mode,
                    prints properly.

survival-plan.txt   The complete plan as plain    E-readers, old phones,
                    UTF-8 text, wrapped to 78     terminals, anything that
                    columns, tables rendered as   chokes on HTML. Prints on
                    aligned columns.              anything.


Both are committed to the repository, so downloading the repo as a ZIP from a
browser gives you the ready-to-use files with nothing to build or install.


Rebuilding them

If you edit the plan — and you should, it is your plan — regenerate with:

  python3 tools/build_offline.py      # or: make offline

The build script uses only the Python standard library: no pip install, no
internet, no pandoc, no Node. It runs on any machine with Python 3, today or
in ten years. It also self-checks the output and fails if the page would try
to fetch anything at all from the network.

  make offline    build both files
  make check      verify the build fetches nothing, without writing
  make links      verify every internal link and anchor resolves
  make print      print instructions
  make clean      remove generated files


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Devices that actually work when the power is uncertain
---------------------------------------------------------

Device                    Battery life               Verdict
------------------------  -------------------------  -------------------------
E-reader (e-ink)          Weeks per charge, ~1       * The best offline
                          Wh/day                     library there is. Holds
                                                     thousands of books, sips
                                                     power, readable in bright
                                                     sun, charges from a tiny
                                                     solar panel. Load it with
                                                     this plan plus the
                                                     reference library below.

Old phone, wiped,         Days, as a dedicated       * Excellent second copy.
airplane mode             reader                     No SIM, no apps, WiFi
                                                     off, screen dim. It
                                                     becomes a library, not a
                                                     phone.

Current phone             1–2 days                   Fine, but it is doing ten
                                                     other jobs and it will be
                                                     flat

Tablet                    1–2 days                   Good screen for build
                                                     drawings; power-hungry

Laptop                    Hours                      Only for editing and
                                                     printing, not for reading
                                                     in a crisis

Paper                     Infinite                   * The one that cannot
                                                     fail


Charging all of these is covered by the P0 tier in Chapter 02 — a 20 W folding
panel and a power bank, about €150, which is the cheapest meaningful
preparation in the whole plan.


Honest note on digital media longevity

- USB flash and SD cards: reliable for a few years, then increasingly not.
   Unpowered charge leakage is real. Copy the data to a fresh stick every 2
   years, keep two sticks, and never treat one as an archive.
- Hard drives: fine while used, unreliable after years on a shelf.
- Optical discs: ordinary burned DVDs degrade in 5–10 years; archival M-DISC
   is rated far longer but you need a drive to read it.
- Paper in a dry box: decades to centuries, readable by anyone, no format
   obsolescence, no reader required.

Conclusion: paper is the archive. Digital is the convenience. Keep both, and
refresh the digital copies on the annual review (Chapter 12 §9).


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. The offline reference library — download and print BEFORE you need it
------------------------------------------------------------------------

This plan is deliberately self-contained: everything you need to act is in
these pages. But a few outside references are genuinely worth having, and
every one of them must be obtained while you still have a connection.


Get these now

The rule

| Anything that exists only as a bookmark does not exist. Download it, print
| the parts you would need in the dark, and put the files on the e-reader and
| both USB sticks.

Do a download day once a year, at the annual review. It takes two hours and it
is the cheapest preparation on the list.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Make it findable and readable by someone else
------------------------------------------------

This is the single-point-of-failure rule applied to the plan itself. If only
you can find it and only you understand it, then you getting ill — which is
exactly what a pandemic does — takes the whole household's plan out with you.

  [ ] The binder lives in ONE obvious, named place. Everyone knows where.
  [ ] It is labelled on the spine, in plain language, not "prep stuff".
  [ ] The quick-reference cards are ON THE WALL, not in the binder.
  [ ] The household audit is filled in, in pen, at the front.
  [ ] The system notes are written: how the power system starts, where the
      stopcock is, how the filter is backflushed, what the alarms mean.
  [ ] At least two people have read the binder and run a drill from it.
  [ ] The contacts card is filled in, including the out-of-area contact.
  [ ] A relative in another town has a copy.

Write in the binder. Annotate it, correct it, add your own frost dates, your
own well depth, your own meter readings. A marked-up binder is a working
document; a pristine one has never been used.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. Keeping it current
---------------------

On the annual review (Chapter 12 §9):

1. Re-run the household audit — people, medicines and endurance all change.
2. Update the plan with what you learned from the year's drills.
3. make offline to regenerate the HTML and text files.
4. Reprint the chapters that changed and swap the pages in the binder.
5. Refresh both USB sticks onto new sticks; post one to the off-site holder.
6. Re-sync the e-reader.
7. Do the download day (§6).
8. Check the printed cards are still legible and still on the wall.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. Offline checklist
--------------------

  [ ] offline/survival-plan.html opens with the network switched off
  [ ] It prints correctly to A4 (chapters start on new pages)
  [ ] Tier 1 quick-reference cards PRINTED and LAMINATED
  [ ] Cards posted: kitchen, water store, stove, medical kit, toilet
  [ ] Full plan printed and in a labelled ring binder
  [ ] Household audit filled in BY HAND at the front of the binder
  [ ] System notes written (power, water, stove, shut-offs)
  [ ] E-reader or wiped old phone loaded, charged, in the kit
  [ ] Solar panel + power bank that can charge it
  [ ] 2 × USB sticks written; one held off-site by a relative
  [ ] Repository cloned to the family computer so it can be reprinted
  [ ] Reference library downloaded AND the critical parts printed
  [ ] Canning time/pressure tables printed (safety-critical)
  [ ] Paper maps of the region + a compass
  [ ] Two people have read the binder and run a drill from it
  [ ] Annual refresh scheduled in the December review

===========

Construction plans for everything in this survival plan that you can make
yourself. Each build gives dimensions, a materials list, a cut list, step-by-
step assembly, cost, time and the mistakes that ruin it.

Every one of these can be built with hand tools by someone who has never built
anything. Where a build genuinely needs skill or a professional (a wood stove
flue, a mains transfer switch, a structural cellar), it says so plainly.

Before you start: the fundamentals
----------------------------------


The tool kit that builds everything here

Absolute minimum (€120): tape measure · pencil · combination square · handsaw
· hammer · cordless drill/driver + bits · adjustable spanner · utility knife ·
tin snips · 2 clamps · spirit level · safety glasses · work gloves.

Makes it much faster (€200 more): circular saw or mitre saw · jigsaw · impact
driver · staple gun · long spirit level (1.2 m) · workbench or two trestles ·
a chalk line · a spade and a shovel.

Chapter 11 covers the full tool and spares list.


Timber sizes used in these plans

European metric sawn timber. If yours differs, adapt — none of these designs
are fussy about a few millimetres.

Name here  Actual size        Used for
---------  -----------------  ------------------------------------------------
45 × 45    45 × 45 mm (2×2)   Frames, studs, coop structure, small builds
45 × 95    45 × 95 mm (2×4)   Load-bearing frames, bed edges, stands, run
                              posts

70 × 70    70 × 70 mm (3×3)   Legs, posts
22 × 100   22 × 100 mm board  Cladding, bed edges, shelving
Batten     25 × 38 mm         Roofing battens, glazing bars, trim
Sheet      2440 × 1220 mm     OSB3, exterior ply, MDF

Sheet goods: OSB3 (moisture-resistant, cheap, ugly) for anything that will be
painted or covered · exterior/marine plywood for floors, doors and anything
that gets wet or scrubbed · 18 mm for structure, 12 mm for panels, 6 mm for
linings.


Timber preservation and food safety

- Never use CCA-treated, creosoted or old railway-sleeper timber where animals
   live, where food grows, or where water is collected. The preservatives
   leach.
- Modern tanalised/pressure-treated (ACQ, copper azole) timber is generally
   considered safe for raised beds and animal housing, but line the inside
   face of raised beds with plastic sheeting if you want to be conservative.
- Best untreated options that last outdoors: larch, Douglas fir, sweet
   chestnut, oak, western red cedar, black locust. Larch is usually the
   cheapest durable option in Central Europe and needs no treatment at all.
- Raw linseed oil (2–3 coats, thinned) is a non-toxic finish for coops, beds
   and tool handles.
- Keep wood off the ground. A post in soil rots in 5–8 years; the same post on
   a concrete pad or a metal post shoe lasts 25.


Fixings

- Screws, not nails, for almost everything — they can be undone, they hold
   better, and you can build alone. Exterior-grade (coated or stainless)
   outdoors.
- Rule of thumb: the screw should go at least twice as deep into the second
   piece as the first piece is thick.
- Pre-drill near ends to stop splitting (a pilot hole ≈ the screw's core
   diameter).
- Galvanised or stainless outdoors. Plain steel bleeds rust stains within a
   season and fails within five years.
- Structural connections: use a bolt (M8/M10 with washers), not a screw.
- Coach screws for post-to-rail, angle brackets for anything you'd otherwise
   butt-joint badly.


Mesh — the one you must not get wrong

Mesh                      Use                        [!]
------------------------  -------------------------  -------------------------
12.5 mm (½") galvanised   Everything predator-       The correct answer
weld mesh, 19 g           related: coop vents, run
                          walls, aprons, feed
                          stores

25 mm (1") weld mesh      Run tops, upper sections,  Acceptable above 1.2 m
                          rabbit runs

Chicken wire (hexagonal   Keeping chickens in.       [NO] A fox tears through
netting)                  Nothing else.              it and a marten walks
                                                     through it. Never use it
                                                     as predator protection.

Insect mesh (0.6–1 mm)    Brassica cages, window
                          screens

Bird netting              Fruit cages, run tops
                          against hawks

Hardware cloth 6 mm       Rodent-proofing feed
                          stores, vents



Safety

- Eye protection whenever cutting, drilling or hammering. Every time.
- Dust mask (FFP2) when cutting OSB, MDF or treated timber — the dust is
   genuinely harmful.
- Never cut towards yourself. Clamp the work; don't hold it.
- Ladders: 1-in-4 angle, three points of contact, someone footing it.
- Chainsaw: full PPE and training, never alone, never above shoulder height.
- Anything burning: read the CO warning in Chapter 04 §6. It applies to every
   stove in these plans.
- Anything electrical touching house wiring: a qualified electrician. Not you.
- Anything holding water above head height: 1,000 L weighs 1,000 kg. Build the
   stand to hold three times what you think it needs.
- Excavations over 1.2 m deep can collapse and kill. Batter the sides or shore
   them. This is the most dangerous build in this whole folder.


Adapt, scavenge, substitute

Almost everything here can be built from salvage: pallets (heat-treated "HT"
stamped only — never MB/methyl bromide), old windows, scaffold boards, IBC
totes, blue barrels, bread crates, scrap timber, offcuts from a building site,
a demolished shed. The dimensions in these plans are a starting point, not a
law. Build to the materials you can actually get.

Keep every offcut. In a crisis the timber pile is a resource.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Back to: main README


==============================================================================
17. 01 — WATER BUILDS
==============================================================================


01 — Water Builds
=================

Read Chapter 01 first. The three layers are stored water, a source you
control, and treatment. These builds cover all three.

Contents: Bucket gravity filter · Tippy-tap · First-flush diverter · IBC stand
& manifold · Slow sand filter · Solar water heater · Ollas & bucket drip


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. Bucket gravity filter
========================

30 min · €50 · Skill #.. · Build this first. Today if possible.

Two stacked buckets and a hollow-fibre filter give you a continuous, hands-
free supply of safe water at 1–2 litres a minute, with no power and no
pumping. It does the same job as a €300 commercial gravity filter for a sixth
of the price.

          ┌───────────────────┐ ← lid (keeps dust and insects out)
          │  ░░░ RAW WATER ░░ │
          │  ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │  TOP BUCKET — 20 L, drilled for the filter
          └────────╥──────────┘
                   ║ ← Sawyer/hollow-fibre filter, gasket + washer both sides
          ┌────────╨──────────┐
          │                   │
          │   CLEAN WATER     │  BOTTOM BUCKET — 20 L, with a tap 50 mm up
          │                   │
          └──────────────[TAP]┘
               ═══════════════   stand on a stable shelf at working height

Materials

Item                                                   Qty  ~€
-----------------------------------------------------  ---  --
Food-grade 20 L buckets with lids                      2    16
Sawyer-type hollow-fibre filter (0.1 µm) with gaskets  1–2  30
Plastic bucket tap (spigot) + washers                  1    6
Silicone sealant (food-safe)                           —    4

Build

1. Drill a hole in the centre of the base of the top bucket to suit your
    filter's threaded fitting (usually 19–20 mm).
2. Drill a matching hole in the lid of the bottom bucket — or omit the lid and
    let the filter hang straight into the bottom bucket.
3. Fit the filter through both, with a rubber gasket and washer on each side,
    and tighten the nut hand-tight plus a quarter turn. Do not over-tighten —
    cracked plastic is the usual failure.
4. Fit the tap into the bottom bucket, 50 mm up from the base, with washers
    inside and out. The gap leaves any sediment behind.
5. Run a bead of silicone around both fittings; let it cure 24 h.
6. Test with clean water first, and look for drips. Any leak at the filter
    seal means untreated water is bypassing the filter.

Use it

- Two filters double the flow rate. For a family of four, fit two.
- Pre-filter cloudy water through 4–8 layers of cotton, or settle it first —
   silt clogs the fibres fast.
- Backflush weekly with the syringe that comes with the filter, and whenever
   flow drops.
- [!] Filtration is not disinfection. Hollow-fibre removes bacteria and
   protozoa but not viruses. After filtering, add a NaDCC tablet or 2 drops of
   bleach per litre, or boil, if the source was surface water. From your own
   rainwater tank, filter + chlorine residual is the sensible standard.
- [!] Never let the filter freeze. Freezing ruptures the fibres invisibly and
   it will then pass everything without any change you can see. Bring it
   indoors in winter.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. Tippy-tap handwashing station
================================

20 min · €5 · Skill #.. · Highest disease-prevention per euro in the whole
folder

Handwashing with soap cuts diarrhoeal disease by 30–50%. A tippy-tap uses 0.2
litres per wash, and — the important part — you never touch it with dirty
hands.

          ┌───────┐          Two uprights + a crossbar
         ╱│ 5 L   │╲         (or hang it from a branch or a nail)
        ╱ │  jug  │ ╲
       │  └───┬───┘  │        Jug pivots on a string through two
       │      │      │        holes near the neck
       │     ╱ ╲     │
       │    ╱   ╲    │  ← 3 mm hole in the cap or upper shoulder
       │   soap on   │    lets water out ONLY when tilted
       │   a string  │
       │      │      │
       │   ───┴───   │  ← foot lever, string to the jug's handle
      ═╧═════════════╧═
        gravel or a soakaway below, or a bucket to catch greywater

Build

1. Take a clean 5 L jug or bottle with a handle.
2. Drill a 3 mm hole in the cap (or in the shoulder, 30 mm below the neck).
3. Drill two small holes either side of the neck and thread a string through
    as a pivot; hang it from a crossbar so it swings freely.
4. Tie a second string from the handle down to a foot lever (a stick pivoted
    on a peg) or simply a loop you pull with your foot.
5. Hang a bar of soap in a net or on a nail beside it — soap is what does the
    work; running water alone does very little.
6. Put gravel or a bucket underneath. Catch the greywater for the garden.

Site one at the toilet and one at the kitchen. Refill them as a daily job. In
winter, bring the jug indoors at night or it splits.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. First-flush diverter
=======================

1 h · €25 · Skill #.. · Do more for water quality than any filter

The first rain off a roof carries the bird droppings, dust, pollen, moss and
insects that accumulated since the last shower. A first-flush diverter throws
that away and sends only the clean water to the tank.

               roof gutter
                   │
                   ▼
              ┌────┴────┐
              │  tee    ├──────────► to TANK (once the chamber is full)
              └────┬────┘
                   │
              ┌────┴────┐  ← ball floats up as the chamber fills
              │    ●    │     and seals the tee, diverting flow onward
              │         │
              │ 110 mm  │     CHAMBER — volume = roof area × 0.5 L/m²
              │  pipe   │
              │         │     110 mm pipe holds ~8.5 L per metre
              │         │     160 mm pipe holds ~18 L per metre
              └────┬────┘
                ┌──┴──┐  ← end cap with a 3 mm hole:
                │  •  │     drains slowly between rain events so the
                └─────┘     chamber is empty and ready for the next shower

Sizing. Aim for 0.5 litres per m² of roof area:

Roof area  First flush volume  110 mm pipe  160 mm pipe
---------  ------------------  -----------  -----------
40 m²      20 L                2.4 m        1.1 m
60 m²      30 L                3.5 m        1.7 m
100 m²     50 L                5.9 m        2.8 m

| Most DIY diverters are undersized because a 6 m pipe is impractical. An
| undersized diverter is still far better than none — 20 L takes the worst of
| it. Use 160 mm pipe, or split the roof across two downpipes with a diverter
| on each, or accept a smaller chamber and clean your gutters more often.

Materials: 110 or 160 mm soil pipe to the length above · a tee · an end cap ·
2 pipe clips · a floating ball that fits the pipe loosely (a hard plastic
ball, or a sealed empty bottle) · solvent cement or rubber seals.

Build

1. Cut the downpipe and fit the tee so the side outlet goes to the tank and
    the straight run goes down into the chamber.
2. Glue the chamber pipe below the tee, clipped to the wall.
3. Drop the ball in. As the chamber fills, the ball rises and seals the
    opening, so further water passes out of the side outlet to the tank.
4. Fit the end cap and drill a 3 mm hole in it. The chamber empties over a few
    hours, ready for the next shower.
5. Add a leaf screen / 1 mm mesh basket at the gutter outlet above the tee. It
    keeps out leaves, and — critically — mosquitoes and rodents, which is a
    disease issue, not a tidiness one.

Maintenance: clear the mesh monthly and after storms. Flush the chamber and
check the drain hole twice a year. Disconnect and drain it before a hard
frost.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. IBC tank stand and linked manifold
=====================================

4 h · €80 · Skill ##.

[!] A full 1,000 L IBC weighs 1,000 kg. Build the stand for three times that.
A collapsing tank will destroy a wall, a car or a person.

      TWO LINKED IBCs, plan view                SIDE VIEW
     ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐              ┌────────────┐
     │  IBC 1   │   │  IBC 2   │              │    IBC     │
     │  1000 L  │   │  1000 L  │              │            │
     └────╥─────┘   └─────╥────┘              └──╥─────────┘
          ╚════ 50 mm ════╝  ← linked at the      ║ outlet
               manifold        BASE: they fill  ══╬══════  ← 45 × 95 bearers
                               and draw as one   ███  ███  ← concrete blocks
                    │                            ▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓  ← compacted hardcore
                ┌───┴───┐                                     on level ground
                │  TAP  │ ← 500–600 mm above ground
                └───────┘   gives enough head for a hose and to fill a can

Source the tanks carefully. Buy only food-grade IBCs that previously held food
(syrup, juice, glycerine). Ask what was in it and do not accept "not sure". A
tank that held a chemical can never be made safe for drinking water. Rinse
thoroughly before use regardless.

Build the stand

1. Level and compact the ground. Everything depends on this. Dig out 150 mm,
    fill with hardcore/gravel, compact it hard, and check level in both
    directions.
2. Lay concrete blocks on edge (or paving slabs on a full bed) to 400–500 mm
    high, supporting the full perimeter of the IBC pallet base, not just the
    corners.
3. Lay 45 × 95 bearers across the blocks, running the same way as the pallet's
    own bearers, so the load goes straight down into the blocks.
4. Check level again with the tank empty, and again after the first fill.

Link the tanks

5. Connect the bottom outlets together with 50 mm pipe and the standard IBC
    camlock/S60x6 adaptors (cheap, available online). Linked at the base, the
    tanks equalise — they fill and empty together and you draw from one tap.
6. Link the tops too with a short overflow pipe, and take a final overflow
    away from the house foundations to a soakaway, a ditch or a pond.
7. Fit an isolating valve at each tank so you can take one offline to clean
    it.

Finish

8. Block the light — paint the tanks, wrap them in tarpaulin, or build a
    simple timber surround. Sunlight grows algae, and algae fouls filters and
    makes the water taste foul.
9. Fit a tap 100 mm above the tank floor so sediment stays behind, and a
    separate bottom drain to flush the sludge out twice a year.
10. Winter: drain the pipework and the tap, leave the tanks no more than ¾
     full, or lag them. Water expands as it freezes and will split a tank.
     Partially burying a tank, or surrounding it with straw bales, keeps it
     liquid.

Insulating one tank (straw bales or 50 mm foam board in a timber box) means
you still have liquid water in January, which is when you need it most.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Slow sand (biosand) filter
=============================

4 h to build + 2–3 weeks to mature · €40 · Skill ##.

A genuinely effective, permanent, zero-consumable filter. Once the biolayer (a
living film of micro-organisms on the sand surface) has matured, it removes
95–99% of bacteria, over 99% of protozoa, and most turbidity — indefinitely,
with no cartridges to buy. This is a real L3 renewable.

          ┌─────────────────────────┐
          │   ▓▓ diffuser plate ▓▓  │ ← drilled plate, 50 mm above the sand:
          │═════════════════════════│   pouring water straight onto the sand
          │  ~~ 50 mm standing ~~   │   destroys the biolayer
          │  ~~~~~ water ~~~~~~~~~  │
          │▪▪▪▪ BIOLAYER ▪▪▪▪▪▪▪▪▪▪ │ ← the living filter, top 10 mm
          │░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │
          │░ FILTRATION SAND        │   500 mm of 0.15–0.7 mm washed sand
          │░ 500 mm                 │
          │░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │
          │▒▒ coarse sand 50 mm ▒▒▒ │   1–6 mm
          │●● drainage gravel 50 ●● │   6–12 mm
          └──┬──────────────────────┘
             │ outlet pipe rises from the base...
             └────────────┐
                          └──► outlet at the level of the standing water
                               (this is what keeps 50 mm of water permanently
                                above the sand, keeping the biolayer alive)

Materials: a 60–120 L food-grade barrel or a purpose-built concrete body · 20
mm pipe and elbows · washed sand, graded · gravel · a drilled plastic plate
for the diffuser.

Build

1. Fit the outlet pipe: from the very bottom of the barrel, rising outside (or
    inside) to discharge at a height that leaves 50 mm of water standing above
    the sand surface. Getting this height right is the whole design — it is
    what keeps the biolayer permanently wet.
2. Wash the sand until the rinse water runs completely clear. This takes
    longer than you think — an hour of work, several changes of water.
    Unwashed sand clogs the filter and it never recovers. Do not skip it.
3. Layer in: 50 mm of 6–12 mm gravel, 50 mm of 1–6 mm coarse sand, then 500 mm
    of 0.15–0.7 mm filtration sand.
4. Fit the diffuser plate 50 mm above the sand.
5. Charge it daily with water for 2–3 weeks before trusting it. The biolayer
    has to establish. Flow will slow as it matures — that is the filter
    working.

Operate it

- Pour a batch (10–20 L) through, once or twice a day. It is a batch filter,
   not a continuous one. The pause between batches is when the biolayer feeds.
- Flow rate of roughly 0.4–0.6 L/min is right.
- [!] Never let it dry out. A dry biolayer is a dead biolayer and you start
   the three weeks again.
- [!] Never let it freeze.
- When flow drops too far, do the "swirl and dump": stir the top 20 mm of sand
   gently, let the dirt suspend, scoop the dirty water out. Expect 1–2 weeks
   of reduced performance while the biolayer recovers.
- [!] Still disinfect afterwards. A biosand filter is excellent but it is not
   a virus barrier. Chlorinate or boil the output for drinking.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Solar batch water heater
===========================

1 day · €60 · Skill ##. · Free hot water, April–September


6.1 The simple version — solar shower bag
-----------------------------------------

A 20 L black PVC bag (€15) hung on a sunny south wall reaches 40 °C in 3–4
hours on a clear day. Zero build. Buy two. This is the sensible starting point
and it is genuinely enough for washing.


6.2 The batch heater — 100–200 L of hot water
---------------------------------------------

               ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
         glass │  ╔════════════════════════════════╗  │ ← old window or
          lid  │  ║   BLACK-PAINTED TANK / BARREL  ║  │   twin-wall polycarbonate
         (30°) │  ╚════════════════════════════════╝  │
               │  ▓▓▓ 50 mm insulation all round ▓▓▓  │ ← except the glazed face
               └──────────────────────────────────────┘
                cold in at the BOTTOM · hot out at the TOP
                (hot water rises — never plumb it the other way)

Build: a plywood box with 50 mm of insulation on the base and all sides, a
barrel or steel tank painted matt black inside it, and an old double-glazed
window or twin-wall polycarbonate as the lid, tilted to face the sun at about
your latitude. Line the inside of the box with foil or white paint to bounce
light back onto the tank.

Plumb it: cold feed into the bottom, hot draw-off from the top. On a clear
Central European summer day expect 45–60 °C; in April and September, 30–40 °C
— still ample for washing, dishes and laundry.

[!] Drain it completely before the first frost. A frozen batch heater splits
the tank and the pipework. [!] Never seal it as a pressure vessel. Always
leave a vent or an open feed. Heated water expands, and a sealed tank is a
bomb.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Ollas and bucket drip irrigation
===================================

1 h · €15 · Skill #.. · Cuts garden watering by 50–70%


7.1 Ollas — buried clay pots
----------------------------

Ancient, silent, and the most water-efficient irrigation there is. Water seeps
through the unglazed clay directly to the roots as the soil dries, with almost
zero evaporation.

Build: take two unglazed terracotta pots, plug the drainage hole of the lower
one with silicone or a cork, and glue the two rim-to-rim with silicone to make
a sealed vessel with a filling neck at the top. Or simply use a single large
unglazed pot with the hole plugged.

Use: bury to the neck, leaving 30 mm proud, between plants. One 3–4 L olla
waters roughly 1 m². Fill weekly, cover with a stone or a saucer to stop
evaporation and mosquitoes. Lift them before winter or they crack.


7.2 Bucket drip line
--------------------

Build: a 20 L bucket on a stand 500 mm above bed level, with a tap at the base
feeding 16 mm poly tube laid along the bed, with 4 mm drippers or simply a 1
mm hole every 300 mm. Gravity does the rest.

Details: a fine mesh or a piece of cloth over the bucket outlet stops the
holes blocking · run it under mulch for maximum effect · 500 mm of head is
enough for a soaker line but not a sprinkler · connect it to the IBC manifold
above for a garden-wide system. Feeding it with comfrey or nettle tea
(strained!) makes it a fertigation system too.

The rule from Chapter 07: mulch saves more water than any irrigation system.
Do the mulching first, then add drip for what mulch alone cannot cover.

==============================================================================
18. 02 — POWER BUILDS
==============================================================================


02 — Power Builds
=================

Read Chapter 02 first — especially the load audit. Never size a system before
you have measured your loads. These builds assume you have done that and know
your Wh/day figure.

Contents: 12 V solar system · Cable sizing · Fusing · DC lighting circuit ·
Battery box · Panel tilt frame · Generator noise screen

| [!] The line you must not cross. Everything in this file is extra-low-
| voltage DC, isolated from your house wiring. That is legal, safe and DIY-
| able everywhere. Anything that connects to your mains wiring — a transfer
| switch, an interlock, a hybrid inverter feeding house circuits — must be
| done by a qualified electrician. Not because of bureaucracy: a backfed
| generator kills line workers, and a badly earthed inverter kills you.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. The 12 V solar system — complete build
=========================================

1 day · €400–1,200 depending on size · Skill ##.

This is the P1 "Essentials" system from Chapter 02: lights, phones, laptop,
radio, and a fridge if you size it up. It is the system every household should
have, and it is genuinely a one-day job.


1.1 The wiring diagram
----------------------

    ┌────────────┐
    │   PANELS   │  400–600 W, wired in SERIES for MPPT
    │ ▒▒▒▒ ▒▒▒▒  │  (higher voltage = thinner cable, better dull-day harvest)
    └─────╥──────┘
          ║  PV cable (4 or 6 mm², MC4 connectors)
     ┌────╨────┐
     │ PV DC   │ ← isolator switch: turn the array off before touching anything
     │ ISOLATOR│
     └────╥────┘
     ┌────╨─────────┐
     │ MPPT CHARGE  │  30–50 A. Buy a SPARE — this is the most likely failure.
     │  CONTROLLER  │
     └──╥────────╥──┘
        ║        ║
        ║   ┌────╨─────────────────────────────────┐
        ║   │  ⚡ MAIN FUSE — within 300 mm of the │
        ║   │     battery POSITIVE terminal        │
        ║   └────╥─────────────────────────────────┘
        ║   ┌────╨────┐
        ╚═══╡ BATTERY │  100–200 Ah LiFePO4 (1.2–2.4 kWh)
            │  12 V   │  with an integrated BMS
            └────╥────┘
         ┌───────╨────────┐
         │  BATTERY       │ ← main isolator switch you can reach IN THE DARK
         │  ISOLATOR      │
         └───┬────────┬───┘
             │        │
      ┌──────┴──┐  ┌──┴──────────┐
      │ DC FUSE │  │ ⚡fuse      │
      │  BLOCK  │  │  INVERTER   │ 1000–1500 W pure sine
      └──┬───┬──┘  │  (switch it │ [!] modified sine damages motors,
         │   │     │   OFF when  │    chargers and medical devices
         │   │     │   not used) │
         │   │     └──────╥──────┘
         │   │            ║
     lights USB      230 V AC sockets (STANDALONE — not wired to the house)


1.2 Parts list (a 600 W / 1.2 kWh example)
------------------------------------------

Item                                  Spec                             ~€
------------------------------------  -------------------------------  -------
Solar panels                          2 × 300 W mono, wired in series  220
Mounting rails / frame                see §4                           80
PV cable + MC4 connectors             6 mm², 20 m + 4 pairs            45
MPPT charge controller                30 A, 12/24 V auto               110
Spare MPPT controller                 same                             110
PV DC isolator                        600 V, 25 A                      20
LiFePO4 battery                       12 V 100 Ah with BMS             320
Main fuse + holder                    125 A Class T or MEGA            25
Battery isolator switch               300 A marine type                20
Battery cable                         25 mm², lugs, heat shrink        40
DC fuse block                         6-way, blade fuses               15
Pure sine inverter                    1000 W, 12 V                     130
Cable, crimps, glands, trunking,      —                                60
labels

Battery monitor (shunt-based)         optional but excellent           60
Total                                                                  ~€1,255

Halve this for a 300 W / 50 Ah starter system (~€500), or scale up to 24 V for
anything over 1.5 kW of load.


1.3 Cable sizing — the table that prevents fires
------------------------------------------------

Undersized DC cable is the classic off-grid fire cause. At 12 V the currents
are large and the voltage drop is brutal. Size for both voltage drop and
current-carrying capacity, and use whichever is bigger.

Minimum cable size (mm²) for 12 V, 3% voltage drop, for the ONE-WAY run
length:

Current  1 m  2 m  3 m  5 m  8 m
-------  ---  ---  ---  ---  ---
5 A      1.5  1.5  1.5  2.5  4
10 A     1.5  2.5  4    6    10
20 A     2.5  4    6    10   16
30 A     4    6    10   16   25
50 A     10   10   16   25   50
100 A    25   25   35   50   95

(Values in bold are set by ampacity, not voltage drop.)

Rules:

- At 24 V you can use half the area for the same power; at 48 V, a quarter.
   This is the main reason to go to a higher voltage as systems grow.
- Keep the battery-to-inverter run as short as physically possible.  Under 1.5
   m, ideally under 1 m.
- Use flexible fine-strand cable (welding or marine cable), not solid house
   wire — it survives vibration and tight bends.
- Crimp lugs properly with a proper crimping tool, then heat-shrink. A bad
   crimp is a resistance heater. Do not twist-and-tape anything on the DC
   side.
- Colour code red positive / black negative and label every cable at both
   ends.


1.4 Fusing — non-negotiable
---------------------------

A fuse protects the CABLE, not the device. Its job is to blow before the wire
melts.

Location                  Fuse                       Why
------------------------  -------------------------  -------------------------
Within 300 mm of the      100–150 A Class T or MEGA  [!] The most important
battery positive                                     fuse in the system. A
                                                     shorted battery cable
                                                     delivers thousands of
                                                     amps and will start a
                                                     fire in seconds. Class T
                                                     has the high interrupt
                                                     rating lithium needs.

Inverter feed             Sized to the inverter      1000 W ÷ (12 V × 0.85) ≈
                                                     98 A → 125 A fuse, 25 mm²
                                                     cable

Each DC branch circuit    5–20 A blade fuse          Lights, USB, pumps,
                                                     fridge

PV array to controller    DC isolator +              Fit the isolator so you
                          controller's own           can safely work on the
                          protection                 system


Sizing a fuse: rate it at roughly 125% of the expected continuous current, and
never above the cable's ampacity.


1.5 Build sequence
------------------

1. Plan the layout on paper first. Battery, controller and inverter close
    together; panels as far away as necessary. Draw it, label it, keep the
    drawing.
2. Mount the panels (§4). Wire them in series for an MPPT controller — higher
    voltage means thinner cable and better output in dull conditions. Check
    the controller's maximum PV voltage and remember open-circuit voltage
    rises in the cold: allow 25% headroom.
3. Run the PV cable into the building through a proper gland or a drip loop,
    with the DC isolator at the entry point.
4. Mount the controller on a non-combustible surface with 100 mm of clear air
    all round (they get hot).
5. Connect in this order — it matters: `` BATTERY first  →  then the
    CONTROLLER  →  then the PANELS  →  then the LOADS `` Most MPPT controllers
    need to see the battery voltage first to detect the system voltage.
    Connecting panels first can damage them.
6. Fit the main fuse and the isolator as you connect the battery, not
    afterwards.
7. Set the controller to the right battery chemistry — LiFePO4, not "lithium"
    generically, and definitely not the lead-acid default. Wrong charge
    profile is the fastest way to kill a €320 battery.
8. Wire the DC fuse block off the isolator, and run the lighting and USB
    circuits from it (§2).
9. Mount the inverter on its own short heavy cable with its own fuse. Put it
    on a switched circuit — idle draw of 10–30 W is 240–700 Wh/day wasted on
    nothing.
10. Label everything, pin the diagram on the wall beside the equipment, and
     write the operating notes — how to check state of charge, what the alarms
     mean, what to do if it stops. Chapter 11's single-point-of-failure rule
     applies: someone else must be able to run this.
11. Test under load. Run the fridge off it for 48 hours and watch the numbers
     before you trust it.


1.6 Common mistakes
-------------------

  [NO] Cable too thin                     → voltage drop, heat, fire
  [NO] No main battery fuse               → the one that burns houses down
  [NO] Modified sine inverter             → damages motors, chargers, medical devices
  [NO] Inverter left on 24/7              → 240–700 Wh/day of pure waste
  [NO] Battery in an unheated garage      → LiFePO4 refuses to charge below 0 °C
  [NO] Controller set to the wrong chemistry → a dead battery in months
  [NO] Sized on annual average sun        → works in June, fails in December
  [NO] Shading "only a little"            → one shaded cell can halve a string
  [NO] No spare charge controller         → €60 part strands a €1,200 system
  [NO] Only one person understands it     → they get ill, the house goes dark


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. 12 V DC lighting and USB circuit
===================================

2 h · €40 · Skill #.. · Do this even on the smallest system

Running lights and charging directly from the battery at 12 V avoids the
inverter entirely — which saves the 10–15% conversion loss and, far more
importantly, lets you switch the inverter off and stop its idle drain.

Materials: 12 V LED strip (warm white, 4.8–9 W/m) or 12 V LED bulbs with
fittings · 12 V USB charging sockets (dual, 3 A) · 12 V cigarette sockets for
accessories · switches · 1.5–2.5 mm² twin cable · blade fuses.

Build

1. Run a 2.5 mm² pair from the DC fuse block to each room.
2. Lights: LED strip along a ceiling cornice or under a shelf, on a switch. 5
    W lights a room adequately; 10 W lights it well.
3. USB points: one per room, plus two at the "charging station" where phones,
    headlamps, radios and battery packs live.
4. Fuse each circuit at the block (5 A for lights, 10 A for USB/accessories).
5. Keep polarity consistent everywhere — reversed polarity destroys most 12 V
    devices instantly.

Energy reality: a whole house on 12 V LED is 30–60 Wh per evening. Your phone
is 15 Wh. You can light and communicate for weeks on a 1 kWh battery. This is
why the P0 tier removes the panic from a blackout for €200.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Insulated battery box
========================

2 h · €50 · Skill #..

LiFePO4 must not be charged below 0 °C — the BMS will simply refuse, and you
will think the system is broken. In a Central European winter an outbuilding
regularly sits below freezing.

          ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ ░░░ 50 mm insulation, lid ░░░░░░░░░ │
          ├─────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ ░│                             │░   │
          │ ░│      BATTERY                │░   │  Cable exits through a
          │ ░│                             │░   │  sealed gland, LOW down
          │ ░│                             │░─────►
          │ ░└─────────────────────────────┘░   │
          │ ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │
          └─────────────────────────────────────┘
            18 mm ply box · 50 mm PIR/foam lining · battery strapped down

Build: an 18 mm ply box, 50 mm larger than the battery all round, lined with
50 mm PIR foam board (taped joints), with a lifting lid. Strap the battery
down so it cannot move. Take the cable out through a cable gland at low level
and seal it.

Details:

- LiFePO4: seal it up. The battery's own charge/discharge losses keep it a few
   degrees above ambient inside an insulated box. Add a thermostatically
   controlled 10–20 W heat pad for a cold shed (that is 240–480 Wh/day —
   budget for it, or just keep the battery indoors, which is free).
- [!] Lead-acid: NEVER seal it. Flooded and AGM batteries vent hydrogen, which
   is explosive. Vent the box to the outside at the top, and keep all sparks
   and switches outside the box.
- Put a thermometer inside and check it on the coldest nights.
- Best answer of all: put the battery inside the heated envelope of the house.
   LiFePO4 is safe indoors and it needs no ventilation. This costs nothing.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Adjustable panel tilt frame
==============================

4 h · €80 · Skill ##. · Gains 20–30% in December

At 46 °N, panels at a fixed 35° lose a lot in winter. A frame you can re-tilt
twice a year to latitude + 15° in winter (≈ 60°) and latitude − 10° in summer
(≈ 35°) gains 20–30% in the months that matter — and a steep winter angle
sheds snow, which is worth more than the angle itself.

      SUMMER ~35°                  WINTER ~60°
           ╱▒▒▒▒▒▒▒                    ╱▒
         ╱▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒                   ╱▒▒
       ╱▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒                   ╱▒▒▒
     ╱─────.────                 ╱──.         . = pivot bolt (M10) at the
    ╱  strut in   ╲             ╱ strut in        bottom rail
   ╱   hole A      ╲           ╱  hole B
  ═╧════════════════╧═════════╧═══════════   base frame, weighted or anchored

Materials: 45 × 95 treated timber or 40 × 40 steel angle · M10 bolts and
washers · panel clamps or 45 × 45 rails · ground anchors, or concrete blocks
as ballast.

Build: a rectangular base frame sized to the panels · a hinged panel frame
bolted to the base at the front edge with M10 pivot bolts · a rear strut with
two or three drilled holes for the seasonal angles.

Critical details:

- [!] Wind load is the killer. A 2 m² panel at 60° in an 80 km/h gust carries
   a load of several hundred kilos. Anchor the frame to the ground or ballast
   it with 80–150 kg of concrete blocks. Panels that fly are lethal and they
   take the roof with them.
- Keep the bottom edge at least 300 mm above ground/snow level or winter
   drifts bury the array exactly when you need it.
- Leave 100 mm of air behind the panels — they lose about 0.4%/°C of output as
   they heat, so ventilation matters in summer.
- Survey the winter sun path before siting. The sun is very low from November
   to February; trees and buildings that cast no shadow in June will shade you
   completely in December.
- Ground mounting beats roof mounting for anyone who can: you can clean it,
   clear snow, re-tilt it, and repair it without a ladder.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Generator noise screen
=========================

4 h · €100 · Skill ##.

A generator is audible for hundreds of metres and announces that your house
has fuel and things worth powering (Chapter 10). You can cut that
substantially — but not by putting it in a box.

| [!][!] NEVER enclose a running generator. Enclosure causes overheating,
| engine fire, and — above all — carbon monoxide accumulation that kills.
| Generators need enormous airflow for cooling and combustion. Every year
| people die building "generator sheds". Do not build one.

What actually works, safely:

1. Distance and position first. Every doubling of distance cuts perceived
    noise. Site it ≥ 5 m from any door, window or vent, downwind, with the
    exhaust pointed away from the house and away from neighbours.
2. A three-sided screen, open at the top and open at the exhaust end. A mass
    barrier between the generator and the listener: 18 mm ply or two layers of
    board, with a dense core (mineral wool, or a rubber/mass-loaded vinyl
    layer). Mass blocks sound; foam does not.
   - Three panels, 1.2 m high, forming a U around the sides and back
   - Completely open above and at the front for airflow
   - At least 1 m of clear space on all open sides
3. Line the inside faces of the screen with 50 mm mineral wool behind a
    perforated board — this absorbs the reflections rather than bouncing them.
4. Put it on a rubber mat or paving slabs on sand, not on a hollow wooden deck
    (which acts as a soundboard) and not on bare resonant ground.
5. Exhaust extension: a flexible steel exhaust pipe routed away from the house
    and screened separately makes a big difference — most of the noise is
    exhaust. [!] Keep it clear of anything combustible; it glows.
6. The bigger win is operational: run it in batch-charging mode (Chapter 02
    §5) — 1–2 hours in daylight, at 60–80% load, while other noise exists,
    instead of all day. That cuts noise exposure, fuel and engine wear
    together.

CO rules, every time, no exceptions:

  [!] Outdoors only. Never in a garage, shed, porch, cellar or under an overhang.
  [!] ≥ 5 m from any opening, exhaust pointing away.
  [!] CO alarms in every sleeping room and on the nearest wall.
  [!] Never refuel a hot engine.
  [!] Never backfeed into a socket — use a transfer switch fitted by an electrician.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Related: Chapter 02 — Electricity · Apartment builds · Build index


==============================================================================
19. 03 — COOKING & HEAT BUILDS
==============================================================================


03 — Cooking & Heat Builds
==========================

Read Chapter 04 first, and read §6 on carbon monoxide before you light
anything. Every stove here is outdoor or properly flued only.

Contents: Brick rocket stove · Tin-can rocket stove · Haybox / thermal cooker
· Solar box oven · Solar dehydrator · Wood store · Warm-room tent


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. 16-brick rocket stove
========================

20 minutes · €15 · Skill #.. · Outdoors only

The highest fuel-efficiency-per-euro object in this entire folder. It boils 5
litres of water on a double handful of twigs, burns almost smokelessly, and
costs the price of sixteen bricks. Every household with any outdoor space
should have one.

              SECTION — the L-shaped rocket
                       ┌───┐
                       │ ▲ │  ← pot sits on the top, on 3 spacers,
                       │ ▲ │     leaving a gap for exhaust all round
          ┌────────────┤ ▲ ├─────┐
          │            │ ▲ │     │   RISER — the chimney.
          │            │ ▲ │     │   Height = 2–3 × the feed opening.
          │            │ ▲ │     │   This is the engine of the stove:
          │   ┌────────┘ ▲ └──┐  │   hot gas rising pulls air in hard,
          │   │  ═══════════  │  │   which is why it burns so cleanly.
        ══╪═══╡ FUEL SHELF    │  │
          │   │  ↑ air under  │  │ ← air MUST flow UNDER the sticks.
          └───┴───────────────┴──┘   Remove the shelf and it becomes a
                FEED                 smoky, useless fire.

The three rules that make it work:

1. Equal cross-section throughout. Feed opening, burn tunnel and riser should
    all be about the same area — roughly 90 × 90 mm for a cooking stove. A
    pinch anywhere kills the draw.
2. Riser height = 2–3 × the feed opening height. The taller the riser, the
    harder it pulls.
3. A fuel shelf, so primary air flows under the burning sticks. This one
    detail is the difference between a rocket stove and a smoky hole.

Materials: 16–20 standard bricks (firebricks are better and last far longer;
ordinary bricks will spall and crack after a season or two of hard use) · 3
small metal spacers or a cast-iron trivet · optionally wood ash, perlite or
vermiculite to insulate around the riser.

Build

1. Lay 4 bricks as a base pad on level ground.
2. Build an L-shaped channel: a horizontal feed tunnel about 2 bricks long,
    turning up into a vertical riser 3–4 bricks tall. Dry-stack, no mortar —
    so you can adjust it and take it apart.
3. Slide a flat brick or a piece of steel plate into the feed tunnel as the
    fuel shelf, leaving an air gap of 25–30 mm beneath it.
4. Pack wood ash, perlite or vermiculite into the gaps around the riser if you
    have a double-skin design. Insulation makes the riser hotter, which makes
    it burn cleaner and hotter still.
5. Put 3 spacers on top so the pot sits 20–25 mm above the riser, letting
    exhaust escape all round. Sitting the pot flat on the riser smothers it.

Use it: feed small, dry sticks (finger-thick) in horizontally, pushing them
forward as they burn. Only the tips burn — this is what makes it so efficient.
Use a windshield. Expect to boil 5 L in 10–15 minutes on about 300 g of wood.

[!] Outdoors or in an open-sided shelter only. It produces CO like any fire.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. Tin-can rocket stove
=======================

45 min · €0–5 · Skill #.. · Portable, outdoors only

The same principle in scrap steel. Genuinely useful, and buildable from a
recycling bin.

Materials: one large can (4–5 L catering size) · one medium can (~1 L) for the
riser · one small can (soup size) for the feed · wood ash, perlite,
vermiculite or dry sand for insulation · tin snips · gloves.

Build

1. Cut a hole in the side of the large can, near the bottom, the diameter of
    the small can.
2. Cut a matching hole in the side of the medium can (the riser), near its
    bottom.
3. Push the small can through the large can's hole into the medium can's hole,
    forming an L. Seal the joints with foil or tuck the cut tabs in.
4. Stand the riser upright inside the large can and pack the void between them
    with ash, perlite or vermiculite. Insulation is not optional — it is what
    makes it burn hot and clean.
5. Cut a small rectangle of steel as a fuel shelf for the feed tube.
6. Make a pot stand from three bent tabs or a wire trivet, giving the same
    20–25 mm exhaust gap.

Lasts 1–3 seasons before the steel burns through. Build two.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Haybox / thermal cooker
==========================

1 h · €0–20 · Skill #.. · Cuts cooking fuel by 50–80%

If you build one thing from this folder, consider this one. Bring food to the
boil for 10–15 minutes, then seal it in heavy insulation. It keeps cooking on
stored heat for 3–6 hours with zero further fuel. Beans, grains, stews, soups,
stocks, porridge — everything that needs a long simmer, which in a storage-
food diet is nearly everything.

          ┌───────────────────────────────────┐
          │ ░░░░ insulated lid / cushion ░░░░ │ ← must press down onto the pot
          ├───────────────────────────────────┤
          │ ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │
          │ ░░░  ┌───────────────────┐   ░░░  │
          │ ░░░  │  POT — lid on,    │   ░░░  │   100 mm+ of insulation
          │ ░░░  │  FULL, straight   │   ░░░  │   on ALL SIDES and
          │ ░░░  │  from a hard boil │   ░░░  │   underneath
          │ ░░░  └───────────────────┘   ░░░  │
          │ ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │
          └───────────────────────────────────┘

Build: a wooden box, a cardboard box, a cool-box, or a laundry basket — at
least 100 mm larger than the pot on every side and underneath. Pack the void
with straw, hay, wool, shredded paper, polystyrene, old duvets, blankets or
sleeping bags. Make a thick insulated cushion for the top that presses down
onto the pot lid. Line it with foil or an old emergency blanket for a few
extra percent.

Use it

1. Bring the food to a hard rolling boil and hold it there 10–15 minutes (20
    for dry beans, 5 for rice). The pot must be genuinely hot all through.
2. Lid on, transfer immediately — every minute on the counter is heat lost.
3. Pack it in, cushion on top, close.
4. Do not open it. Every peek costs an hour of cooking.
5. Leave 3–6 hours. Dry beans that need 2 hours of simmering need 15 minutes
    of boil plus 4 hours in the box.

Why it matters: it turns your cooking fuel store into 2–4× as much cooking, it
works with any stove, it cannot burn food, and it keeps food hot for serving.
Combined with a pressure cooker (which cuts the boil phase too) it is the
biggest fuel saving available to any household.

[!] Food safety: the food must go in boiling hot and stay above 60 °C. A well-
built box holds a full pot above 70 °C for 5–6 hours. A half-empty pot in a
thin box does not — use a full pot (thermal mass is the point) and don't
stretch it past 6 hours.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Solar box oven
=================

4 h · €25 · Skill #.. · Free cooking, April–September

Reaches 120–150 °C on a clear Central European summer day — enough to bake
bread, cook rice, stew vegetables, and pasteurise water. No fuel, no smoke, no
CO, no noise, and it cannot burn the house down.

                reflector lid (foil-covered, propped to bounce
               ╱  extra sun in — roughly doubles the temperature)
              ╱
           ┌─╱──────────────────────────┐
           │╱  GLASS or twin-wall lid   │ ← sealed, this is the greenhouse
           ├────────────────────────────┤
           │ ░░░ 50 mm insulation ░░░░  │
           │ ░ ┌──────────────────┐ ░   │
           │ ░ │ ▓▓ BLACK ▓▓▓▓▓▓  │ ░   │ ← black interior + a black, lidded
           │ ░ │ ▓▓ interior ▓▓▓  │ ░   │   cast-iron or dark enamel pot
           │ ░ └──────────────────┘ ░   │
           │ ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │
           └────────────────────────────┘

Materials: two cardboard boxes (one 50 mm smaller all round) or a plywood box
· 50 mm of insulation (crumpled newspaper, wool, straw, foam board) ·
aluminium foil · black matt paint or black card · a sheet of glass, an old
double-glazed unit, or twin-wall polycarbonate · black cooking pots with lids
· a thermometer.

Build

1. Nest the small box inside the large one, packing insulation between them.
2. Line the inside of the inner box matt black (paint or black card) — black
    absorbs, shiny reflects. The interior must be black; only the reflector is
    shiny.
3. Make a well-fitting glazed lid. Seal it — the trapped air is what makes it
    work. A double-glazed unit is far better than a single sheet.
4. Add a hinged reflector flap lined with foil, propped at about 30° from the
    glass, to bounce extra sunlight in. This roughly doubles the temperature
    rise.
5. Put the thermometer inside where you can read it through the glass.

Use it: start 30–60 minutes before you need to cook (it needs to warm up) ·
re-aim it at the sun every hour · use dark, lidded pots · cooking takes
roughly twice as long as on a stove, and food cannot burn or dry out, so
timing is forgiving. It works from roughly March to October in Central Europe
and not at all in winter — so it is a summer fuel-saver and a preserving-
season workhorse, not a year-round solution.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Solar food dehydrator
========================

1 day · €50 · Skill ##. · Preserve a glut with no jars and no power

An indirect dehydrator — the sun heats air in a collector, and that hot air
flows up through the food. Better than drying in direct sun: no UV damage,
better colour and vitamin retention, and insects are kept out.

                                ┌────┐ ← chimney vent (hot air out;
                                │ ↑↑ │    this is what drives the airflow)
          ┌──────────────────┐  │    │
          │ ▤▤▤ TRAY 4 ▤▤▤▤▤ │  │    │
          │ ▤▤▤ TRAY 3 ▤▤▤▤▤ │  │    │   DRYING CHAMBER
          │ ▤▤▤ TRAY 2 ▤▤▤▤▤ │  │    │   mesh trays, 100 mm apart
          │ ▤▤▤ TRAY 1 ▤▤▤▤▤ │  │    │   shaded, insect-screened
          └────────▲─────────┘  └────┘
                   │ warm air rises
          ┌────────┴─────────────────┐
         ╱  glazed COLLECTOR, black   ╲  ← 1 m² of glazed black-painted
        ╱   inside, 30–45° to the sun  ╲    box; cool air enters at the
       ╱  ←←← cool air in (screened)    ╲   bottom, heats, and rises
      └──────────────────────────────────┘

Materials: plywood or salvaged timber · glass or twin-wall polycarbonate for
the collector · matt black paint · food-safe mesh (stainless, nylon or food-
grade plastic — not galvanised, zinc migrates into acidic food) for the trays
· insect screen · a small chimney.

Build

1. Collector: a shallow box about 1 m × 0.6 m, painted matt black inside,
    glazed on top, angled 30–45° south. Air inlet at the low end, screened.
2. Chamber: an insulated, shaded box above the collector's high end, with
    runners for 4–6 mesh trays at 100 mm spacing, and a door.
3. Chimney: a vent at the top of the chamber creates the stack effect that
    pulls air through. Screen it.
4. Everything screened against flies and wasps, which will otherwise find it
    within an hour.

Use it: slice thinly and evenly (3–6 mm) · a single layer per tray, not
touching · rotate the trays through the day since the bottom dries fastest ·
bring it all indoors at night, or moisture re-absorbs · dry until cracker-
crisp — anything leathery will mould in storage. Then condition it in a jar
for a week, shaking daily, and watch for condensation before packing for the
long term.

In a Central European summer: fruit and tomatoes take 1–3 days, herbs a few
hours, mushrooms a day. An electric dehydrator (300–500 W) run off surplus
midday solar is the weather-independent complement.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Wood store
=============

1 day · €80 · Skill ##. · Holds ~4 m³ — one warm-room winter

Seasoning is the whole point. Fresh wood is 45–60% water, gives half the heat,
and coats your chimney in creosote. A proper store gets wood to under 20%.

                ← roof slopes back, 200 mm overhang all round
          ╱▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔╲
         │  ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │
         │  ░░░ THIS YEAR'S ░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │  3000 wide
         │  ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │  × 1000 deep
         │  ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │  × 1800 high
         │  ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │
         ═╪═══════════════════════════════╪═ ← bearers: wood 150 mm OFF the ground
          │                               │
        OPEN SIDES — wind must blow through. A closed store is a mould farm.

Materials: 6 × 70 × 70 posts (2400 mm) · 45 × 95 rails and bearers ·
corrugated roof sheet · 45 × 45 battens · post shoes or gravel · screws.

Build

1. Posts at 1.5 m centres along a 3 m × 1 m footprint, in post shoes or on
    gravel — never concreted straight into soil, they rot in 5 years.
2. Rails front and back, roof slope 10° to the back.
3. Bearers 150 mm above the ground, running front-to-back so air passes under
    the stack. Pallets work perfectly.
4. Roof with 200 mm of overhang on every side. Corrugated sheet on battens.
5. Leave the sides completely open. Cover the top only. Airflow through the
    stack is what dries it; wrapping a stack in tarpaulin traps moisture and
    grows fungus.
6. Site it in the sun and the prevailing wind, not tucked against a shaded
    north wall.

Stack it properly: split before stacking (split wood dries 4× faster than
round) · bark side up in the open, bark side down under cover · cross-stack
the ends for stability · leave finger gaps between rows.

Run two stores, or one store divided: this winter's seasoned wood and next
winter's drying wood. Hardwood needs 18–24 months, softwood 9–12. Buy a €15
moisture meter and actually measure a freshly split face — under 20% or it
does not go in the stove.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Warm-room tent
=================

1 h · €0–120 · Skill #.. · [FLAT] the apartment heating answer

When you cannot heat a room, heat a volume. An ordinary 3–4 person dome tent
pitched inside a living room, or a canopy over a bed, traps body heat in a
small space. It sounds absurd and it works extremely well: a tent with two
people in it sits 10–15 °C above the room, with no fuel at all.

Options, cheapest first:

1. A four-poster bed canopy: heavy blankets or a duvet draped over a frame of
    canes or a clothes airer around the bed. Free.
2. An inner tent pitched in the room. €60–120 for a used dome tent. Add a
    groundsheet, a foam camping mat and a rug underneath — the floor is where
    most of the heat goes.
3. A room-in-a-room: curtains or blankets hung to enclose the smallest corner
    of the room, ideally one with an internal wall on two sides.

Details:

- Insulate underneath. A mattress or two camping mats on the floor matters
   more than the walls.
- Vent it. A sealed tent with two people in it condenses heavily. Leave the
   top vent open — you will lose a little heat and gain dry bedding.
- Hot water bottles at the foot of each bed, refilled from the pot on the
   stove. A body in a good sleeping bag with a hot water bottle needs no room
   heating at all, down to well below freezing.
- [!] Never put any flame, catalytic heater or stove inside a tent or canopy.
   Ever. This is the single most reliable way to die of carbon monoxide.
- Keep the rest of the flat above 5 °C to protect the plumbing, and drain the
   pipes if you cannot.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Related: Chapter 04 — Heating, cooking & fuel · Food storage builds · Build
index


==============================================================================
20. 04 — SANITATION BUILDS
==============================================================================


04 — Sanitation Builds
======================

Read Chapter 05 first. This is the category that historically kills more
people than any other in a crisis, and these are the cheapest, simplest builds
in the folder.

Contents: Bucket toilet · Urine diverter · Humanure compost bays · Handwash
station · Plunger washing machine · Greywater mulch basin · Trench latrine


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. Bucket toilet (sawdust toilet)
=================================

2 h · €40 · Skill #.. · Build this in your first weekend

Odour-free, fly-free, usable indoors, and it works whether you are in a
farmhouse or on the ninth floor. Done properly it smells of nothing but
sawdust. Done badly it is unbearable — and the difference is one habit.

          ┌─────────────────────────────┐
          │ ╔═══════════════════════╗   │ ← standard toilet seat + LID,
          │ ║      SEAT OPENING     ║   │   screwed to the top
          │ ╚═══════════════════════╝   │
          ├─────────────────────────────┤
          │      ┌───────────────┐      │  Box: 500 W × 450 D × 430 H
          │      │               │      │  (430 = normal seat height)
          │      │    20 L       │      │
          │      │   BUCKET      │      │  Front or back panel hinged/lift-off
          │      │               │      │  so the bucket lifts straight out
          │      └───────────────┘      │
          └─────────────────────────────┘
                beside it: a bin of COVER MATERIAL and a scoop

Materials

Item                                                    Qty  ~€
------------------------------------------------------  ---  --
18 mm plywood or OSB (one half-sheet)                   —    20
45 × 45 corner battens                                  3 m  5
Standard toilet seat with lid                           1    15
20 L buckets (at least 2 — one in use, one in reserve)  2–4  16
Screws, hinges or a turn-button                         —    5
Cover material bin + scoop                              1    10

Build
