1. Cut the ply: top 500 × 450 · two sides 450 × 430 · back 500 × 430 · front
    500 × 430 (make the front lift off or hinge — this is how you change the
    bucket).
2. Batten the corners inside and screw the box together.
3. Measure your actual bucket rim and cut the hole in the top so the bucket
    rim sits just under the seat, catching everything. A hole that is too big
    lets things miss the bucket; too small and the seat overhangs. Cut it 10
    mm smaller than the bucket rim diameter.
4. Screw the toilet seat over the hole.
5. Sand, and finish with paint or oil so it wipes clean.
6. Put a large bin of cover material and a scoop right beside it. If the cover
    material is not within arm's reach, people will not use it, and then it
    stinks.

The method — this is the whole thing

  1. Start every empty bucket with 30–50 mm of cover material in the bottom.
  2. Use it normally. Urine and faeces together is fine (but see §2).
  3.  COVER COMPLETELY AFTER EVERY SINGLE USE
     A generous scoop, until nothing is visible. This is the entire trick.
     A covered deposit does not smell and does not attract flies.
  4. Lid down between uses.
  5. Swap the bucket at two-thirds full. Never carry a full one.
  6. Rinse the empty bucket, tip the rinse water on the compost pile, air it.

Cover material — you need a lot, so plan it: sawdust or shavings (untreated
wood only) · coconut coir bricks (one 5 kg brick expands to ~70 L — the
apartment answer, compact to store) · shredded dry leaves · shredded paper and
card · peat-free compost · chopped straw · dry soil · rice hulls. Budget 1–2 L
per person per day → ~150–250 L per person per three months.

[!] Never use cat litter (clay clumps and won't compost), and never use
sawdust from treated, painted or MDF timber.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. Urine diverter
=================

1 h · €0–80 · Skill #.. · The upgrade that changes everything

Urine is ~90% of the volume and almost all of the smell once mixed with
faeces. Separating it means the solids bucket fills five times more slowly,
barely smells at all, needs far less cover material, and gives you a free
nitrogen fertiliser.

Three ways, cheapest first:

1. A dedicated bottle or jug for men and children, used before sitting. Free,
    and it captures most of the volume immediately.
2. A funnel and tube fitted at the front of the seat opening, draining to a 5
    L jerry can beside the box. €10 of parts.
3. A commercial urine-diverting seat or insert (€40–80). The comfortable,
    reliable option, and worth it if this is going to run for months.

What to do with the urine

- Dilute 1:8 to 1:10 with water and pour onto soil around growing plants. Not
   on leaves, not on anything about to be eaten raw, and stop 3–4 weeks before
   harvest.
- One adult's annual urine contains roughly enough nitrogen for 200–300 m² of
   vegetable garden — a serious free input when fertiliser cannot be bought.
- [FLAT] In a flat: dilute and pour down a still-working drain, or onto
   balcony pots and the trees below (ask first). Undiluted urine burns plants
   and stinks within hours — dilute it and use it the same day.
- Don't use urine from anyone on medication you would rather not concentrate
   in your soil.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Two-bay humanure compost system
==================================

4 h · €40 (free from pallets) · Skill #.. · [LAND] land only

        ┌─────────────┬─────────────┐
        │             │             │   Two bays, 1 m × 1 m × 1 m each
        │   BAY A     │   BAY B     │
        │  FILLING    │  RESTING    │   Fill A for ONE YEAR.
        │  this year  │  (sealed,   │   Then seal it and fill B for a year.
        │             │   year 2)   │   Two years total before use.
        │             │             │
        └─────────────┴─────────────┘   Front boards slide out for access
         ▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓
         300 mm base of straw / wood chips to absorb all liquid

Siting — get this right first

- At least 30 m from any well, spring, stream or watercourse, and downhill
   from your water source. Non-negotiable.
- Out of sight, downwind of the house, but close enough that you will actually
   walk there twice a day in February.
- On soil, never on concrete — the soil biology and the drainage are part of
   the process.

Build: four pallets (or 22 × 100 boards on 70 × 70 posts) forming two
adjoining 1 m³ bays, with removable front boards sliding in grooves. Line the
inside with cardboard if the pallet gaps are large. A simple lid or a sheet of
corrugated tin keeps the rain off (but it must not be sealed — this is an
aerobic process and it needs air).

Operate it

1. Start with a 300 mm base of straw or wood chips in the bay. This is the
    "biological sponge" — it absorbs every drop of liquid so nothing leaches
    out.
2. Empty the bucket into the centre of the pile, then immediately cover it
    completely with straw, leaves or chips. Never leave deposited material
    uncovered, even for an hour.
3. Add kitchen waste, garden waste, weeds and animal bedding to the same pile
    — they add carbon, nitrogen and biological diversity and help it heat.
4. Keep the sides and top well covered with a thick carbon layer. A correctly
    managed humanure pile has no odour and no flies. If it smells, add more
    cover.
5. Fill one bay for a full year. Then seal it and leave it completely
    undisturbed for a second full year while you fill the other.
6. A hot pile reaching 50–60 °C kills pathogens in days, but a pile built from
    small daily additions in cold weather often will not get there. The long
    rest is the real safety margin, not the temperature. Do not shorten it.

Using the finished compost

- [YES] Fruit trees, berry bushes, ornamentals, hedges, grain and fodder
   crops, and building soil in new beds.
- [NO] Not on root vegetables, salads, or anything eaten raw.
- Be conservative. Caution here costs you nothing.

Hygiene: dedicated gloves and boots for this job, a brush at the door, and
hands washed at the tippy-tap on the way back. Every time.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Plumbed handwash station
===========================

1 h · €25 · Skill #..

The tippy-tap is the free version. This is the permanent indoor/outdoor one.

Build: a 20 L jerry can with a tap, mounted on a shelf or bracket at about 1.1
m, over a basin that drains into a bucket. Soap on a rope beside it, and a
nail for a towel.

Details:

- Site one at the toilet and one at the kitchen. Handwashing at the point of
   need is what works; a sink in another room is not used.
- Refilling it is a named daily job on the rota.
- Catch the greywater in a bucket underneath and put it on the fruit trees or
   use it for flushing.
- A foot-operated tap (a lever pressing the tap arm) is better still — no hand
   contact with the tap at all, which is the whole point.
- Warm water doubles compliance in winter. A jug of hot water from the stove
   into the jerry can once a day makes the difference between people washing
   and people not washing.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Bucket plunger washing machine + wringer
===========================================

1 h · €35 · Skill #..

Hand-washing a family's laundry with a plunger is hard work; with a plunger
and a wringer it is a manageable Monday morning.

       WASH               RINSE            WRING
     ┌────────┐        ┌────────┐      ╔═══════════╗
     │ ║║║║║  │        │ ║║║║║  │      ║  mop      ║
     │ ║plunger        │        │      ║  wringer  ║ ← or a hand mangle
     │ ║║║║║  │        │        │      ║  bucket   ║
     │ hot +  │        │  cool  │      ╚═══════════╝
     │  soap  │        │  clean │       drips back into the rinse bucket
     └────────┘        └────────┘
     20 L bucket       20 L bucket
     with a lid,       reuse this water as
     hole cut for      TOMORROW'S wash water
     the handle

Materials: 2 × 20 L buckets · a bucket lid with a 40 mm hole cut in the centre
· a "breathing mobile washer" plunger (€25) or a clean toilet plunger with six
20 mm holes drilled through the cup · a mop bucket wringer (€20) or a hand
mangle · a washboard (€15, genuinely useful for collars and cuffs).

Method

1. Wash bucket: hot water, soap or washing soda, and no more than 3–4 garments
    at a time. Overloading is why hand washing feels impossible.
2. Lid on with the plunger handle through the hole (stops it splashing
    everywhere and keeps the heat in). Plunge 2–3 minutes.
3. Soak 20 minutes, plunge again for 1 minute.
4. Wring, transfer to the rinse bucket, plunge 1 minute, wring again.
5. Reuse the rinse water as the next load's wash water, and the final
    greywater on the garden. A family wash costs about 30 L this way instead
    of 150.

Drying is the real bottleneck in winter. A wringer or mangle removes far more
water than hands can, which can halve the drying time. Dry in the warm room
with the window cracked — wet laundry in a sealed room causes condensation,
mould and respiratory problems. A rack over the stove, or a line in the loft
above the warm room, works best.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Greywater mulch basin
========================

2 h · €20 · Skill #.. · [LAND]

Turns washing water into irrigation without pipes, filters or pumps.

          greywater bucket or pipe
                   │
                   ▼
          ┌─────────────────────────┐  ← 300 mm deep, 1 m across
          │ ░░░ wood chips / straw ░│    basin around a FRUIT TREE
          │ ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │    (not vegetables)
          │ ░░░░  ░░░░  ░░░░  ░░░░  │    Filled with coarse mulch, which
          └─────────────────────────┘    filters, absorbs and feeds the soil
                   tree

Build: dig a shallow basin 300 mm deep and about 1 m across around a fruit
tree or berry bush, outside the trunk's drip zone (never against the trunk —
that causes collar rot). Fill it with coarse wood chips or straw. Pour or pipe
greywater into it. Top up the mulch twice a year as it breaks down.

Rules

- Use it within 24 hours. Stored greywater goes anaerobic and foul fast.
- Plant-safe soap only: no boron, no chlorine bleach, low sodium. Simple soap
   and potash soaps are fine; most laundry powders are not.
- Apply to soil under mulch, never to foliage, and never to root crops or
   anything eaten raw.
- Fruit trees, berry bushes, sweetcorn, potatoes and ornamentals are ideal
   targets.
- Never let it pond or run off. Keep it away from the water source and from
   where children play.
- Kitchen sink water with grease and food scraps clogs quickly — screen it
   first, or send that one to the compost heap instead.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Trench latrine
=================

1 h · €0 · Skill #.. · [LAND] short-term only

For a large group, a temporary camp, or when the bucket system is overwhelmed.

Rules:

- At least 30 m from any water source, and downhill from it.
- 300 mm wide, 1 m deep, as long as needed. Pile the spoil beside it with a
   spade stuck in.
- A log or a simple frame to sit on, and a screen for privacy.
- A scoop of soil and wood ash after every use.
- Backfill and mound over when it is 300 mm from the top, and mark it. Do not
   fill to the brim.
- Not usable in frozen ground or where the water table is high.
- Handwashing station at the exit, always.

[!] A trench latrine is a short-term measure. For anything lasting more than a
few weeks, the covered bucket system with proper composting is cleaner, safer,
closer to the house, works in winter, and returns fertility instead of burying
it.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Related: Chapter 05 — Sanitation & hygiene · Water builds · Build index


==============================================================================
21. 05 — FOOD STORAGE BUILDS
==============================================================================


05 — Food Storage Builds
========================

Read Chapter 03 first. Growing food is only half the job — preservation
capacity, not growing capacity, is the real limit on a homestead.

Contents: Root cellar · [[!] Cellar gas safety](#12-the-warning-nobody-gives-
you) · Buried barrel cellar · Root clamp · Cool pantry · Mylar packing station
· Cold smoker · Drying racks


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. Root cellar (excavated)
==========================

1–2 weeks · €600+ · Skill ### · [LAND]

The highest-return "appliance" a homestead can own. It holds 2–8 °C year-round
on zero energy, forever, and stores a winter's potatoes, roots, cabbages and
apples. It does the job of a chest freezer without the freezer, the
electricity, or the dependence.


1.1 The four storage zones
--------------------------

Different crops want different conditions. A good cellar has more than one
place.

Zone         Temp      Humidity  Stores                             Duration
-----------  --------  --------  ---------------------------------  ----------
Cold & damp  0–4 °C    90–95%    Carrots, beetroot, parsnip,        4–6 months
                                 turnip, celeriac, leeks (in damp
                                 sand)

Cold & damp  4–8 °C    85–90%    Potatoes (dark!), cabbage, apples  5–8 months
                                 (kept separate)

Cold & dry   0–5 °C    60–70%    Onions, garlic, shallots           6–9 months
                                 (hanging, airy)

Cool & dry   10–15 °C  50–70%    Winter squash and pumpkins         4–8 months

[!] Potatoes below 4 °C turn sweet and unpleasant (starch converts to sugar).
[!] Keep apples away from everything else — their ethylene sprouts potatoes
and spoils neighbours. A separate bay, or a sealed box.


1.2 The warning nobody gives you
--------------------------------

Stored vegetables respire. In a sealed or poorly ventilated cellar they
consume oxygen and produce carbon dioxide, which is heavier than air and pools
at the bottom. People have died walking into their own root cellars, silos and
covered pits.

  [!] VENTILATE BEFORE ENTERING a cellar that has been closed up.
  [!] Open the hatch and the vents, and wait several minutes.
  [!] Never enter alone if the cellar is below ground and has been shut.
  [!] If a candle will not stay lit at floor level, the air is not safe. Get out.
  [!] Dizziness, breathlessness or headache on entering = leave IMMEDIATELY.

The candle test is crude but it works, and it is free. Use it. This applies to
buried barrels and covered pits just as much as to walk-in cellars.


1.3 The design
--------------

                        ┌────┐ ← EXHAUST: from the CEILING, up and out
                        │ ↑↑ │    (warm, stale, humid air leaves at the top)
      ═══════════════╤══╧════╧═══════  ground level
       ▓▓▓▓▓▓ 600 mm+ of earth cover ▓▓▓▓▓▓▓
      ┌──────────────┴─────────────────────────┐
      │                                        │  ⇓ INTAKE: pipe down to
      │  ┌───────┐  ┌───────┐  ┌───────┐       │  ⇓ 150 mm ABOVE THE FLOOR
      │  │ROOTS  │  │POTATO │  │ONIONS │       │  ⇓ (cold air falls in)
      │  │in sand│  │ dark  │  │hanging│       │  │
      │  └───────┘  └───────┘  └───────┘       │  │
      │  gravel or earth floor (humidity)      │◄─┘
      └────────────────────────────────────────┘
         2–3 m below ground where the earth sits at the local annual
         mean temperature (~10 °C in Central Europe) — winter ventilation
         then chills it down to 2–8 °C and the mass holds it there

The two-pipe rule is the whole design. One pipe low (intake, ending 150 mm
above the floor) and one pipe high (exhaust, from the ceiling, rising above
ground). Cold air falls in, warm humid air rises out, and the cellar breathes
by itself with no fan. A cellar with one vent, or with both vents at the same
height, does not work.


1.4 Build
---------

1. Check before you dig: the water table (a cellar that floods is worthless),
    buried services, and your local building regulations. In many places an
    excavation of this size needs consent.
2. [!] An excavation over 1.2 m deep can collapse and kill. Batter the sides
    back at 45°, or shore them properly, or hire someone with a machine and
    the competence. This is the most dangerous build in this folder. Never
    work alone in a trench.
3. Excavate to 2–3 m depth. Deeper is more stable in temperature.
4. Structure: reinforced concrete, concrete block with a poured lintel, dry
    stone with a vaulted roof, or a proprietary precast chamber. The roof
    carries 600 mm+ of wet earth — that is well over a tonne per square metre,
    so it must be engineered, not guessed.
5. Waterproof the outside and lay a French drain around the base, draining to
    daylight or a soakaway. Water ingress is the number one cellar failure.
6. Insulate the roof (100 mm of foam board under the earth) — the roof is
    where summer heat comes in.
7. Earth cover: 600 mm minimum over the roof, graded to shed water away.
8. Fit the two pipes (110 mm) as above, with mesh caps against rodents and
    insects, and dampers so you can throttle them — you close them down in
    hard frost and open them wide on cold autumn nights to chill the cellar.
9. Floor: gravel or bare earth. Sprinkling it with water raises the humidity,
    which is exactly what roots want. A concrete floor makes it too dry.
10. Fit out: timber slatted shelves (never against the wall — leave 50 mm for
     airflow), boxes of damp sand for roots, hanging nets for onions, and a
     thermometer and hygrometer you actually read.
11. Door/hatch: insulated, and in a cold climate a double door creating an
     airlock stops a blast of frost every time you open it.


1.5 Operating it
----------------

- Chill it in autumn: open both vents wide on cold nights in October and
   November to drive the temperature down before you load it. Close them on
   warm days. This is active work and it decides how well the cellar performs.
- Load in cool weather, not warm. Cure everything first (see §7).
- Check weekly. Remove anything spoiling immediately — one rotten apple really
   does take the box.
- Never store fuel, paint or chemicals in a food cellar.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. Buried barrel root cellar
============================

4 h · €60 · Skill #.. · [LAND] The 95%-of-the-benefit version

If you cannot build a real cellar, build this. It stores 150 kg of roots and
takes an afternoon.

        ═══════╤═══════════════╤═══════  ground level
               │  ▓ straw ▓    │  ← 300 mm of straw/leaves over the lid,
          ┌────┴───────────────┴────┐     held down with a board and a stone
          │  ╔═══════════════════╗  │
          │  ║   LID (sealed,    ║  │
          │  ║    liftable)      ║  │
       ▓▓▓│  ╠═══════════════════╣  │▓▓▓
       ▓▓▓│  ║                   ║  │▓▓▓  200 L food-grade barrel,
       ▓▓▓│  ║  roots in damp    ║  │▓▓▓  buried UPRIGHT with the lid
       ▓▓▓│  ║  sand, layered    ║  │▓▓▓  100 mm proud of the soil
       ▓▓▓│  ║                   ║  │▓▓▓
       ▓▓▓│  ╚═══════════════════╝  │▓▓▓
          └─────────────────────────┘
              gravel base for drainage

Build

1. Dig a hole to take a 200 L food-grade barrel upright, with 100 mm of the
    rim left above ground so surface water cannot run in.
2. 150 mm of gravel in the bottom of the hole for drainage — a barrel sitting
    in water will float out and your crop will rot.
3. Drop the barrel in, backfill and firm the soil around it.
4. Drill four 10 mm holes just under the rim, above soil level, for a little
    air exchange. Cover them with mesh.
5. Layer produce in damp (not wet) sand, sawdust or leaves, with the barrel
    lid on.
6. Pile 300 mm of straw or dry leaves over the lid and weigh it down with a
    board and a stone. This is the insulation that stops it freezing.

Use: one barrel for carrots/beets/parsnips in sand, another for potatoes in
the dark. [!] The same gas warning applies — lift the lid and let it air for a
minute before reaching in, and don't put your head inside.

Even simpler: an old chest freezer with a dead compressor, buried to its lid,
is an excellent insulated root cellar. So is a cast-off fridge. Drill drainage
holes in the base, and remove any door latch before burying anything a child
could climb into.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Root clamp
=============

2 h · €5 · Skill #.. · [LAND] Free, ancient, and it works

The traditional way to store a tonne of potatoes or mangels with no structure
at all.

                   ╱▔▔▔▔╲  ← straw "chimney" left open at the top for
                  ╱ ▓▓▓▓ ╲    ventilation, capped with a tile if wet
            ┌────╱────────╲────┐
           ╱  ░░ 200 mm SOIL ░░ ╲
          ╱ ▒▒▒ 200 mm STRAW ▒▒▒ ╲
         │ ●●●●●●● ROOTS ●●●●●●●  │   Heap on a bed of straw on
         │ ●●●●●●●●●●●●●●●●●●●●   │   FREE-DRAINING ground
         └──▒▒▒ straw base ▒▒▒────┘
          ═══════════════════════════ drainage ditch dug around it

Build

1. Pick a well-drained, slightly raised spot. Dig a shallow drainage ditch
    around where the clamp will sit.
2. Lay 200 mm of straw as a bed.
3. Heap the crop into a ridge or cone, up to about 1 m high. Only sound, dry,
    unwashed, uncut produce — one damaged root rots the clamp.
4. Cover with 200 mm of straw, leaving a twist of straw poking out of the top
    as a ventilation chimney.
5. Cover the straw with 200 mm of soil, firmed and smoothed to shed rain,
    leaving the chimney clear.
6. In very hard frost, add more straw over the chimney.

Open from one end only, take what you need, and re-seal. Works for potatoes,
carrots, beetroot, swede, turnip and mangel-wurzel (your winter animal fodder
— see Chapter 08).


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Cool pantry conversion
=========================

1 day · €150 · Skill ##. · The no-digging option

Convert an existing north-facing room, cupboard, porch or garage corner into a
passively cooled larder. In Central Europe this holds 5–12 °C from October to
April — which is most of what a cellar does, for a twentieth of the work.

Build

1. Choose the coolest, north-facing space with an external wall. A cupboard
    under the stairs against a north wall is ideal.
2. Insulate it from the house: 50–100 mm of insulation on the walls, ceiling
    and door that face the heated part of the building, and a well-sealed
    insulated door. You are insulating it FROM the house, not from outside —
    that is the key inversion people get wrong.
3. Two vents through the external wall: one low (intake, 150 mm above floor)
    and one high (exhaust, near the ceiling), both 110 mm, both mesh-screened
    against rodents and insects, both with closable dampers.
4. Leave the external wall uninsulated — it is your cooling surface.
5. Slatted timber shelves, 50 mm clear of the walls. A cold stone or tiled
    floor and a thermometer.
6. Operate the dampers: open at night and in cold weather, closed in warm
    spells. Close them down in hard frost to stop things freezing.

[FLAT] In a flat: an unheated north-facing balcony cupboard or a box on a
north balcony does the same job from November to March — insulated on the
inside face, vented to outside air, mesh-screened. It is free refrigeration
for half the year.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Mylar packing station
========================

Setup: 1 h · €50 for the kit · Skill #..

Not a structure — a process and a small jig that turns a chaotic all-day job
into a steady production line. Packing 300 kg of staples goes much faster when
the bench is set up properly.

The kit: mylar bags (5–7 mil, 20 L) · oxygen absorbers (2,000 cc per 20 L
bucket) · food-grade buckets with gasket lids · an impulse sealer (€30) or a
hair straightener or a clothes iron and a board · a permanent marker and
labels · a scale · a funnel or a scoop · a jar with an airtight lid for unused
absorbers.

The jig: cut a piece of ply or card to hold the mylar bag open and upright
inside the bucket while you fill it — a simple collar, or two clamps on a
board. Without it you need three hands.

The production line

  1. FREEZE the grain/beans for 72 h at −18 °C first — kills weevil eggs, which
     are already in almost all grain when you buy it.
  2. Let it return to room temperature and stay DRY. Never seal anything damp:
     moisture + no oxygen = a botulism risk. Shop-dry grain and beans are fine.
  3. Line the bucket with the mylar bag. Fill.
  4. Drop in the O2 absorbers — WORK FAST, they start the moment the packet opens.
     Keep the unused ones in a sealed jar with the air squeezed out.
  5. Squeeze the air out, seal across the top with the iron/sealer, leaving one
     corner open. Press the last air out and seal the corner.
  6. LABEL: contents, weight, date packed, review date. Marker on tape, on the
     OUTSIDE, and a paper label INSIDE the bag too.
  7. Lid on the bucket. Store cool (<18 °C), dark, dry, off the floor.
  8. Check the next day: a correct seal goes hard and "bricked". That is your proof.

[!] Never pack anything with fat in it this way for the long term — nuts,
brown rice, ground flour, anything oily. Fat goes rancid with or without
oxygen. Those go in the rotating pantry.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Cold smoker
==============

1 day · €80 · Skill ##. · [LAND]

| [!] Read this first. Cold smoking does not preserve food on its own. It adds
| flavour and some surface antimicrobial effect, but the preservation comes
| from the curing that must happen first — salt, and for slow cold- smoked
| products, curing salt containing nitrite. Cold smoking uncured meat at 20–30
| °C is an incubator for botulism and listeria. Always follow a tested,
| published cure recipe. Do not improvise, do not use family guesswork, and if
| you are new to this, start with hot smoking (60–80 °C, which cooks the food)
| or with fish and cheese rather than charcuterie.

     SMOKE CHAMBER                         FIREBOX
     ┌──────────────┐
     │  ═══ hooks ══│  ← 1000 × 600 × 600 mm     smoke travels 2–4 m and
     │   │  │  │    │     insulated ply or       COOLS on the way: it must
     │  fish/meat   │     an old fridge/cabinet  arrive UNDER 30 °C for
     │   │  │  │    │                            cold smoking
     │  ═══ racks ══│
     │              │        ╔════════════════════╗
     └──────┬───────┘        ║  smouldering       ║
            └────────────────╢  sawdust /         ║
             110 mm pipe     ║  wood chips        ║
             or a covered    ╚════════════════════╝
             trench, 2–4 m   (or a separate smoke generator / maze burner)

Materials: a plywood cabinet, an old fridge with the plastic and insulation
foam stripped out, or a purpose-built box · stainless rods and hooks · 110 mm
pipe or a covered trench · a firebox (an old steel drum or a brick box) · a
damper at the chamber top · a thermometer at food level.

Build

1. Chamber: 1000 × 600 × 600 mm, with racks and hooks, a door, and an
    adjustable vent at the top. Untreated timber, stainless or an old fridge
    body (strip out all plastic, insulation foam and the compressor first).
2. Firebox 2–4 m away and lower than the chamber.
3. Connect them with buried 110 mm pipe or a covered trench. The distance is
    what cools the smoke. Longer = cooler.
4. Thermometer at food level. Cold smoke must stay under 30 °C; over that you
    are in the danger zone without cooking.
5. Fuel: beech, oak, alder, apple, cherry, hickory. [NO] Never resinous
    softwood (pine, spruce, fir) — acrid and unpleasant — and never treated,
    painted or MDF wood.
6. A maze or spiral sawdust burner (€25) gives 8–10 hours of clean cold smoke
    from one filling and is far easier to control than a fire.

Use: cure first, per a tested recipe · dry the surface until a tacky pellicle
forms (smoke will not adhere to a wet surface) · smoke in cool weather —
autumn and winter are the traditional smoking seasons for exactly this reason
· keep a written log of cure weights, times and temperatures.

For hot smoking (60–80 °C — cooks as it smokes, far more forgiving for a
beginner) move the firebox right under the chamber, or build a simple drum
smoker.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Drying and curing racks
==========================

2 h · €30 · Skill #..

Most storage crops need a curing period before they go into the cellar. Skip
it and they rot in December.

Crop                      Cure                       Then store
------------------------  -------------------------  -------------------------
Onions & garlic           2–3 weeks, warm, dry,      Hanging, cold and dry
                          airy, out of rain until
                          the necks are papery

Winter squash & pumpkins  10–14 days in the sun (or  10–15 °C, dry, on a shelf
                          25–30 °C indoors) to
                          harden the skin

Potatoes                  2 weeks in the DARK at     4–8 °C, dark, damp
                          10–15 °C to heal the
                          skins

Sweet potatoes            7–10 days at 27–30 °C and  13–15 °C
                          high humidity

Beans for drying          On the plant until the     Sealed jars, after 72 h
                          pods rattle, then indoors  freezing
                          until the bean cracks
                          rather than dents under a
                          hammer

Herbs                     Hung in small bunches,     Airtight jars, whole
                          shade, airy, 1–2 weeks     leaves


Build the rack: a simple frame with removable mesh trays (food-safe stainless,
nylon or food-grade plastic — not galvanised, zinc migrates into acidic food),
100 mm apart, in a shaded, airy, rain-proof place: a porch, a carport, an open
barn, a greenhouse with the doors open.

Onion and garlic braids hung from the rafters are the classic and they work
perfectly — plaiting the dried leaves together and hanging them where air
moves. Squash on a slatted shelf in a spare bedroom stores better than in any
cellar.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Related: Chapter 03 — Food storage · Cooking & heat builds · Build index


==============================================================================
22. 06 — GARDEN BUILDS 🏡
==============================================================================


06 — Garden Builds [LAND]
=========================

Read Chapter 07 first. Build in this order: beds, compost, protection, then
structures. A polytunnel over poor soil grows poor food.

Contents: No-dig beds · Three-bay compost · Brassica cage · Cold frame · Low
tunnel · Walk-in polytunnel · Seed-starting rack · Liquid feed barrel · Leaf
mould cage


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. No-dig growing beds
======================

1 h per bed · €15 per bed · Skill #.. · Start here, this weekend

No digging, no rotavating, no double-digging. Cardboard on the ground, compost
on top, plant into it. It suppresses weeds, feeds the soil biology, and after
2–3 years outperforms dug beds — while costing about a fifth of the labour. In
a crisis, where your own calories are the fuel, labour efficiency is survival
efficiency.

       1200 mm bed                450–600 mm path        1200 mm bed
    ├──────────────────┤        ├──────────────┤     ├──────────────────┤
    ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░        ▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒       ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░
    ░ 100 mm COMPOST  ░        ▒ wood chip /  ▒       ░                  ░
    ▓▓▓▓ CARDBOARD ▓▓▓▓        ▒ cardboard    ▒       ▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓
    ═══ grass / soil ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════
  
    1200 mm wide so you can reach the middle from either side.
    YOU NEVER STEP ON THE BED. Compacted soil is the commonest cause of poor yields.

Build

1. Mow or strim the grass/weeds short. Don't remove them — they are the first
    feed for the soil life.
2. Lay plain brown cardboard, overlapping every joint by 150 mm so no light
    gets through. Remove all plastic tape and staples. Wet it thoroughly.
3. 100 mm of good compost on top — municipal green waste compost, well-rotted
    manure, or your own. A 1.2 × 5 m bed takes about 0.6 m³.
4. Plant straight into the compost. Transplants go in immediately; for direct
    sowing of small seed (carrots, parsnip), wait until year two or sow into a
    drill of finer compost.
5. Edges are optional. Unedged beds are free, easy to reshape, and don't
    harbour slugs. Timber edges look tidy and hold the compost on a slope —
    use untreated larch, Douglas fir, sweet chestnut or oak, or line treated
    timber with plastic.
6. Mulch the paths with cardboard and wood chip. No weeding, and it becomes
    compost in place.
7. Top up with 25–50 mm of compost every autumn. That is the entire ongoing
    maintenance.

Perennial weeds (couch grass, bindweed, ground elder) need more: two layers of
cardboard plus 150 mm of compost, or a season under black plastic first. They
will still push through at the edges — keep at them.

Persuading the perimeter: 4 beds of 1.2 × 5 m = 24 m² of growing space, which
is the right size for a first year. Add two beds a year after that.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. Three-bay compost system
===========================

4 h · €0–60 · Skill #.. · Compost is the engine of the whole garden

     ┌──────────┬──────────┬──────────┐   Each bay 1000 × 1000 × 1000 mm
     │  BAY 1   │  BAY 2   │  BAY 3   │
     │ FILLING  │ TURNING  │  READY   │   Fill 1 → turn into 2 → turn into 3
     │          │          │          │   Turning is what makes it fast and hot
     │ new      │ 3 months │ finished │
     │ material │   old    │ compost  │
     └──────────┴──────────┴──────────┘
      ↑ removable front boards on all three, sliding in grooves

Materials: 9 pallets (free — heat-treated "HT" stamped only, never MB), or 22
× 100 boards on 70 × 70 posts · galvanised wire or brackets · 4 lengths of 45
× 45 as front-board guides · a tarpaulin or old carpet as a cover.

Build: stand pallets on edge to form three adjoining 1 m cubes, wired or
screwed at the corners · line the insides with cardboard if the gaps are wide
· fit vertical guides at the front of each bay so boards slide in and out as
the heap grows · leave the bays open to the soil beneath (worms and soil life
must get in) · a loose cover on top to keep rain off without sealing it.

Run it

- Roughly 2:1 browns to greens by volume. Browns = straw, dry leaves,
   cardboard, wood chip, paper, spent bedding. Greens = kitchen waste, grass
   clippings, weeds, manure, comfrey.
- Damp as a wrung-out sponge. Too dry and nothing happens; too wet and it goes
   anaerobic and stinks.
- Turn it into the next bay when it cools — every 3–6 weeks. Turning is the
   difference between finished compost in 3 months and finished compost in 18.
- 1 m³ is the minimum size for a heap to heat up. Smaller heaps compost, just
   slowly and cold — which does not kill weed seeds or pathogens.
- Bury kitchen waste in the middle and cover it, or you feed the rats.
- [NO] Not in the heap: meat, dairy, cooked food (rats), dog/cat faeces,
   diseased plant material, persistent perennial weed roots, anything treated
   with aminopyralid herbicides.

Keep the humanure system entirely separate — see Sanitation builds §3.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Insect mesh cage
===================

2 h · €50 · Skill #.. · Do this before the damage, not after

Physical barriers are 90% of pest control without sprays. A mesh cage over the
brassica bed defeats cabbage white butterfly, cabbage root fly, flea beetle
and pigeons in one go, permanently, with no inputs.

          ╔═══════════════════════════════════╗
          ║  ░░░░░ INSECT MESH 0.6–1 mm ░░░░░ ║  ← mesh, not netting.
          ║ ░                               ░ ║    Butterfly netting stops
          ║░       brassica bed              ░║    pigeons; only fine mesh
          ║░   1200 × 5000, hoops at 1 m     ░║    stops root fly and flea
          ║░                                  ░║   beetle.
          ╚═╤═══════════════════════════════╤═╝
            └── mesh edges BURIED or weighted with boards/stones all round
                (a 5 mm gap at the bottom is an open door)

Materials: 25–32 mm MDPE water pipe or 8 mm steel rod for hoops · 500 mm
lengths of rebar or timber pegs as sockets · insect mesh, 0.6–1 mm aperture
(€3–5/m², buy a big roll) · clothes pegs or clips · boards or stones for the
edges.

Build: drive rebar stakes 300 mm into the soil at 1 m intervals down both
sides of the bed, leaving 200 mm proud · slide a length of MDPE pipe over each
opposing pair to form a hoop about 700 mm high · run a ridge line of pipe or
string along the top to stop them flopping · drape the mesh over the whole
thing and seal every edge to the ground with boards, stones or buried soil.

Details: mesh is fine enough that you can water straight through it · it also
gives light frost protection and wind shelter · leave it on from the day you
plant until the day you harvest — one afternoon of access is all a cabbage
white needs · lift it for weeding, and check for anything that got trapped
inside.

Buy plenty of mesh, netting and fleece now. They last 5–10 years, they are the
single most effective non-chemical pest control, and there is no substitute
for them if they cannot be bought.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Cold frame
=============

4 h · €20 (free from old windows) · Skill #.. · Best value structure in the
garden

Does most of what a greenhouse does for a twentieth of the cost: hardening
off, early sowing, winter salad, overwintering, propagation, and season
extension at both ends.

              ╱▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔  ← old window as the lid, hinged at the
            ╱   glazed lid, 15–20°      BACK, propped open with a notched stick
          ╱                             (ventilation is the main daily job)
        ┌─────────────────────────┐
        │                         │  BACK 400 mm high
        │   1200 × 600 internal   │  FRONT 250 mm high
        │                         │  Slope faces SOUTH
        └─────────────────────────┘
         sitting on soil, not concrete — the earth is your heat store

Materials: an old window, a double-glazed unit, or twin-wall polycarbonate ·
22 × 150 boards or 18 mm exterior ply · 45 × 45 corner battens · 2 hinges · a
notched prop stick.

Build: a simple four-sided box sized to your window, back higher than front so
the lid sheds rain and faces the low winter sun · hinge the window at the back
· make a notched prop stick with 3 or 4 notches so you can vent it by degrees
· set it on soil against a south-facing wall if you have one (the wall stores
heat and releases it at night).

Use it: [!] The main risk is cooking your plants, not freezing them. On a
sunny March day a closed cold frame reaches 40 °C+ and kills everything
inside. Vent it every morning, close it every evening, from February onwards.
Put an old duvet or a straw mat over it on hard frost nights. Two frames is
better than one big one — different crops want different management.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Low tunnel
=============

2 h · €40 · Skill #..

A row-scale version: hoops and a cover over one bed. Buys 2–4 weeks at each
end of the season, protects against frost, wind, pigeons and cabbage white,
and rolls up in ten seconds.

Build: the same hoop construction as the insect cage (§3) — rebar sockets,
MDPE pipe hoops at 1 m centres, a ridge line — but covered with horticultural
fleece (frost protection, 2–4 °C, breathable, lets rain through) or clear
polythene (more warmth, but you must vent it, and it needs watering).

A single set of hoops per bed, with three different covers (insect mesh,
fleece, polythene) swapped through the year, is the most flexible and cheapest
protection system there is. Build the hoops once; change the skin by season.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Walk-in polytunnel 3 × 6 m
=============================

2 days, 2 people · €500 · Skill ###

Changes your growing calendar by 6–8 weeks at each end and makes winter salad
possible. The highest-value structure on a food-producing property after the
root cellar.

                      ╱▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔╲          Ridge 2200 mm
                    ╱              ╲
                  ╱                  ╲
                ╱                      ╲    Hoops at 1200 mm centres
              ╱                          ╲  → 6 hoops over 6 m
             │                            │
             │      3000 mm span          │
             │                            │
        ═════╧════════════════════════════╧═════
         foundation tubes 600 mm into the ground
         polythene trenched in 300 mm on both sides, or on base rails
  
         DOORS AT BOTH ENDS — non-negotiable. A tunnel you cannot cross-ventilate
         cooks its contents in April and grows mildew all summer.

Materials: a polytunnel kit is usually cheaper and far better than improvising
— 25–32 mm galvanised steel hoops, foundation tubes, ridge bar, crop bars,
door frames (~€350–500 for this size) · 720 gauge (180 micron) UV-stabilised
horticultural polythene, 5–7 year life · anti-hot-spot tape · timber for door
frames · a spare polythene sheet for when this one goes.

Build

1. Site it: open, sunny, sheltered from the prevailing wind but not in a frost
    pocket. Run it north–south for even light. Level ground, or terrace it.
2. Set out accurately. Mark the rectangle and check the diagonals are equal
    before anything goes in the ground. Everything downstream depends on it.
3. Foundation tubes 600 mm into the ground at 1.2 m centres, all at the same
    height, plumb.
4. Assemble the hoops and drop them into the foundation tubes; fit the ridge
    bar and crop bars.
5.  Apply anti-hot-spot tape along every hoop before the polythene goes on.
    Bare metal gets hot in the sun and degrades the film exactly where it
    touches. Skipping this can halve the life of a €150 cover. It is the
    single most commonly skipped step.
6. Build and fit the door frames at both ends.
7. Cover on a warm, still day — warm polythene stretches and pulls drum-tight;
    cold polythene never does, and wind makes it dangerous. Two or three
    people. Never attempt it in a breeze.
8. Trench the film in: dig a 300 mm trench down each side, pull the film
    tight, lay it in and backfill. (Or fit base rails, which make re-covering
    far easier later.)
9. Fix the film to the door frames with battens.

Use it: [!] Ventilation is the whole job. Open both doors on any sunny day
from March onwards — it hits 45 °C inside on a mild spring day. Water in the
morning, at the base, never overhead. Rotate crops inside it too; tunnel soil
gets exhausted and disease-ridden fast. Mulch heavily — a tunnel dries out
extremely quickly.

Buy the replacement polythene sheet now and store it dry and dark. In five
years you may not be able to get one, and a tunnel without a skin is a pile of
hoops.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Seed-starting rack
=====================

2 h · €80–150 · Skill #..

Starting plants indoors is what makes a short season productive. Windowsills
give leggy, weak seedlings; a simple lit rack gives stocky ones.

Build: a 4-tier steel or timber shelf unit (or plain shelves on brackets) with
an LED grow bar or a plain 4000–6500 K LED batten suspended 100–150 mm above
each shelf, on adjustable chains so you can raise it as the plants grow. Add a
heat mat (20 W) with a thermostat on the bottom shelf for germinating
tomatoes, peppers and aubergines, which need 20–25 °C.

Numbers: 20–40 W of LED per shelf, 14–16 h/day → 280–640 Wh/day per shelf.
That is real load — plan it in your power budget (Chapter 02), or run it only
in daylight hours off surplus solar, or accept a windowsill and slower growth.

Also: a bench, seed trays and modules (buy plenty of spares — they crack),
labels and a pencil (ink fades in a month outdoors), a fine-rose watering can,
sieved compost, and a notebook.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. Comfrey / nettle liquid feed barrel
======================================

30 min · €20 · Skill #.. · Free fertiliser, forever

Comfrey (potassium — for tomatoes, potatoes, fruit) and nettles (nitrogen —
for leafy growth) make excellent liquid feeds from plants that grow
themselves.

Build: a 60–200 L barrel with a tap at the base, on blocks. Inside, a mesh bag
or a perforated bucket holding the leaves so the liquid runs clear. A lid — a
tight one, because it smells genuinely appalling. Site it well away from the
house and from the neighbours.

Method: stuff the bag with comfrey or nettle leaves, weigh it down, fill with
water, cover · leave 3–4 weeks · draw off from the tap and dilute 1:10 to 1:20
until it is the colour of weak tea · water onto soil, not leaves · top the
barrel up with fresh leaves and water as you use it.

Concentrate version (no water, no smell): pack a barrel with comfrey leaves
only, weighted down, with a tap at the bottom. Over a few weeks they collapse
into a black concentrate that drips out. Dilute 1:40. No water added, far less
smell, and it stores.

Plant a dozen Bocking 14 comfrey roots in a corner now. Cut it 3–4 times a
year. It is sterile so it will not seed itself across the garden, its roots
reach down 2 m to mine minerals nothing else can, and it is also chicken and
rabbit feed and a fine compost activator.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. Leaf mould cage
==================

30 min · €15 · Skill #.. · Free soil improver

Autumn leaves become the best water-retaining soil conditioner there is, for
nothing but time.

Build: a cylinder of 1 m-high galvanised wire mesh, about 1 m across, held
with 4 stakes. Or four pallets. Or just a heap in a corner.

Use: fill with autumn leaves, tread down, water if dry, and wait 1–2 years
(leaves break down by fungal action, not bacterial — it is slow and it needs
no turning, no nitrogen and no attention). Shredding with a mower first halves
the time. Oak and beech take longest; they also make the best mould.

Build two or three cages, one for each year's leaves, and you get a barrow of
free soil conditioner every autumn in perpetuity. Use it as a mulch, a seed
compost ingredient, and to bulk out potting mixes when compost is scarce.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Related: Chapter 07 — Land & garden · Animal builds · Build index

23. 07 — ANIMAL BUILDS 🏡🏢

07 — Animal Builds [LAND][FLAT]
===============================

Housing that keeps animals alive, predators out, and cleaning down to a
30-second job. Read Chapter 08 first — especially the feed arithmetic.

Contents: Chicken coop · Run · Chicken tractor · Feeder & drinkers · Rabbit
hutch · Quail cage · Black soldier fly bucket · Fodder rack · Feed store


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. The chicken coop — full plan
===============================

For 6–8 hens · 2–3 days · €250–450 new, €80–150 from salvage · Skill ##.


1.1 The design rules that actually matter
-----------------------------------------

Everything else is preference. Get these wrong and the coop fails.

Rule                   Number                      Why
---------------------  --------------------------  ---------------------------
Coop floor area        0.4 m²/bird minimum         Less and you get feather-
                                                   pecking, bullying and
                                                   disease

Run area               2 m²/bird absolute          A bare overgrazed run is a
                       minimum, 10 m² is much      parasite and mud problem
                       better

Perch length           200 mm/bird                 They all roost at night;
                                                   short perches cause fights

Perch shape            45 × 45 mm with rounded     Chickens roost flat-footed
                       top edges — NOT a round     and must cover their toes
                       dowel                       with their body in winter,
                                                   or they get frostbite

Nest boxes             1 per 4 hens, 300 × 300 ×   More and they sleep in them
                       300 mm                      (and foul them)

Ventilation area       ~0.1 m² per 1 m² of floor   Damp, not cold, is what
                       → 0.24 m² for this coop     kills chickens. Ammonia and
                                                   moisture cause respiratory
                                                   disease

Vent position          Above the birds' heads,     Ventilation at perch height
                       always                      is a draught, and a draught
                                                   is what makes them ill

Mesh                   12.5 mm (½") galvanised     [NO] A fox tears through
                       weld mesh, 19 gauge         chicken wire and a marten
                                                   walks through it

Raised off the ground  500 mm                      Keeps the floor dry, denies
                                                   rats a nest, gives the
                                                   birds shade and shelter
                                                   underneath

Pop door               300 × 400 mm


1.2 The design
--------------

                          FRONT ELEVATION (2000 mm wide)
      ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ ← roof, 250 mm overhang
      │▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒ meshed vent strip 2000 × 80 ▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒│    (shaded by the overhang)
      ├────────────┬───────────────────┬──────┬──────────────┤
      │            │                   │      │              │  1150 mm
      │            │   ACCESS DOOR     │      │              │  front wall
      │            │    900 × 800      │      │              │
      │            │   (hinged, top)   │      │  ┌────────┐  │
      │            │                   │      │  │POP DOOR│  │
      └────────────┴───────────────────┴──────┴──┤300×400 ├──┘
           ║                                     └───┬────┘   ║
           ║                                         │ramp    ║   500 mm legs
           ║                                        ╱         ║
      ═════╩═════════════════════════════════════╱════════════╩═════ ground
  
  
                          SIDE SECTION (1200 mm deep)
      ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
      │ ← 250 overhang    roof  14° pitch   │ ← 150 overhang
      ├─────────────────────────────────────┤
      │▒vent▒                        ▒vent▒ │  1150 front → 850 back
      │                                     │
      │      ┌───┐         ┌───┐            │ ← perches 45×45, 450 above floor,
      │      └───┘         └───┘            │   400 apart, 300 off the walls
      │   ═════════════════════════         │ ← droppings board, removable,
      │                                     │   250 below the perches
      │  bedding ~100 mm deep litter        │        ┌──────────┐
      └─────────────────────────────────────┤        │NEST BOXES│ ← external, on the
      ║                                     ║        │  hinged  │   back wall
      ║           500 mm clear              ║        │   lid    │
      ══╩═════════════════════════════════╩═╝        └──────────┘

Overall: 2000 (L) × 1200 (D) coop box, floor 500 mm above ground. Front wall
1150 high, back wall 850 → 14° roof pitch (enough for corrugated sheet and for
rain and snow to clear). Floor area 2.4 m² = 6 hens comfortably, 8 at a
squeeze.


1.3 Materials
-------------

Item                                                         Qty         ~€
-----------------------------------------------------------  ----------  -----
45 × 45 mm treated timber, 2.4 m lengths                     24          72
45 × 95 mm treated timber, 2.4 m lengths (floor perimeter)   3           18
70 × 70 mm treated timber, 2.4 m length (legs)               1           8
18 mm exterior plywood sheet 2440 × 1220 (floor)             1           45
12 mm OSB3 sheets 2440 × 1220 (walls, roof deck, doors,      5           125
nest box)

Corrugated bitumen roof sheet (Onduline type) 2000 × 950     2           30
Roof fixings with washers                                    30          10
12.5 mm galvanised weld mesh, 1 m × 5 m (vents)              1           25
Butt hinges 75 mm (doors, nest lid)                          4 pairs     25
Hasp-and-staple + two-step latch or carabiner                3           18
Exterior screws 4 × 40 / 5 × 70 mm                           1 box each  20
M8 coach bolts + washers (legs)                              8           10
Cord + small pulley + cleat (remote pop door)                1 set       8
Raw linseed oil or exterior paint                            2.5 L       25
TOTAL                                                                    ~€439

| Cut the cost: clad with salvaged pallet boards or featheredge instead of OSB
| (−€100), use scavenged corrugated steel for the roof (−€30), build the whole
| thing from pallets (−€200). A coop is the single most pallet-friendly build
| there is. [!] Pallets: heat-treated (stamped "HT") only. Never MB (methyl
| bromide).


1.4 Principal cut list
----------------------

Cut these to length. Short infill studs and noggins you cut to fit as you go —
that is normal and it is faster than measuring everything twice.

Floor frame — 45 × 95 on edge

----------  ------  ---
Long rails  2000    2
End rails   1110    2

Part                         Length  Qty
---------------------------  ------  ---

Legs — 70 × 70

Part                    Length                                         Qty
----------------------  ---------------------------------------------  ---
Legs                    600                                            4
Lower braces — 45 × 45  cut to fit between legs, ~150 mm above ground  4

Part                       Length      Qty
-------------------------  ----------  ---
Top and bottom plates      2000        2
Studs                      1060        6
Door and pop-door framing  cut to fit  —

Stud positions along the 2000 mm: 0 · 250 · 1150 · 1400 · 1750 · 2000 → leaves
a 900 mm access-door opening and a 350 mm pop-door bay.

Back wall — 45 × 45 (2000 × 850 overall)

Part                       Length      Qty
-------------------------  ----------  ---
Top and bottom plates      2000        2
Studs (at 500 mm centres)  760         5
Nest-box opening framing   cut to fit  —

---------------------------------  --------------------  ---

Front studs                        1060                  2
Back studs                         760                   2

Part                                                     Length       Qty
-------------------------------------------------------  -----------  ---
Rafters (500 mm centres, 250 front + 150 back overhang)  1650         5
