Internal — 45 × 45 / 12 mm ply

Part                                 Size                    Qty
-----------------------------------  ----------------------  ---
Perches (round the top edges!)       45 × 45 × 1000          2
Perch brackets (notched)             —                       4
Droppings board — 12 mm ply          1100 × 500              1
Droppings board battens              cut to fit              2
Nest box (external, 2 compartments)  650 W × 350 D × 400 H   1
Ramp — 22 × 200 board                1000 long               1
Ramp cleats — 20 × 20                200 long, every 100 mm  9


1.5 Build sequence
------------------

Step 1 — Floor frame and legs (2 h)

1. Screw the 45 × 95 perimeter together: 2000 rails outside, 1110 end rails
    between → external 2000 × 1200. Check the diagonals are equal before the
    last screw. A square floor makes everything after it easy.
2. Fit the three 1110 joists at ~490 mm centres.
3. Bolt a 600 mm leg into each inside corner with 2 × M8 coach bolts, top of
    leg flush with the top of the frame. 95 mm of overlap, two bolts, no
    wobble.
4. Screw the lower 45 × 45 braces between the legs ~150 mm above ground. This
    triangulates the base — do not skip it, an un-braced coop racks and
    eventually leans.
5. Stand it on paving slabs or gravel, not soil. Check it is level.
6. Lay the 18 mm ply floor, screwed at 200 mm centres. Seal the edges with
    paint or oil before you go further — the floor edge is what rots first.

Step 2 — Wall frames (4 h)

7. Build each wall flat on the ground, as a picture frame with studs between
    top and bottom plates. Screw through the plates into the stud ends (2
    screws each).
8. Cut the side-wall top plates to the 14° slope. Trick: build both side
    frames as rectangles 1150 high, stand them up, mark the slope in place
    with a straight edge from the front wall to the back wall, then cut. Far
    easier than trigonometry.
9. Frame the openings: access door 900 × 800 with the sill 150 mm above the
    floor (stops bedding spilling out), pop door 300 × 400, sill 150 mm up.
10. Stand and screw the walls to the floor frame and to each other. Check
     square and plumb at every corner.

Step 3 — Cladding and vents (3 h)

11. Clad the outside with 12 mm OSB or boards. Leave the vent openings:
    - A continuous 2000 × 80 mm strip along the top of the front wall, under
       the roof overhang (0.16 m²)
    - Two 200 × 150 openings high on the back wall (0.06 m²)
    - The triangles at the top of each side wall (~0.06 m²)
    - Total ≈ 0.28 m² — all of it above perch height. This is the single most
       important detail in the whole build.
12. Staple 12.5 mm weld mesh over every opening, on the inside, then screw a
     batten over the edge. Staples alone pull out; a marten only needs one
     loose corner.
13. Overlap or seal all cladding joints. Water getting into the wall is what
     ends a coop's life.

Step 4 — Roof (2 h)

14. Fix the 5 rafters across the walls at 500 mm centres, with 250 mm
     overhanging the front and 150 mm the back. Notch them over the wall
     plates, or use angle brackets.
15. Deck with 12 mm OSB, then the corrugated sheet, fixing through the crest
     of the corrugation (never the trough) with washered nails or screws.
16. Or, cheaper: skip the deck and fix the corrugated sheet straight onto 45 ×
     45 purlins at 450 mm centres.
17. Overhangs matter: they keep rain out of the vents and off the walls. Don't
     trim them back to save material.

Step 5 — Doors, nest boxes, internals (3 h)

18. Access door: 12 mm ply with a 45 × 45 frame behind it, hung on 2 hinges,
     closed with a hasp and a two-step latch or a carabiner. Foxes and martens
     solve simple turn-buttons and slide bolts. This is not a myth — use a
     fastener that needs two separate actions.
19. Pop door: a ply panel running in two vertical battens as guides, lifted by
     a cord over a small pulley on the roof edge to a cleat outside the run —
     so you can let them out without going in.
20. Ramp: 22 × 200 board, 1000 mm, with 20 × 20 cleats screwed every 100 mm.
21. Nest boxes: build a 650 × 350 × 400 box, cut two 300 × 300 openings
     through the back wall, hang the box outside with a sloped, hinged lid
     (slope it at 30–40° so they cannot roost on top of it and foul it). Put a
     100 mm lip on the front of each compartment to hold the bedding in. Nest
     floor 300 mm above the coop floor.
22. Perches: 45 × 45, round the top two edges with a plane or sandpaper,
     sitting loose in notched brackets at 450 mm above the floor, 400 mm
     apart, 300 mm from any wall. Removable = 10-second cleaning.
23. Droppings board: 12 mm ply on battens, 250 mm below the perches. Slides
     out. Catches ~60% of all the manure the birds produce — scrape it into a
     bucket each morning and the coop stays clean with almost no effort. The
     single best quality-of-life feature in the design.

Step 6 — Finish (1 h)

24. Two or three coats of raw linseed oil or exterior paint. Inside as well as
     out — the inside is what gets damp.
25. Deep litter: 100 mm of wood shavings or chopped straw on the floor.
26. Leave it a few days to air before the birds move in.


1.6 What ruins a coop
---------------------

  [NO] Chicken wire instead of weld mesh          → you will lose the flock
  [NO] Not enough ventilation                     → respiratory disease all winter
  [NO] Ventilation at perch height                → draughts, sick birds
  [NO] Round perches / sharp-edged perches        → frostbitten toes, bumblefoot
  [NO] No droppings board                         → you stop cleaning it, then disease
  [NO] Sitting on the ground                      → rot, rats nesting underneath
  [NO] Simple slide bolts                         → martens and foxes open them
  [NO] Nest boxes at or above perch height        → they sleep in them and foul the eggs
  [NO] Too small "because they're outside all day" → winter, rain and broodiness say otherwise
  [NO] Flat roof / no overhang                    → leaks, wet walls, wet vents
  [NO] No access for cleaning                     → you won't clean it


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. The predator-proof run
=========================

12 m² for 6 birds · 1 day · €230–280 · Skill ##.

                PLAN — 3000 × 4000 mm                    SECTION at the fence
      ┌────────────────────────────────────┐          ║ 25 mm mesh or netting
      │  .        .         .         .    │          ║   (600 mm)
      │                                    │          ╠═══ 1200 mm
      │            COOP                    │          ║ 12.5 mm WELD MESH
      │          (in a corner,             │          ║   (predator band)
      │           or outside               │          ║
      │           with a pop-             │  ▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓╩▓▓▓  ← ground level
      │           door tunnel)             │  ▓ 450 mm APRON, mesh laid FLAT
      │                                    │  ▓ on the ground, pegged, turfed over
      │  .        .      GATE   .     .    │
      └────────────────────────┬───────────┘   A fox digs AT the fence line, hits
         . = 70 × 70 post at 2 m centres       the apron, and gives up. Far easier
                                               and more effective than burying.

Materials: 8 × 70 × 70 posts, 2400 mm (or 75 × 75 fence posts) · 14 m of 45 ×
45 top rail · 1 roll of 12.5 mm weld mesh, 1.2 m × 25 m · 14 m × 0.6 m of 25
mm mesh or netting for the upper band · 12 m² of bird netting for the top ·
galvanised staples · 40 ground pegs · gate hardware.

Build:

1. Set the posts at 2 m centres, 600 mm into the ground on gravel (or in metal
    post shoes — they last far longer). 1800 mm above ground so you can walk
    in.
2. Top rail all round, at post height.
3. Weld mesh 1200 mm high stapled to the posts every 100 mm, tight.
4. The apron: lay 450 mm of mesh flat on the ground, running outward from the
    base of the fence, all the way round. Peg it every 300 mm, then cover with
    soil or turf. Grass grows through it and it disappears in a season.
5. Upper band of cheaper 25 mm mesh from 1200 to 1800 mm.
6. Net or mesh the top. Hawks take chickens in broad daylight, and hens fly
    better than people expect.
7. Gate 900 mm wide, with the same two-step latch as the coop.
8. Rotate if you can: two runs used alternately, six months each, stops the
    ground becoming a bare parasite-laden mud pit. Sow the resting one with
    grass, clover and fodder kale.

[!] Check the whole perimeter monthly and after every storm. One lifted staple
is a whole flock.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Chicken tractor (mobile)
===========================

For 3–5 birds · 1 day · €150 · Skill ##.

A bottomless A-frame coop-and-run on wheels. Moved a metre or two each day, it
gives the birds fresh grass and insects continuously, spreads their manure
evenly, and needs no run at all. The best way to cut the feed bill, and
excellent for clearing and fertilising a bed before planting.

             ╱╲              2000 mm long × 1200 wide × 1200 high
            ╱  ╲             45 × 45 A-frames at 0 / 1000 / 2000 mm
           ╱ ▓▓ ╲            One third enclosed and clad (shelter, perch, nest)
          ╱ ▓▓▓▓ ╲           Two thirds open frame with 12.5 mm weld mesh
         ╱________╲          Handles one end, two wheels the other
        ═╧════════╧═         Weight target: under 25 kg so one person can move it

Key points: keep it light (45 × 45 and thin cladding only) · a skirt of mesh
laid flat around the base at night, or move it inside a fence · no floor —
that is the point · a nest box you can reach from outside · move it every day
or the grass under it dies.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. No-waste feeder and drinkers
===============================


4.1 Port feeder — 30 min, €20
-----------------------------

A 20 L bucket with 90° PVC elbows (75 mm) pushed through holes cut 80 mm up
from the base. The bird puts its head into the elbow to feed; nothing gets
scratched out onto the floor.

1. Mark 3–4 holes around the bucket, 80 mm from the bottom.
2. Cut with a hole saw to a tight fit on the elbow.
3. Push the elbows in, opening pointing down and out, seal with silicone.
4. Fill, fit the lid, hang or stand it at bird-back height (about 300 mm) —
    feeding at back height stops them scratching feed out and keeps rats off.

Saves 20–30% of your feed bill, which over a year is 60–90 kg of grain. It
also keeps feed dry and much less attractive to rodents. Build this one first.


4.2 Nipple drinker — 30 min, €20
--------------------------------

A 20 L bucket with 3–4 horizontal poultry nipples screwed into the base (8 mm
drill, they self-tap). Clean water, no droppings, no spillage, no daily
scrubbing. Hang at head height so the birds reach slightly up.


4.3 Keeping water liquid in winter — the job nobody plans for
-------------------------------------------------------------

A frozen drinker is the most common winter chicken killer. Options, in order:

- Two drinkers, swapped morning and evening from the house. Free, reliable,
   and it always works.
- Black rubber tub in the sun — thaws itself on sunny days, and you can flex
   the ice out rather than chipping it.
- Insulated box around the bucket (polystyrene in a plywood case), lid on top.
- Solar-powered: a 5–15 W aquarium or pipe heater on a thermostat, run from
   the 12 V system (see Power builds). 15 W for 8 hours is 120 Wh/day — well
   within a modest system, and it removes the job entirely.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Rabbit hutch — 4 compartment
===============================

1 buck + 3 does · 1 day · €120–180 · Skill ##.

                  2400 mm wide × 700 deep × 2 tiers
      ┌───────────────────┬───────────────────┐
      │  DOE 1            │  DOE 2            │   Each compartment:
      │  1200 × 700 × 650 │                   │   1200 W × 700 D × 650 H
      ├───────────────────┼───────────────────┤
      │  DOE 3            │  BUCK             │   Nest box space at one end
      │                   │                   │   Wire floor over a slide-out tray
      └───────────────────┴───────────────────┘
        ║               ║               ║         Raised 700 mm on legs
        ║               ║               ║         Sloping roof, 300 mm overhang
      ══╩═══════════════╩═══════════════╩══       all round (sun and rain)

Materials: 45 × 45 frame · 12 mm OSB or ply for the backs, ends and dividers ·
12.5 mm weld mesh for the doors and fronts · galvanised weld mesh 12 × 12 or
16 × 16 mm for the floors · corrugated roof sheet · galvanised trays or sheet
metal for the droppings · hinges and latches.

The details that matter:

- Shade is more important than warmth. Rabbits handle −15 °C easily but die
   above 28–30 °C. Site the hutch in shade, with a deep roof overhang, and
   give it cross-ventilation in summer. Overheating is the number one killer
   of hutch rabbits.
- Wire floors (16 × 16 or 12 × 25 mm) let droppings fall through to a tray —
   far cleaner and healthier than solid floors. But always give a solid
   resting board (a plain piece of ply, 300 × 300) in each compartment, or
   they get sore hocks.
- Nest box for each doe: 400 × 300 × 300 with a 150 mm lip, added 3 days
   before kindling and removed at weaning.
- Hay rack on the front of each compartment — hay is their staple and it must
   be available constantly and kept off the floor.
- Raise it 700 mm — comfortable to work at, dry, and no rats underneath.
- Rabbit manure is "cold" — it goes straight onto the garden with no
   composting. Put the trays where you can slide them out and barrow them to a
   bed.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Quail cage — stackable
=========================

10 birds per tier · 4 h · €60 · Skill ##. · [FLAT] works on a balcony

              1000 mm wide × 600 deep × 250 high per tier
      ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
      │  mesh top (soft: netting or plastic)    │ ← 250 mm ceiling ONLY
      │                                          │   [!] quail startle and fly
      │   10 birds                     ╲         │   straight up — a hard roof
      │                                 ╲ 7°     │   at 400 mm breaks necks
      ├──────────────────────────────────╲──────┤
      │  droppings tray (slide out)       └─egg─┘ ← floor sloped 7° so eggs
      └─────────────────────────────────────────┘    roll to a front gutter
        feeder and drinker on the OUTSIDE, birds feed through the front mesh

Build: a 45 × 45 or 20 × 45 frame, 12.5 mm weld mesh on the sides, 16 × 16 or
12 × 12 mm mesh floor sloped 7° to a front lip that catches the eggs, a slide-
out tray underneath (a baking tray or sheet metal), and a soft top — netting
or plastic mesh, not plywood.

Details:

- 0.1 m²/bird. 10 birds in 1000 × 600 is right; crowding causes feather
   pecking, which in quail escalates fast.
- 250 mm ceiling height, soft top. Quail flush vertically when startled. A low
   soft ceiling is a welfare requirement, not a space-saving trick.
- Feeder and drinker outside the cage, accessed through the mesh — keeps feed
   clean and makes daily care a 20-second job.
- Stack 2–3 tiers with the trays between them. 3 tiers = 30 birds = ~25 eggs a
   day in 0.6 m² of floor space.
- Litter the tray with sawdust or paper and change it every 2–3 days — quail
   produce a lot for their size and ammonia builds fast in a flat.
- [!] They do not go broody. A small incubator (€60, ~40 W for 17 days) is
   what makes this a renewable cycle. Build it into your power plan.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Black soldier fly larvae bucket
==================================

30 min · €15 · Skill #.. · Free protein from kitchen waste

BSF larvae convert food waste into 40%-protein feed, and they harvest
themselves — when mature they crawl up a ramp looking for somewhere dry to
pupate, and fall into a jar.

          ┌─────────────────┐  vented lid (mesh)
          │  food waste     │
          │  ░░░░░░░░░░░░   │        ╱── 35–40° ramp (a strip of ply or
          │  ░ larvae ░░    │      ╱      corrugated plastic) up to a hole
          │  ░░░░░░░░░░░░  ╱│    ╱
          │              ╱  │  ╱  ┌────┐
          └────────────╱────┘─────│ jar│ ← self-harvested larvae collect here
           20 L bucket            └────┘
           drainage holes + gravel in the base

Build: drill 6 mm drainage holes in the base of a 20 L bucket and add 50 mm of
gravel · fit a ramp at 35–40° from the base to a 40 mm hole near the rim · a
pipe or chute from the hole to a collecting jar · mesh-vented lid · keep it in
shade, 25–30 °C.

Feed it: fruit and vegetable waste, spoiled produce, bread, brewing waste,
manure. Not meat or dairy in a domestic setting (smell and rats). Yield:
roughly 1 kg of larvae per 10 kg of waste — straight to chickens, quail or
fish. [!] BSF need warmth; in Central Europe they work April–October outdoors,
or year-round in a warm shed.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. Fodder sprouting rack
========================

2 h · €40 · Skill #.. · Fresh green feed in winter

1 kg of barley becomes 6–8 kg of green fodder mat in 7 days. Be clear about
what this does: it does not create calories (dry matter falls slightly) — it
converts stored dry grain into fresh green feed with vitamins, which is
exactly what confined animals lack from November to March.

Build: a shelf unit with 7 trays (one per day of the cycle), each tray drilled
with 3 mm drainage holes and tilted 10° to a collection gutter, the whole rack
draining into a bucket.

Run it:

  Day 0  Soak 1 kg of barley 12 h → drain → spread 10–15 mm deep in tray 1
  Day 1–6  Rinse every tray twice a day; they drain into each other and the bucket
  Day 7  Tray 1 is a 6–8 kg root-bound green mat. Cut it out, feed it whole,
         re-sow the tray. One tray harvested every single day, forever.

Keep it at 18–21 °C, out of direct sun, with good airflow. Mould is the enemy
— rinse properly, don't sow too thick, and throw out any tray that smells
sour.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. Rodent-proof feed store
==========================

1 h · €40–80 · Skill #..

Rats and mice will eat, foul and destroy more of your feed than your animals
do, and they carry disease into the coop. A sack of feed on a shed floor is a
rodent feeding station.

Options, best first:

1. Galvanised steel dustbins with tight lids (€30 each, ~60 kg of feed each).
    Rodents cannot chew galvanised steel. Bungee the lid down against foxes.
2. A plywood box lined completely with 6 mm hardware cloth — floor, walls and
    lid. More work, holds more, and looks like furniture.
3. IBC tote with a screw lid, for bulk grain.
4. [NO] Not plastic bins, not sacks, not "up on a shelf". Rats climb, and they
    chew through 5 mm polypropylene in an evening.

Also: keep the store away from the coop (don't concentrate the attraction
where the birds sleep), sweep spills immediately, and keep 12 snap traps set
around the perimeter as standard practice, not as a response to a problem.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Related: Chapter 08 — Animals & protein · Garden builds · Build index


==============================================================================
24. 08 — APARTMENT BUILDS 🏢
==============================================================================


08 — Apartment Builds [FLAT]
============================

Read Chapter 09 first. You cannot feed yourself from a flat — but you can
produce the fresh nutrition that stored calories lack, in any season, in a few
square metres, with almost no power. These are the builds that do it.

Contents: Sprouting rack · Microgreen shelf · Mushroom bucket · Wormery ·
Self-watering container · Potato sack · Balcony growing frame · Balcony rain
catch · Air cleaner box · Under-bed storage

| [!] Before you build anything on a balcony: check your lease and building
| rules (plants, animals, gas cylinders, solar panels, anything fixed to the
| structure) and your balcony's weight limit. Wet compost is ~700 kg/m³ — a
| row of large pots is a real structural load. Keep heavy items over the edge
| beams, close to the building wall, never massed in the middle of a
| cantilevered slab.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. Sprouting rack
=================

20 min · €20 · Skill #.. · Build this first. It is the single most important
apartment food technique.

Fresh, vitamin-C-rich food in 3–5 days, from dry seed you already store, with
no light, no soil, no power, no season and no garden. Nothing else in this
entire plan delivers as much nutritional security per euro or per litre of
storage space.

          ┌─────────────────────────────────┐  ← a dish tray or baking tray
          │    ╲       ╲       ╲       ╲    │    catches the drips
          │   ╔═╲═╗   ╔═╲═╗   ╔═╲═╗   ╔═╲═╗ │
          │   ║JAR║   ║JAR║   ║JAR║   ║JAR║ │  Jars at 45°, MOUTH DOWN,
          │   ║ 1 ║   ║ 2 ║   ║ 3 ║   ║ 4 ║ │  resting in a rack or a
          │   ╚═══╝   ╚═══╝   ╚═══╝   ╚═══╝ │  dish drainer
          └─────────────────────────────────┘
            Day 4    Day 3   Day 2   Day 1
            (eat)                    (soak)
  
          One jar started every day = a continuous daily harvest, forever.

Materials: 4 wide-mouth 1 L jars (€8) · mesh sprouting lids or muslin and
rubber bands (€8) · a dish drainer, a wire rack, or a wooden block with 45°
holes · a tray underneath.

Build: honestly, the "build" is a rack that holds four jars at 45° mouth-down
over a tray. A plastic dish drainer does it perfectly. If you want something
neater, cut four 45° notches in a piece of 45 × 95 timber and screw it to a
base.

The method

  1. RINSE 2–4 tbsp (30–60 g) of seed, put it in the jar.
  2. SOAK 6–12 hours in plenty of cool water.
  3. DRAIN COMPLETELY and stand at 45°, mouth down.
  4. RINSE AND DRAIN TWICE A DAY (three times in hot weather).
      Drainage is everything. Standing water is what causes spoilage.
  5. HARVEST at 2–5 days. Rinse, drain, keep cool, eat within 3–5 days.
  6. START A NEW JAR EVERY DAY.

Yields (by weight): mung bean 4–6× · alfalfa 8–10× · lentil 3–4× ·
radish/broccoli 5–6× · chickpea 3×.

[!] Never sprout kidney/red beans (toxic raw), tomato, potato, aubergine,
pepper or rhubarb seed. [!] Food safety: sprouts grow warm and damp, which
also suits Salmonella. Rinse and drain properly, clean jars between batches
with boiling water, and discard anything that smells sour, slimy or musty —
trust your nose absolutely. If anyone in the household is pregnant, elderly,
very young or immunocompromised, cook the sprouts (stir-fried mung sprouts are
excellent).

Store 5 kg of sprouting seed per person (€25–40, keeps 4+ years sealed).


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. Microgreen shelf with LED
============================

2 h · €100 · Skill #.. · Fresh greens every 10 days, all year

     ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
     │ ▬▬▬▬▬▬ LED bar, 100–150 mm above ▬▬ │ ← on adjustable chains/hooks
     │ ▤▤▤▤ TRAY 1 ▤▤▤▤   ▤▤▤▤ TRAY 2 ▤▤▤▤ │   so you can raise it
     ├─────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ ▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬ │
     │ ▤▤▤▤ TRAY 3 ▤▤▤▤   ▤▤▤▤ TRAY 4 ▤▤▤▤ │   4–6 trays on rotation
     ├─────────────────────────────────────┤   = a harvest every 2–3 days
     │ ▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬▬ │
     │ ▤▤▤▤ TRAY 5 ▤▤▤▤   ▤▤▤▤ TRAY 6 ▤▤▤▤ │
     └─────────────────────────────────────┘
       Each tray 200 × 300 mm, drilled, sitting in a solid tray below

Materials: a 3–4 tier shelf unit (€40, or a plank-and-bracket shelf) · LED
battens or grow bars, 4000–6500 K, 20–40 W per shelf (€15–25 each) · chains or
adjustable hooks · 12 seed trays (6 drilled + 6 solid as drip trays, €20) ·
coir, compost or hemp mats · a spray bottle · a timer plug.

Build: mount an LED batten under each shelf, hung on chains so the height is
adjustable · put the drilled trays inside solid trays so nothing drips onto
your floor · a timer plug set to 12–16 h/day.

Grow it

  Day 0     Sow 10–15 g of seed THICKLY on 20–30 mm of damp coir/compost.
  Day 0–3   COVER (a tray on top, or a dark cloth). Darkness gives an even,
            upright start. Mist once a day.
  Day 3     Uncover, light on, 12–16 h/day. Water from BELOW where you can —
            wet leaves in a still flat go mouldy.
  Day 7–14  Harvest with scissors just above the medium. 150–250 g per tray.
            Re-sow the tray the same day.

Best for substance and volume: pea shoots and sunflower shoots. Ordinary dried
marrowfat/whole peas from the supermarket, soaked overnight and sown thick,
give a big tray of sweet shoots in 10 days and will regrow a second cut.
Cheapest fresh green there is. Then radish, broccoli, mustard, kale, beet,
coriander.

Power: 6 shelves × 30 W × 14 h = 2.5 kWh/day if you run the lot — that is a
serious load. Options: run 2 shelves not 6 · use a sunny windowsill for half
of them and accept slower growth · run the lights only during daylight from
surplus solar. Do the power arithmetic before you buy six lights.

Reuse the medium by adding it to the wormery, then back into trays with fresh
coir mixed in.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. Oyster mushroom bucket
=========================

1 h · €25 · Skill #.. · Protein and texture from waste, in the dark

          ┌───────────────────────┐ ← lid with a few holes
          │  .    .    .    .     │
          │     substrate +       │  20 L bucket, 10 mm holes
          │ .   grain spawn   .   │  drilled in a grid at
          │     mixed through     │  ~100 mm centres all over
          │  .    .    .    .     │
          │     .    .    .       │  Mushrooms fruit OUT of the holes
          └───────┬───────────────┘
               drainage holes in the base

Materials: a 20 L bucket with a lid · oyster mushroom grain spawn (€10–20/kg,
from a reputable supplier) · substrate: used coffee grounds, shredded
cardboard, straw, or hardwood sawdust · a drill.

Build & run

1. Drill 10 mm holes in a grid over the sides of the bucket, ~100 mm apart,
    plus drainage holes in the base.
2. Prepare the substrate:
   - Coffee grounds: use within 24 h of brewing — already effectively
      pasteurised. The easiest option, and free from any café.
   - Straw or cardboard: soak in hot water (65–75 °C) for 1 hour, then drain
      until it is damp, not dripping. Squeeze a handful — a few drops, not a
      stream.
3. Mix in 5–10% spawn by weight, layering as you pack the bucket.
4. Lid on, dark cupboard, 18–24 °C, 2–4 weeks. It turns white as the mycelium
    colonises.
5. When fully white, move it somewhere with light and humidity — mist through
    the holes twice a day, or put the bucket inside a clear bag with slits.
6. Mushrooms appear from the holes over 5–10 days. Harvest by twisting the
    whole cluster off. Expect 2–3 flushes, 200–400 g of mushrooms per kg of
    substrate.
7. Spent substrate goes to the wormery or the balcony pots.

Make it a cycle: you can propagate your own spawn from a piece of a mushroom
stem butt laid on damp cardboard in a sealed bag. This is what turns a
purchase into a renewable system — do it while you still have healthy stock.

[!] Only grow from spawn you bought from a reputable supplier. Never eat a
wild or unknown mushroom. Contaminant moulds (green, black, pink) mean discard
the whole bucket outdoors — do not try to rescue it, and do not open it
indoors.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


4. Wormery
==========

1 h · €25 · Skill #.. · Solves kitchen waste AND feeds the balcony

With no rubbish collection, a flat fills with food waste in days. A wormery
eats it silently under the sink, produces no smell when run properly, and
gives you the compost and liquid feed your containers need.

          ┌───────────────────────┐ ← lid (keeps light out, flies out)
          │  ▓▓ WORKING TRAY ▓▓▓  │   add waste here
          ├───────────────────────┤
          │  ▓▓ FULL TRAY ▓▓▓▓▓▓  │   worms migrate UP to the food,
          ├───────────────────────┤   leaving finished castings behind
          │  ▓▓ FINISHED ▓▓▓▓▓▓▓  │
          ├───────────────────────┤
          │   liquid collects  [TAP]  ← "worm tea": dilute 1:10, feed plants
          └───────────────────────┘
            Stackable boxes, each drilled with 5 mm holes in the BASE

Materials: 3–4 stackable plastic storage boxes (€15) · a drill · composting
worms — Eisenia fetida, tiger/brandling worms (€20 for 500 g; [!] not
earthworms from the garden, they die in a wormery) · shredded card and coir as
bedding · a tap fitted to the bottom box (optional but useful).

Build: drill 5 mm holes across the base of each box (so worms can migrate up)
and a few ventilation holes near the top of the sides · leave the bottom box
undrilled as a liquid sump, with a tap fitted low · stack them.

Run it

- Bedding first: 100 mm of damp shredded card and coir. Add the worms and
   leave them a week before feeding.
- Feed: vegetable and fruit peelings, coffee grounds, tea, crushed eggshells,
   shredded paper and card, spent microgreen medium.
- [NO] Not: meat, dairy, oily food, citrus or onion in quantity, or anything
   cooked and salty.
- Chop it small and bury it under the surface. Add a handful of shredded card
   with every feed — too wet and sloppy is the usual failure.
- Feed little and often, and only when the last lot is mostly gone.
   Overfeeding makes it sour and smelly.
- When the top box is full, add a new box on top with fresh bedding and food;
   the worms migrate up over a few weeks and the box below is finished
   castings.
- Drain the liquid regularly, dilute it 1:10, and use it on your containers.
   It is an excellent free feed.

Troubleshooting: smells → too wet or overfed; add dry card and stop feeding ·
worms escaping → too wet, too acidic, or they've run out of food · fruit flies
→ bury the food properly and cover the surface with damp newspaper · too dry →
mist it.

Site it under the sink, in a utility cupboard, or on a shaded balcony (bring
it inside below 5 °C and above 30 °C — worms die at both extremes).


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. Self-watering container
==========================

30 min · €10 · Skill #.. · Halves your balcony watering

A sub-irrigated planter has a water reservoir underneath that the compost
wicks up from. Plants take what they need, you water once or twice a week
instead of twice a day, and a missed day does not kill anything. On a windy
high balcony this is the difference between plants that live and plants that
don't.

          ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
          │        COMPOST                  │
          │                                 │  ┌──┐ ← fill tube
          │   ╔═══════════════════════════╗ │  │  │   (a pipe to the bottom;
          │   ║ wicking compost column    ║ │  │  │    fill until it runs
          │  ═╩═══════════════════════════╩═│══│  │    out of the overflow)
          │  ░░░░░ RESERVOIR ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │  └──┘
          └──────────────────────────────●──┘ ← OVERFLOW HOLE at reservoir top
                                                (stops waterlogging — essential)

Build from two stacked boxes or buckets:

1. Take two identical containers, or one container plus a smaller one to sit
    inside it as a spacer.
2. Make a platform about 80–100 mm above the base (a perforated tray, an
    upturned pot, or a drilled plastic box) — this creates the reservoir.
3. Cut two or three holes in the platform and stuff them with compost or
    strips of capillary matting to form wicks that reach down into the water.
4. Fit a fill tube (20–30 mm pipe) from the top down into the reservoir.
5.  Drill an overflow hole at the top of the reservoir level  — this is the
    critical detail. Without it the compost waterlogs and the roots rot.
6. Fill with compost, plant, and water from the top the first time to settle
    it. Thereafter fill through the tube until it runs out of the overflow.

Best for: tomatoes, chillies, cucumbers, beans, leafy crops — anything that
hates drying out. Not for herbs like rosemary, thyme and sage, which want it
dry.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. Potato sack
==============

15 min · €5 · Skill #.. · The only real calorie crop a balcony can do

Build: a 40 L woven sack, a heavy-duty bag-for-life, or a 40 L bucket with
drainage holes drilled in the base.

Grow:

1. Roll the sack down so it is only 200 mm tall. Put 100 mm of compost in.
2. Plant 2–3 chitted seed potatoes, cover with 100 mm more compost.
3. As the shoots reach 200 mm, unroll the sack and add more compost, burying
    two-thirds of the stem. Repeat 2–3 times until the sack is full.
4. Water consistently — irregular watering is what causes hollow and cracked
    tubers. Feed with diluted worm tea or comfrey feed once the plants flower.
5. Harvest 12–16 weeks after planting, when the foliage yellows. Tip the whole
    sack onto a sheet.

Yield: 1–2 kg per sack. Five sacks on a sunny balcony is 5–10 kg — a week of a
family's carbohydrate. Not self-sufficiency, but real food, and children love
tipping the sack out.

Use blight-resistant varieties (Sarpo Mira/Axona) and fresh compost each year.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


7. Balcony vertical growing frame
=================================

3 h · €60 · Skill ##.

Vertical space is the one dimension a flat has plenty of.

          ▓▓▓ wall ▓▓▓
          ▓  ┌──────────┐  ← shelf 3: herbs, salad (lightest, highest)
          ▓  ├──────────┤
          ▓  ├──────────┤  ← shelf 2: medium pots
          ▓  ├──────────┤
          ▓  └──────────┘  ← shelf 1: heavy pots, tomatoes, potato sacks
          ▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓     HEAVIEST AT THE BOTTOM, AGAINST THE WALL
          ═════════════════   over the edge beam, not the middle of the slab

Build: a ladder-style or A-frame shelf from 45 × 45 and 22 × 150 boards,
leaning against or fixed to the wall, with shelves stepped so each one gets
light. Or railing planters plus wall pockets plus a trellis of strings from
the railing to the ceiling for climbing beans and cucumbers.

The rules:

- [!] Weight at the bottom, against the building wall. Never load the outer
   edge of a cantilevered balcony.
- [!] Secure everything against wind. A pot falling from the fifth floor is
   lethal. Tie railing planters on with wire, not hooks. Check after every
   storm.
- Wind dries pots brutally at height — use bigger pots, mulch the surface,
   screen the windward side with mesh, and use self-watering containers (§5).
- Follow the sun: tallest at the north/back, shortest at the south/front.
- Check your lease before fixing anything to the wall or railing.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. Balcony rain catch
=====================

1 h · €40 · Skill #..

Even a small balcony collects usefully: a 2 m² awning or tarp gives 1.6 litres
per millimetre of rain — 30–80 L in a decent month. Not drinking water, but it
covers flushing, washing and watering, which is where most of your litres go.

          ╲╲╲╲╲ tarp or awning, sloped to one corner ╱╱╱╱╱
                ╲                                 ╱
                  ╲___________________________  ╱
                            ┃  funnel/hose
                            ┃
                      ┌─────┸─────┐
                      │  water    │ ← 100–200 L butt or stacked jerry cans
                      │   butt    │   with a mesh-screened inlet
                      └────[TAP]──┘

Build: a tarp or awning tensioned with a low corner, draining through a
grommet into a hose, into a butt or jerry cans. Mesh over every opening —
against mosquitoes above all, which will breed in open standing water within
days and are a disease issue, not a nuisance.

Rules: [!] 200 L = 200 kg. Site it against the wall over the edge beam, never
mid-slab, and check the balcony's load limit. Keep it covered and dark
(algae). Treat it before any drinking use (Water builds §1). Drain it before a
hard frost.

The much bigger prize is the building's roof. One downpipe from a flat roof
feeds a shared 1,000 L IBC in a bin store or utility room. That is a building-
committee conversation to have in GREEN, and it is transformative for a whole
block — see Chapter 09 §10.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. Corsi–Rosenthal air cleaner box
==================================

30 min · €60 · Skill #.. · Genuinely effective, and cheap

A box fan and four filters taped into a cube. It moves far more clean air per
euro than most commercial purifiers, runs on ~50–80 W, and meaningfully
reduces airborne virus, smoke and dust indoors. For a household in isolation,
or with someone ill in a sick room, this is one of the best €60 you can spend.

                ┌─────────────────┐
                │   ▣▣▣ FAN ▣▣▣   │ ← box fan on top, blowing UP/OUT
                │                 │   + a cardboard shroud on the fan face
          ┌─────┴─────────────────┴─────┐   (the shroud adds 20–50% airflow —
          │ ▦▦▦ │               │ ▦▦▦   │    do not skip it)
          │ ▦F▦ │               │ ▦F▦   │
          │ ▦I▦ │   air drawn   │ ▦I▦   │  4 filters forming the sides,
          │ ▦L▦ │   IN through  │ ▦L▦   │  arrows pointing INWARD
          │ ▦T▦ │   all 4 sides │ ▦T▦   │
          │ ▦E▦ │               │ ▦E▦   │
          │ ▦R▦ │               │ ▦R▦   │
          └─────┴───────────────┴───────┘
                  cardboard base, fully taped — no air leaks

Materials: 4 filters, MERV-13 or European F7 / ePM1 50–65%, 500 × 500 mm (or
20" × 20"), 50 mm deep · a box fan of the same face size · stiff cardboard for
the base and the shroud · a lot of duct tape.

Build

1. Tape the four filters into a cube, arrows on the filter frames all pointing
    INWARD (air flows in through the filters, out through the fan).
2. Tape a cardboard base on the bottom. Seal every seam — any leak is
    unfiltered air bypassing the whole thing.
3. Sit the fan on top, blowing outward/upward, and tape it down all round.
4. Cut a cardboard shroud — a square of card covering the fan's face with a
    circular hole cut to match the fan blade circle — and tape it over the fan
    outlet. This one piece of cardboard adds 20–50% to the airflow by stopping
    recirculation at the blade tips.

Use it: run it on low or medium — it is quieter and barely less effective per
watt · site it in the middle of the room, not against a wall · one in the
shared living space and one in the sick room · filters last 6–12 months in
normal use, less in smoke; buy spares.

In Europe, 20" box fans can be hard to find. Alternatives: a smaller 4-filter
cube using 400 × 400 filters with a suitable fan · a single-filter "bucket"
design with a PC fan array · or simply a commercial HEPA unit if you can get
one. The principle — a lot of filter area and a modest fan — is what matters,
not the exact format.

Pair it with a CO2 meter (Chapter 06): a filter cleans the air, but only
ventilation removes CO2. Below 800 ppm means you are actually exchanging air.
Use both together.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


10. Under-bed storage system
============================

2 h · €60 · Skill #.. · Where a year of food actually goes

A year of staples for four is roughly 965 kg and 1.5–2 m³. A flat has that
space; it is just distributed in awkward places.

     Standard double bed: 2000 × 1400 mm of floor, 200–300 mm of clearance
     = 0.6–0.8 m³ of storage = 250–350 kg of dry staples UNDER ONE BED

Build: low flat boxes or drawers on castors (so they roll out — anything you
have to crawl for, you will not rotate), 200 mm high, sized to your clearance.
Ply, or plastic storage boxes with lids. Bed risers (€20) add another 100–150
mm of clearance and 50% more capacity.

The rules:

- Raise everything off the floor on battens or a ply base — floors are cold,
   they sweat, and a burst pipe or a spilled drink ruins a season's food.
- Keep it cool and dark. Under a bed on an internal wall is good; next to a
   radiator is the worst place in the flat. Shelf life roughly halves for
   every +10 °C.
- Mylar and buckets, or glass jars with O2 absorbers. Jars are rodent-proof
   and look like a pantry rather than a stockpile (OPSEC).
- Label the top of every box and keep the written inventory current (inventory
   template). Storage you cannot see is storage you will not rotate.
- Heaviest at the bottom, densest calories first: oil, nuts, pasta, rice,
   sugar, honey, powdered milk, tinned fish, peanut butter.

The other spaces: wardrobe bases · above kitchen units (to the ceiling) ·
behind and under the sofa · hallway cupboard · the space above internal doors
· a shelf around the top of a room · a cellar locker if the building has one ·
a rented storage unit for the L3 bulk layer.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Related: Chapter 09 — Apartment playbook · Warm-room tent · Bucket toilet ·
Build index


==============================================================================
25. QUICK-REFERENCE CARDS
==============================================================================


Quick-Reference Cards
=====================

The numbers you need at 3 a.m., by torchlight, without reading a chapter.

PRINT THESE FIRST. Print two sets, laminate them or use clear plastic wallets,
and put them on the wall — not in the binder. One set in the kitchen, one in
the emergency kit. Cards 9 and 10 have blanks: fill them in by hand, in pen,
today.

Suggested posting: cards 1–3 by the water store · card 4 by every stove and
heater · card 5 in the medical kit · cards 6–8 in the kitchen · cards 9–10 by
the front door.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


CARD 1 — MAKING WATER SAFE
--------------------------

  ALWAYS DO BOTH:   FILTER (removes dirt, bacteria, protozoa)
                    THEN DISINFECT (kills viruses)
  
  CLOUDY WATER FIRST: settle 2–12 h · pour through 4–8 layers of cotton
                      · a pinch of alum makes it clear in 30 min
  
  BOILING            Rolling boil 1 MINUTE     (3 minutes above 2,000 m)
                     Kills everything. Costs ~1 kg of wood per 10–15 L.
  
  BLEACH             Unscented 5–6% sodium hypochlorite, no additives
    clear water      2 DROPS per LITRE        wait 30 min
    cloudy or cold   4 DROPS per LITRE        wait 60 min
    40 L container   10 mL  (2 teaspoons)
    200 L drum       50 mL
    >> You should smell faint chlorine afterwards. If not, repeat and wait again.
    >> Bleach loses ~20% strength a year. Date the bottle. Replace annually.
  
  NaDCC TABLETS      One tablet per the packet dose. Wait 30 min.
                     5–10 year shelf life. The best thing to store.
  
  POOL SHOCK         Calcium hypochlorite 65–70%, PLAIN (no algaecide/stabiliser)
    make stock       7 g (1 heaped tsp) into 7.5 L water
    then dose        1 part stock to 100 parts water · wait 30 min
    >> Store OUTSIDE the house, dry, sealed, away from fuel and acids.
  
  NEVER DRINK        heating-system water · pool water · anything with
                     antifreeze, inhibitor or algaecide
  KEEP SEPARATE      Mark containers RAW and DRINK. Never swap them.

HOW MUCH: 15–25 L per person per day sustainable · 5–7 L survival minimum ·
chicken 0.5 L · goat 8 L · dog 2 L


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


CARD 2 — DEHYDRATION & ORAL REHYDRATION
---------------------------------------

  * DIARRHOEA AND VOMITING KILL BY DEHYDRATION — FAST.
  * Children and the elderly go downhill in hours, not days.
  
  ORAL REHYDRATION SOLUTION (ORS) — make it exactly:
  
       1 LITRE clean water
     + 6 LEVEL teaspoons SUGAR
     + 1/2 LEVEL teaspoon SALT
       stir until dissolved
  
    >> Too much salt is dangerous. Level spoons. It should taste
       no saltier than tears.
    >> Sip continuously — small amounts, often. Keep sipping even if vomiting.
    >> Adult: 200–400 mL after every loose stool.
       Child: 50–100 mL after every loose stool.
    >> Keep breastfeeding. Keep eating if they can.
  
  SHOP SACHETS ARE BETTER. Keep 40+. They weigh nothing and cost almost nothing.
  
  SIGNS OF SERIOUS DEHYDRATION — SEEK HELP (see CARD 5)
    no urine for 8–12 hours · very dark urine · sunken eyes · no tears in a child
    dry mouth · dizzy on standing · skin stays tented when pinched
    drowsy, floppy, confused · fast breathing or fast weak pulse
  
  DO NOT give anti-diarrhoeal drugs if there is BLOOD or a HIGH FEVER.
  Rehydration is the treatment. Fluids, fluids, fluids.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


CARD 3 — BLEACH & DISINFECTION
------------------------------

  Household bleach = 5–6% sodium hypochlorite. Mix FRESH — it loses
  strength within about 24 hours once diluted.
  
  USE                                 DILUTION            CONTACT TIME
  ------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Drinking water                      2 drops per litre   30 min
  Surfaces, general cleaning          1:100  (10 mL/L)     1 min
  Body fluids, vomit, faeces          1:10   (100 mL/L)   10 min
  Sick-room surfaces                  1:10                10 min
  Dishes/cloths from a sick person    1:100               10 min
  Boot dip / threshold footbath       1:50                 -
  
  * CLEAN FIRST, THEN DISINFECT. Disinfectant on a dirty surface does little.
  * NEVER MIX BLEACH WITH: ammonia · acids · toilet cleaner · vinegar
    It releases chlorine gas. In a small room this will seriously hurt you.
  * Ventilate while using it. Gloves on.
  
  ALTERNATIVES   70% alcohol (fast, flammable) · hydrogen peroxide 3%
                 boiling water (dishes, cloths) · soap + scrubbing (always first)
  NO SUPPLY      wood ash (alkaline, for latrines) · garden lime (pits, drums)
  
  HANDWASHING BEATS ALL OF IT.
  Soap + 20 seconds + rinse. After the toilet, after waste, after animals,
  after outside, before food, before eating, before and after caring for the ill.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


CARD 4 — CARBON MONOXIDE — THIS IS THE ONE THAT KILLS
-----------------------------------------------------

    ██  CO IS COLOURLESS, ODOURLESS AND TASTELESS.  ██
    ██  IT KILLS IN MINUTES. THE EARLY SYMPTOMS     ██
    ██  STOP YOU REALISING YOU NEED TO ACT.         ██
  
  NEVER, EVER, INDOORS — not in a garage, cellar, porch, tent or caravan,
  not "with a window open", not "just for a bit":
  
      ✗ CHARCOAL or a BBQ          ✗ GENERATOR
      ✗ ANY PETROL ENGINE          ✗ PATIO / FLUELESS HEATER
      ✗ A GAS COOKER USED AS A ROOM HEATER
      ✗ ANY FLAME OR STOVE INSIDE A TENT OR BED CANOPY
  
  GENERATOR: outdoors only · at least 5 m from any door, window or vent ·
             exhaust pointing AWAY · never refuel it hot
  
  ANY FLAME uses oxygen and makes CO2, water vapour and some CO.
  VENTILATE — a 2–5 cm window gap costs a little heat and buys a lot of safety.
  NEVER sleep with a flueless combustion appliance running.
  
  SYMPTOMS   headache · nausea · dizziness · sleepiness · confusion ·
             weakness · everyone in the room feels ill at once ·
             BETTER WHEN OUTSIDE, WORSE WHEN INSIDE
  
  IF SUSPECTED:  1. GET EVERYONE OUT, NOW.
                 2. Open everything. Turn the appliance off if you safely can.
                 3. DO NOT GO BACK IN.
                 4. Seek medical help — CO poisoning needs treatment.
  
  FIT CO ALARMS (EN 50291): every sleeping room, every room with a
  combustion appliance, the wall nearest the generator. €20 each.
  TEST THEM MONTHLY. REPLACE EVERY 5–7 YEARS.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


CARD 5 — SEEK MEDICAL HELP NOW
------------------------------

  An overwhelmed hospital is still vastly better than a kitchen table.
  Isolation is not worth a life. GO, or call, for any of these:
  
  BREATHING   SpO2 below 92% (or a clear fall from their normal)
              severe breathlessness · too breathless to finish a sentence
              blue or grey lips or face · coughing blood
              chest pain or pressure
  
  BRAIN       confusion · cannot be roused · unresponsive · seizure
              severe headache with a stiff neck or light hurting the eyes
  
  RASH        a fever with a rash that does NOT fade when you press a
              glass against it  >> possible meningitis/sepsis — EMERGENCY
  
  FLUIDS      no urine for 8–12 hours · sunken eyes · no tears in a child
              skin stays tented when pinched · dizzy or fainting on standing
  
  OTHER       any fever in a baby under 3 months
              severe abdominal pain, especially lower right (appendicitis)
              bleeding you cannot stop · a suspected fracture or head injury
              a burn larger than the person's palm, or on face/hands/genitals
              anything that frightens you AND is getting worse
  
  TWICE A DAY FOR ANYONE ILL, WRITE IT DOWN:
    time · temperature · pulse · breaths/min · SpO2 · fluids in · urine out
    · symptoms · medicine given
  A written trend tells you what a single reading cannot — and it is exactly
  what a professional will ask for.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


CARD 6 — THE TRIGGER LADDER
---------------------------

  GREEN   Normal. Build to your target level. Rotate stock. Train. Grow.
          Check outbreak news monthly. Nothing is urgent.
  
  AMBER   >> Sustained human-to-human spread OUTSIDE the origin country. <<
          * THIS IS THE TRIGGER. NOT "cases in my country" — that is too late.
          ACT WITHIN 72 HOURS — see CARD 7. This is the single most valuable
          action in the whole plan and the window is only a few days wide.
  
  ORANGE  Community transmission confirmed in your country.
          Withdraw from optional contact · work from home · quarantine
          incoming goods and people · final purchases BY DELIVERY ·
          start sprouts and microgreens · set up the isolation room and PPE
          BEFORE anyone is ill · switch to the crisis daily routine now.
  
  RED     Hospitals saturated, OR utilities visibly failing, OR spread
          in your street.
          Full isolation · stored/produced resources only · war-footing
          hygiene · daily health check for everyone · light, noise and
          smell discipline · daily radio check-in with the group ·
          * START RATIONING ON DAY ONE, not when stocks look low.
            Set the daily allowance for a 12-MONTH horizon. It is far
            easier to relax a ration than to impose one late.
  
  BLACK   Utilities down indefinitely, no authority response.
          Full off-grid. Treated water only. Humanure/latrine. Warm room
          only. Cooking on wood. Watch by rotation with the group.
          Preserve everything perishable immediately.
  
  COMING BACK DOWN: step down ONE level after TWO WEEKS of improvement.
  Restock to target BEFORE relaxing the ration. Do not live in RED for years.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


CARD 7 — AMBER: THE 72-HOUR LIST
--------------------------------

  You have about 72 hours before this becomes impossible and expensive.
  One trip, one delivery round, one list.
  
  [ ] FILL every water container — bath, bladder, cans, pots, bottles,
      the washing machine drum
  [ ] FILL the car's fuel tank and every fuel can
  [ ] REFILL every prescription; ask for an early refill and a written repeat
  [ ] DRAW CASH, in small notes
  [ ] TOP UP: flour · oil · rice · pasta · beans · sugar · salt · coffee ·
      tinned fish · UHT milk · BABY FORMULA · PET FOOD
  [ ] TOP UP: soap · bleach · toilet paper · menstrual supplies · nappies ·
      batteries · matches
  [ ] TOP UP: paracetamol · ibuprofen · ORS · antihistamine · vitamins
  [ ] ORDER the long-lead items you postponed — filters, seed, spares, a
      battery, gas cylinders, animal feed
      >> Delivery times collapse immediately after this point.
  [ ] EXTRA GAS CYLINDERS / PROPANE
  [ ] 6 MONTHS of animal feed
  [ ] TELL the mutual-aid group. Agree a check-in schedule.
  [ ] START masking in crowded indoor places; cut optional exposure.
      The goal is to be UNINFECTED AND STOCKED when the wave arrives.
  [ ] SWITCH to the crisis daily routine so it is habit before it matters.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


CARD 8 — THE DAILY ROUTINE
--------------------------

  07:00  Wake — everyone, same time. Drifting sleep is the first crack.
         Health check: anyone symptomatic? Temp + SpO2 if so. Log it.
         Light the stove. Kettle and the big pot on.
         WATER: check levels, run the filter, fill the handwash station.
         POWER: check battery %, clear the panels, plan the day's loads.
         ANIMALS: feed, water (break ice), collect eggs, check security.
         Rinse the sprout jars.
  08:00  BREAKFAST TOGETHER.
  09:00  Radio check-in with the group.
         Main work block — assigned the night before. Everyone has a job.
  12:30  Lunch.
  13:30  Second work block. Children: school work.
  15:00  START THE EVENING MEAL — get it boiling, then into the thermal
         cooker so it finishes itself with no more fuel.
         Finish every daylight task before dusk. Always.
  17:00  SECURE FOR NIGHT: animals in, doors locked, tools in, blackout
         curtains closed, water containers refilled.
         Rinse the sprout jars.
  18:00  DINNER TOGETHER. No radio. No crisis talk.
  19:00  Radio check-in. NEWS: 20 minutes, ONE source, then off.
         Household meeting, 10 min: today, tomorrow, who does what,
         is anyone struggling.
  20:00  NON-PRODUCTIVE TIME, DELIBERATELY. Cards, music, reading aloud.
         This is the maintenance schedule for the people. It is not optional.
  22:00  Lights out. Log the day: weather, stores used, work done, changes.
  
  * ROTATE THE WORST JOBS ON A WRITTEN ROTA. Nothing corrodes a household
    faster than one person silently emptying the toilet bucket every day.
  * ONE FULL REST DAY A WEEK. Defend it. Burnout is a survival risk.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------

CARD 11 — RATION & CALORIE MATHS
--------------------------------

  DAILY NEED       Working adult      2,800 – 3,000 kcal
                   Sedentary adult    1,900 – 2,400 kcal
                   Child 4–10         1,400 – 1,900 kcal
                   Teenager           2,200 – 3,600 kcal
                   Pregnant +300 · Breastfeeding +500
  
  * Crisis life IS manual work, and a cold house burns hundreds more.
    Undereating destroys the physical work you must do AND your immune
    system, exactly when infection is the threat.
  
  ROUGH kcal PER KG (dry staples average ~3,400 kcal/kg)
    Oil 8,800 · Sugar 4,000 · Milk powder 4,900 · Pasta 3,600 · Rice 3,600
    Oats 3,800 · Wheat 3,400 · Beans 3,400 · Honey 3,040 · Potato 770
  
  ENDURANCE CHECK — do this MONTHLY and write the number down:
       total kcal in store  ÷  household kcal per day  =  DAYS REMAINING
  
  ONE YEAR, ONE ADULT:  grain 180 kg · legumes 30 kg · fat 12 kg (13 L)
                        sugar 20 kg · dairy powder 8 kg · SALT 4 kg
  A FAMILY OF FOUR, ONE YEAR: about 965 kg ≈ 1.5–2 m3 ≈ €900–1,600
  
  * VITAMIN C: scurvy in 8–12 weeks on stored food alone.
    SPROUTS (3–5 days, no light, no power) · sauerkraut · vitamin C tablets.
    5 kg of sprouting seed per person costs €25 and keeps 4 years.
    It is the best-value item in the entire plan.

C. The building
---------------

buy.

| The rule that overrides everything on this page: buy for whichever category
| has the lowest endurance in days on your audit. A cheap item in your weakest
| category beats an expensive item in your strongest one, always.

What to buy the moment you go to [AMBER] AMBER
----------------------------------------------

You have 72 hours before this becomes impossible. One trip, one delivery
round, one list. Everything here is a top-up of things you should already
have.

  [ ] Fill EVERY water container, the bath, the bladder, pots, the washing machine
  [ ] Fill the car's fuel tank and every fuel can
  [ ] Refill/request early every prescription; get a written repeat
  [ ] Draw out cash, in small notes
  [ ] Top up: flour, oil, rice, pasta, beans, sugar, salt, coffee, tinned fish,
      UHT milk, formula/baby food, pet food
  [ ] Top up: soap, bleach, toilet paper, menstrual supplies, nappies, batteries
  [ ] Top up: paracetamol, ibuprofen, ORS, antihistamine, vitamins
  [ ] Order the long-lead items you postponed: filters, seed, spares, a battery,
      gas cylinders, feed  ← delivery times collapse after this point
  [ ] Buy the extra gas cylinders / propane now
  [ ] Order animal feed for 6 months
  [ ] Tell the mutual-aid group; agree a check-in schedule
  [ ] Start masking in crowded indoor spaces and cut optional exposure
  [ ] Move to the crisis daily routine so it is habit before it matters

==============================================================================
28. WORKSHEET 3 — DRILL LOG
==============================================================================


Worksheet 3 — Drill Log
=======================

Untested preparation is a guess. Every drill in this file has, for every
household that has ever run one, produced a list of things that did not work.
That is the point. Finding them now costs an uncomfortable weekend. Finding
them later costs much more.

Rule: every drill produces a fix list and a shopping list. Nothing is complete
until those are closed.


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


The schedule
------------

Drill                                         Frequency               Next due
--------------------------------------------  ----------------------  --------
Blackout weekend (main breaker off, Fri       2×/year, one in winter
evening → Sun evening)

Water-off day (stopcock closed, 24 h)         2×/year, one in summer
Eat-the-store week (no shopping, storage      Quarterly
only)

Cold night (heating off, warm-room only)      1×/winter
Isolation weekend (nobody leaves, decon       1×/year
protocol active)

Full inventory count                          2×/year
Comms check with the group                    Monthly
Key-person-silent drill (the person who       1×/year
knows everything says nothing)



------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Drill 1 — Blackout weekend
--------------------------

Setup: Friday 18:00, turn off the main breaker. Turn it back on Sunday 18:00.
No cheating with the car, no takeaways, no charging phones at work.

What it tests

  [ ] Can we light the house comfortably, all evening, for two nights?
  [ ] Did the fridge and freezer contents survive? (thermometer inside — record temps)
  [ ] Can we cook three meals a day on the backup stove? How much fuel did it use?
  [ ] Can we heat the room we're in? (winter run only)
  [ ] Did the battery last? What was the state of charge at each morning?
  [ ] Did the solar produce what we expected? (record the actual figures)
  [ ] Could we charge phones, run the radio, run any medical device?
  [ ] Did the inverter idle-drain surprise us?
  [ ] Did we have to run the generator? For how long? On how much fuel?
  [ ] Water: did anything depend on an electric pump we forgot about?
  [ ] Did everyone know where the torches were, in the dark, without being told?
  [ ] Were we bored? What did we do about it? Was it enough?
  [ ] What did we cheat on, and why?

Record:

  Date: __________ Season: __________ Outside temp: ______ °C
  Battery SoC  Fri 18:00 ____%  Sat 08:00 ____%  Sat 18:00 ____%  Sun 08:00 ____%
  Solar produced: Sat ______ Wh · Sun ______ Wh
  Generator run: ______ h on ______ L
  Cooking fuel used: __________
  Freezer temp after 48 h: ______ °C
  Indoor temp (winter run): morning ______ °C  evening ______ °C

What failed:
_____________________________________________________________________
_____________________________________________________________________ Fix list
/ shopping list:
_____________________________________________________________________
_____________________________________________________________________


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Drill 2 — Water-off day
-----------------------

Setup: close the mains stopcock at 07:00. Reopen after 24 hours. Use only
stored and treated water.

What it tests

  [ ] How much water did we ACTUALLY use in 24 h? (measure it — this is the point)
  [ ] Did the toilet plan work? Did anyone refuse to use it?
  [ ] Did the filter flow fast enough to keep up with demand?
  [ ] Could we wash dishes, hands and bodies to a standard we could sustain?
  [ ] Did the stored water taste acceptable? Did the children drink it?
  [ ] Could we find and operate the stopcock, and drain the hot water cylinder?
  [ ] Did we contaminate anything by mixing clean and dirty containers?
  [ ] How long did hauling/filtering/pouring actually take each day?

Record:

  Date: __________ People: ______ Season: __________
  Water used in 24 h: ______ L total = ______ L/person
    Drinking ____ · Cooking ____ · Washing ____ · Dishes ____ · Toilet ____
  Filter throughput measured: ______ L/hour
  Time spent on water tasks: ______ minutes
  Days of stored water at this rate: ______

What failed / fix list:
_____________________________________________________________________
_____________________________________________________________________


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Drill 3 — Eat-the-store week
----------------------------

Setup: seven days, no shopping, nothing fresh bought. Cook only from your
stored food. Keep cooking on your normal appliances — this drill is about the
food, not the fuel.

What it tests

  [ ] Does the family eat it? Actually eat it, without complaint turning into revolt?
  [ ] What ran out that we didn't expect? (usually: oil, yeast, spices, onions)
  [ ] What did nobody touch? (stop buying it)
  [ ] Do we know how to cook from raw staples — beans, wheat berries, milk powder?
  [ ] How long does it take to cook this way? Is it compatible with a work day?
  [ ] What did we miss most? (that's your comfort-layer shopping list)
  [ ] Did anyone get constipated, tired, or unwell? (nutrition gap signal)
  [ ] How much did we consume vs. what the plan assumed? Recalculate endurance.

Record:

  Date: __________
  Consumed this week: grain ____ kg · legumes ____ kg · oil ____ L · other __________
  Projected 3-month need based on ACTUAL use: ______________________
  Meals that worked: _______________________________________________
  Meals that failed: _______________________________________________
  Ran out of: ______________________________________________________

Fix list:
_____________________________________________________________________


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Drill 4 — Cold night
--------------------

Setup: one winter night, heating off from 16:00 to 08:00. Warm room only.

  [ ] How cold did the warm room get? The rest of the house?
  [ ] Was the bedding enough? Did anyone not sleep?
  [ ] How much fuel did one evening of the wood stove / backup heater use?
  [ ] Did the draught-proofing and curtains make a measurable difference?
  [ ] Did the CO alarm stay quiet? (if not — STOP, and fix it before repeating)
  [ ] Could we dry laundry without condensation problems?
  [ ] Did anyone complain of headache or nausea? (investigate immediately)

Record:

  Date: __________ Outside min: ______ °C
  Warm room at 22:00 ______ °C · at 06:00 ______ °C
  Rest of house at 06:00 ______ °C
  Fuel used: ______________

Fix list:
_____________________________________________________________________


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Drill 5 — Isolation weekend
---------------------------

Setup: Friday evening to Sunday evening. Nobody leaves the property, nobody
comes in. Run the full decon protocol on anything delivered. Utilities stay on
— this drill is about the protocol and the psychology.

  [ ] Did the entrance airlock work? Was it in the way? Did everyone use it?
  [ ] Did we have the PPE we thought we had, and does it fit each adult's face?
  [ ] Did we follow the protocol when it was inconvenient, or quietly skip it?
  [ ] Sick room: could we set it up in 15 minutes? Is everything in the box?
  [ ] Did we get on each other's nerves? What triggered it?
  [ ] Did children have enough to do? Did the schooling plan hold?
  [ ] Did everyone get privacy? Did anyone get none?
  [ ] Did the daily routine hold, or did it dissolve by Saturday afternoon?
  [ ] Did we ration the news, or did it take over?

Record and fix list:
_____________________________________________________________________
_____________________________________________________________________


------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Drill 6 — Key-person-silent
---------------------------

Setup: the person who knows most about the systems (power, water, stove, where
everything is) says nothing and does nothing for a whole weekend. Someone else
runs the house.

This is the drill everyone skips and the one that most often reveals a fatal
gap. A pandemic is precisely the event that takes an adult out of action for
two weeks.

  [ ] Could someone else start, run and monitor the power system?
  [ ] Could someone else filter and treat water?
  [ ] Could someone else light and manage the stove safely?
  [ ] Does everyone know where the stopcock, breaker, gas valve, spares and
      documents are?
  [ ] Is the written system manual good enough to follow without asking?
  [ ] Could someone else feed and secure the animals?

Gaps found:

updated? [ ] Yes — because the answer to almost every gap here is "write it
down and teach it."

29. INVENTORY TEMPLATE

column, which tells you what expires next. The machine-readable copy is
worksheets/inventory-template.csv.
